# Design Decisions

*Each decision records a non-obvious choice and its rationale. Decisions are referenced from [tuglaws.md](tuglaws.md) as `[D##]` and laws are referenced here as `[L##]`.*

**A decision names no test.** A test name is the most perishable thing a decision can cite: the corpus is a set of aids rather than a set of promises, so any one of them may be deleted the day it fails without producing a symptom anyone noticed — and a decision that cited it is left holding a name that resolves to nothing, with nothing to detect that it has. So a decision states what it decided, and where a test's parenthetical carried a fact the decision was relying on, that fact is written into the decision's own prose instead. What covers a decision is found through the corpus's `@covers` declarations, which are checked, rather than through prose here, which is not. This is deliberately not linted: a lint would be machinery guarding a rule short enough to live in this paragraph.

---

## Theme & Token Architecture

**D01.** Theme definitions (color choices + recipe reference) are stored as `.json` files in `tugdeck/themes/` (shipped) and `~/.tugtool/themes/` (authored), not as TypeScript constants. Enables runtime loading without rebuilding and the Prototype pattern for new theme authoring. [L06]

**D02.** Two-directory storage with unique names. Shipped themes in `tugdeck/themes/` (version-controlled, read-only). Authored themes in `~/.tugtool/themes/` (user data, not in repo). Names are unique across both directories — middleware checks authored first, then shipped, but uniqueness means no shadowing.

**D03.** Every theme is a peer — no base layer, no override cascade. Switching themes copies the selected theme's complete CSS file into `styles/tug-active-theme.css`. For brio, the source is `styles/themes/brio.css`. For harmony, the source is `styles/themes/harmony.css`. The active theme file is always complete; it is never empty. [L06]

**D04.** `contrastSearch()`, `darkRecipe()`, `lightRecipe()`, and `RECIPE_REGISTRY` are defined in `theme-engine.ts`. `RECIPE_REGISTRY` is the only derivation dispatch path — all `deriveTheme()` calls route through it.

**D86.** The `formulas?: DerivationFormulas` escape hatch is removed from `ThemeRecipe`. The recipe function is the only derivation path. `RECIPE_REGISTRY` is the extension point for new recipe variants.

**D87.** The theme generator card follows Mac document conventions: New (Prototype pattern, copy existing theme), Open (load from available themes), auto-save (500ms debounce to disk), Apply (inject CSS app-wide). Shipped themes open read-only. No explicit Save button.

**D88.** All theme loading goes through the Vite dev middleware. `ThemeName` is a plain `string` (not a hardcoded union). `themeCSSMap` is populated dynamically at startup via `GET /__themes/list`. Supports arbitrary authored themes without code changes.

**D89.** `canvasColorHex()` accepts derived canvas surface params (`hue`, `tone`, `intensity`) extracted from `ThemeOutput.formulas` after running `deriveTheme()`. The raw JSON `surface.canvas.intensity` differs from the derived `surfaceCanvasIntensity`; callers must use the derived value. The `CANVAS_COLORS` lookup table is removed.

**D90.** A theme's `recipe` field (`"dark"` or `"light"`) is set once at theme creation (copied from the prototype) and is immutable. The generator card displays recipe as a read-only label, not a toggle.

**D91.** The Swift Theme submenu uses `NSMenuDelegate.menuNeedsUpdate(_:)` to populate items dynamically from a cached theme list. The web view pushes updated theme lists to Swift via the `themeListUpdated` bridge message. Eliminates hardcoded menu items and per-theme `@objc` handlers.

**D92.** Bluenote is removed from the entire codebase — Swift menu, action dispatch, theme provider, and any CSS files.

**D70.** Color palette is OKLCH-based. 24 hue families with intensity/tone axes, 5 convenience presets per hue, neutral ramp, P3 gamut support, pure CSS formulas. [L15]

**D71.** Four-prefix token naming: `--tugc-{hue}[-preset]` (palette), `--tug7-*` (seven-slot semantic surface and element tokens), `--tugx-<component>-*` (component alias), `--tug-*` (scale/dimension). [L17, L18]

**D72.** Global dimension scale via `--tug-zoom` multiplier on `:root`.

**D73.** Global timing via `--tug-timing` (duration multiplier) and `--tug-motion` (binary on/off toggle) on `:root`.

**D75.** Achromatic neutral ramp (`--tug-neutral-*`). Alpha via CSS relative color syntax, not separate opacity tokens.

**D80.** `--tug-color()` notation expands to `oklch()` at build time via PostCSS plugin (`postcss-tug-color.ts`). Theme files use the notation; browsers never see it.

**D81.** Token pairings are machine-auditable. Every foreground-on-background relationship is extractable from CSS — either via same-rule `background-color` or via `@tug-renders-on` annotation. `audit-tokens lint` enforces zero violations. [L16]

**D82.** Four semantic contrast roles govern text legibility: `content` (75), `control` (60), `display` (60), `informational` (60). Each role maps to a hue slot derived from the recipe's text and display specs. The pairing map assigns every foreground token a contrast role for threshold enforcement. [L15, L18]

**D83.** Five contrast roles with minimum thresholds: `content` (75), `control` (60), `display` (60), `informational` (60), `decorative` (15). All readable text >= 60. [L16]

**D84.** Theme application uses stylesheet injection (`<style id="tug-theme-override">`), not body class toggling. [L06]

**D85.** Optional palette entries use `var()` fallbacks so themes can omit slots gracefully. [L15]

## Component Model

**D05.** Three component kinds: *wrappers* (thin Radix adapters), *compositions* (assemble multiple primitives), *originals* (built from scratch).

**D06.** `components/tugways/` is the public component API surface.

**D07.** Components are module-scope functions composed via JSX nesting, not class hierarchies or render props.

**D08.** TugButton has two modes: *direct-action* (`onClick` prop) and *chain-action* (`action` prop dispatches into responder chain). [L11]

**D15.** TugPane is composition, not inheritance. It assembles chrome (title bar, tab strip, accessory, content region) around the active Card's content, rendered via `CardHost`. [L09]

**D16.** Card data access via `useCardData()` hook, not render props.

**D17.** Cards compute dynamic min-size from content and report via `onMinSizeChange`.

**D22.** Component Gallery card serves as living inventory — 21 tabbed demos covering all tugways components. [L10]

**D39.** Default button: responder chain designates one button per scope. Enter key routes to it. [L11]

**D43.** Component Gallery is a proper card with tabs, not a floating panel. [L10]

**D105.** "Collapse long content" splits into two primitives along one axis: *does the hidden content stay mounted?* **Logical fold** (`BlockFoldCue` + `useBlockFoldState`) is for known-list bodies — file lines, terminal output, diff hunks — where collapsing must *unmount* the hidden portion for performance (don't mount a CM6 editor for a folded file) and coordinate with the transcript scroller (don't slam the viewport when a huge body folds mid-scroll). It thresholds by item count and is coupled, deliberately, to the tool-block chrome and scroller contexts. **Visual clamp** (`TugClamp`) is for arbitrary content that is cheap to render in full but visually too tall — a wrapped command, an error message, a markdown blurb — where there is no perf reason to unmount and no scroller to coordinate with. It *measures* the rendered height (the visual line count isn't known ahead of wrapping), caps it behind a `data-expanded` + CSS window with a Show more/less reveal, and keeps everything mounted. The two share only the `ChevronsDown/Up` vocabulary, never the machinery — so neither is forced to straddle both jobs. Reach for `TugClamp` in dialogs and inline surfaces; reach for the fold system inside a tool-block body. Tree-shaped per-node collapses (JSON nodes, diff hunks, search-result files) are a third, separate thing and belong to neither. [L06]

---

## Responder Chain & Actions

**D09.** Responder chain operates entirely outside React state. `ResponderChainManager` is a plain TypeScript class. [L01, L02, L07]

**D10.** Four-stage key processing pipeline: keybinding resolution, responder dispatch, default handling, browser passthrough.

**D11.** Two-level action validation: `canHandle` (fast routing check) + `validateAction` (semantic validation with current state). [L07]

**D61.** `ActionEvent` carries typed payload, sender identity, and phase. [L11]

**D62.** Two dispatch modes: *nil-target* (chain walks until a responder handles it) and *explicit-target* (dispatched to a specific responder). [L11]

**D63.** Controls (buttons, sliders, pickers) dispatch ActionEvents into the chain but never register as responder nodes. [L11]

---

## State & Mutation Zones

**D13.** DOM utility hooks for appearance zone: `useCSSVar`, `useDOMClass`, `useDOMStyle`. All bypass React state. [L06]

**D40.** DeckManager is a subscribable store. Implements `subscribe()`/`getSnapshot()` for `useSyncExternalStore`. Exactly one `root.render()` call, at construction. [L01, L02]

**D41.** `useResponder` uses `useLayoutEffect` for registration so responder nodes are wired before any events fire. [L03]

**D42.** No repeated `root.render()` from external code. All state changes flow through `notify()` and `useSyncExternalStore`. [L01]

**D64.** Mutation transactions: `begin` (snapshot CSS inline styles) → `preview` (live CSS mutations) → `commit`/`cancel`. [L08]

**D65.** Transaction previews operate in appearance zone only. Commit handler may cross into store/React state. [L06, L08]

**D66.** Style cascade reader (`StyleCascadeReader`) provides `getDeclared()` with four source layers: `token`, `class`, `inline`, `preview`.

## Cards & Layout

**D27.** *(Retired by [D128].)* Window-shade collapse: double-click title bar toggles card between full and title-bar-only. State stored in `CardState.collapsed`. Removed outright when the width presets landed — it was pointer-only (no command, no chord, no menu item), and the chevron's title-bar slot went to the width popup, which is a control the same cluster had room for and the keyboard can reach. Old blobs carrying `collapsed: true` deserialize with the field dropped and the pane expanded.

**D30.** Tab bar visible only when card has multiple tabs.

**D31.** Tabs are a TugPane chrome feature — a UI affordance that appears when a Pane holds more than one Card. Not a data concept. [L09, L10, L25]

**D44.** Progressive tab overflow: three stages — all visible, inactive tabs collapse to icon-only, overflow into dropdown.

**D45.** Card-as-tab merge: dropping a card onto another card's tab bar merges it as a new tab. `detachTab()` reverses.

**D49.** Per-tab state bag preserves `scroll`, `selection`, and `content` across tab switches and reloads.

**D50.** `useCardStatePreservation` hook: card content registers `onSave`/`onRestore` callbacks. Uses `useLayoutEffect` for registration. [L03]

**D95.** A content-owning + engine card has exactly one text-entry surface — the engine's editor (`tug-prompt-entry` for a session card) — and activation focus has exactly one destination: the engine. A content-owning card (`bag.content !== undefined` — every session card is one) carries no `data-tug-focus-key` / `data-tug-state-key` element of its own, so `captureFocus` only ever classifies its focus as `engine` or `none`; the framework-axis save site leaves `bag.focus` absent in both cases. On restore, the single-channel dispatcher `resolveBagFocus` finds no framework-axis snapshot but an engine-managed `componentId`, and routes to the `engine` resolution — `applyBagFocus` invokes the engine's registered `paintMirrorAsActive` hook (`store.registerEngineHooks` / `store.invokeEnginePaintMirrorAsActive`). The engine is a *callable*, not an autonomous claimant: it no longer claims focus from `onCardActivated`. Focusable-but-not-entry content inside the card (a read-only `TugCodeView` viewport, a block button) carries no focus marker, so `captureFocus` classifies it `none` and activation focus correctly returns to the engine — a viewer is not a text-entry surface, and transient selection-to-copy focus is not preserved across activation. The general framework focus axis (`data-tug-focus-key` / `data-tug-state-key`, the `dom` / `form-control` `FocusSnapshot` kinds) still serves non-engine cards (form-control cards, settings cards); it is simply never populated *inside* a content-owning + engine card. See [state-preservation.md § Focus dispatch model](state-preservation.md#focus-dispatch-model), [component-authoring.md § Focus in content-owning cards](component-authoring.md), `card-host.tsx` (`captureFocus`), and `focus-transfer.ts` (`resolveBagFocus` / `applyBagFocus`). Established by Phase E.11 (single-channel dispatcher) and Phase E.12 (single-text-entry rule; per-block Find removed). Supersedes the pre-E.11 framing of this decision (the engine-vs-framework "two axes" model with `kind: "component-owned"` and `resolveActivationTarget`). Previously documented inline as `[D07]` in `card-host.tsx` comments — that local tag has been normalized to reference this decision (the canonical D07 is the JSX-composition rule above; the focus-boundary rule is D95). [L23]

**D51.** Focused card ID persisted in `DeckState.focusedCardId` for reload restoration.

**D52.** Collapsed state persisted in `CardState.collapsed`.

**D121.** **Layout imposition: a slot is a position anchor, not a rect — the imposer places panes and never sizes them.** The deck's `imposition` is a required `{ kind?, contentWidth?, sidebars, rails? }` record on `DeckState`: `kind` (`"one-up"` through `"six-up"`, absent = off) defines numbered **slots**, `contentWidth` names the width content cards read at ([D128]), `sidebars[componentId] = { side, pinned? }` says which edge each sidebar card holds and whether it is standing there, and `rails[side] = { mode?, order?, shares? }` says how the cards sharing that edge stand against one another ([D134] — absent reads as a stack, which is what every rail was before it could be split). (A blob older than the `sidebars` map names ids nothing registers now — the blob stayed at v4, defensively parsed.) All of them are layout decisions, so all of them are made in one place — the **Layout** card, whose every option is a scale picture of the resulting deck; there is no app-wide sidebar-side preference. Slots are laid across the **span** — the canvas minus each side's rail width and one imposition gap — so an imposed position is never under a rail even though a free pane may still be dragged there. **A sidebar is itself imposed, as the arrangement's fixed end rather than one of its slots** (`imposeSidebarStyle`): it holds its side at a constant pin with the imposition gap on all four sides, keeps its own width, and never overlaps. It has to be the fixed end — a slotted card can end up overlapped when the deck is crowded, and a card sliding under a rail is the one outcome the arrangement forbids. **A rail is draggable, and dragging it by its title bar is what takes it off the pin** (`sidebars[componentId].pinned: false`): it becomes an ordinary free pane and the arrangement spans the whole canvas exactly as it does when the rail is closed. Any choice on the Layout card puts it back — naming the side, or naming the kind, is the gesture that says the rail belongs at the end of the arrangement — as does closing and reopening it. Widening a rail by its deck-facing edge does **not** unpin it: the arrangement keeps laying out against it, live. A sidebar pane carries no marker of its own: it is the pane hosting a card whose registration declares `layoutRole: "sidebar"`, derived by `findSidebarPanes` ([D128]). A slot is an anchor at a fixed fraction of the band: a pane of width *w* in slot *k* of an *N*-slot imposition sits `k / (N − 1) × max(0, band − w)` from the packing edge, and stretches to full canvas height. Slot 0 therefore hugs the far edge and the last slot hugs the rail, by construction rather than by arithmetic that happens to work out. **A pane's position depends on its own width and nothing else's** — closing, opening, widening, or restacking any other card leaves it exactly where it was. A slot is a place in the arrangement, never a place in a queue, and that is worth more than the earlier rule of pooling all the slack in one margin beside the rail (which made the arrangement re-pack whenever its membership changed). Slack now spreads evenly between the cards. That is the whole geometry: **the imposer never touches a pane's width, in any code path.** The width **presets** are no exception and were checked against this rule when they landed: a preset is applied by a *command* through `movePane`, the same door a drag-resize uses, and the imposer goes on passing whatever width it finds through untouched ([D128]). Widths stay the user's, which is what keeps the arrangement from re-creating the tab strip's fight for horizontal space — and it is why overlap needs no handling at all: when the assigned cards are wider than their share, the anchors still hold exactly and the cards simply overlap their neighbours, which is ordinary geometry, not a failure. **Slots are stacks:** any number of panes may hold one, clicking a number always assigns *and* raises (never a toggle, never a no-op), and the filterable, metadata-rich Cards list — not a tab strip — is the switching surface. Assigning a card that shares a pane with others **detaches it first** (`_detachCard`); the imposer slots cards, never tab groups. **The arrangement is also the keyboard's:** ⌘1.⌘N puts the selected card at slot N, where N is whatever the chosen kind has — the Layout card configures the chords by configuring the arrangement, and there is no separate list of shortcuts to keep in step with it. DeckCanvas owns the `move-to-slot` handler because the canvas owns the layout tree, and it converges on the same `assign-slot` the SlotPicker dispatches, so a chord and a click do one thing. The whole digit row is bound even though six-up is the largest arrangement, and every number the current arrangement lacks — along with an empty selection and a selected rail card — is a **silent no-op**: a chord the user has not configured should do nothing, not beep. These chords replaced `jump-to-tab` (⌘1.⌘9 by tab index), which the tab strip's demotion to a switching surface of last resort had already left with little to switch; ⇧⌘[ / ⇧⌘] still walk a pane's tabs. Assignment is **only ever explicit**: nothing is auto-slotted, auto-rebalanced, or auto-evicted. Any manual move **or resize** of an imposed pane evicts it back to free geometry (an explicit option on the commit, never a "position changed" heuristic — a west-edge resize moves `position.x` too). A pinned rail is the exception: its deck-facing edge resizes it in place, re-laying the arrangement live. `arrangeCards` (Window ▸ Cascade / Tile) skips a **pinned** rail for the same reason — tiling writes a stored rect, and a pinned rail paints from its pin; a rail off the pin tiles with everything else. Turning the imposition off **freezes** every imposed pane at its live on-screen rect rather than scattering them back to stale coordinates. A kind change **clamps** out-of-range slots instead of dropping panes out of the arrangement. Geometry is **CSS-derived, not observed**: the span reaches CSS as `--tug-imposer-inset-left` / `--tug-imposer-inset-right` (the rail's render width plus one gap) and an imposed frame's pins are `calc` expressions over them, so a window resize or a rail drag reflows every imposed pane with no JavaScript and no resize observation anywhere on the deck ([L06]). **Every horizontal pin is emitted as `left`**, including the ones measured from the right edge (as `100% - …`) — a frame positioned by one property can transition between two arrangements, while one that switches between `left` and `right` can only cut. That is what the settle is built on: a change to the `imposition` record marks the frames' container with `data-imposer-settling` for `IMPOSITION_SETTLE_MS`, and every derived frame crosses to its new place instead of teleporting. The duration is one number, written onto the container as `--tugx-imposer-settle-duration` and read back when timing the settle, so overriding it there retimes both. The vocabulary is the printing industry's, chosen deliberately: *imposition* is the arrangement of pages at their correct positions on the press sheet. It is also forced — "layout" is the layout tree and the tugbank domain, "position" is the pane rect's origin. `tugdeck/src/lib/layout-imposer.ts` (`resolvePlacement` / `travelFraction` / `imposeStyle` / `imposeSidebarStyle` / `imposeRect` / `resolveSpan` / `isSidebarPinned` / `allocateSidebarWidths`), `layout-tree.ts` (`imposition` / `slot?` / `widthPreset?`, invariant 6: at most one pane per sidebar componentId, and it carries no slot), `deck-store-selectors.ts` (`findSidebarPanes`), `deck-manager.ts` (`setImposition` / `setSidebarSide` / `setContentWidth` / `setPaneWidth` / `pinSidebars` / `_unpinSidebar` / `assignCardToSlot` / `movePane` `evictSlot` / `arrangeCards` / `centerPane`), `components/chrome/tug-pane.tsx` (the pinned and imposed style branches, DOM-seeded drag conversion), `components/chrome/deck-canvas.tsx` (the `move-to-slot` handler), `components/tugways/keybinding-map.ts` (⌘1.⌘9), `components/layout/layout-card.tsx`, `components/layout/layout-miniature.tsx`, `components/cards/slot-picker.tsx`. Full doctrine: [pane-model.md § Three geometry modes](pane-model.md). [L02], [L06], [L09], [L11]

**D128.** **The layout system speaks two card roles — content and sidebar — and content cards have three named widths.** A registration declares `layoutRole: "content" | "sidebar"` (`card-registry.ts`), defaulting to content. **Content cards are the ones the deck arranges**: they take N-Up slots, answer ⌘1.⌘N, and follow the width presets. **Sidebar cards pin to a deck edge and inset the band the content cards live in** — cards, arcs, jots, layout and overview today, and whatever registers next. A sidebar takes no slot, is skipped by `arrangeCards`, is refused by `assignCardToSlot`, and carries a `{ side, pinned? }` entry in the imposition record ([D121]). The taxonomy **constrains the layout system; it does not sort every card into two boxes** — the utility cards (settings, keyboard, devtools, gallery, about) declare nothing and are arranged like anything else. This was a *promotion, not an invention*: every piece of it already existed as a special case shaped around one card (a finder matching one componentId, a single-card entry in the imposition record, a style function of its own, an allocator with one unknown), and lifting Snippets out of that card as its own card is what forced the special case to become a declared trait. **Both edges can carry a rail, and one edge can carry two cards.** Same-side cards **share a rail and stand front-to-back in it**, exactly as two panes sharing a slot do: same pin, same width, same full vertical run, with z-order deciding which one you see and the title bar's stack badge and picker ([D123]) the way to the other. That makes a rail a *place* in the same sense a slot is, which is the whole of why the badge applies to it unchanged. Rails hold a z-band above the free panes (a rail is never occluded by a card) and below the overlay base (or it would bury its own menus); inside the band the members take the deck's array order, so `activateCard` raises one over the other. **Dividing the rail's height between the members was tried first and rejected**: it spends a rail to show two half-cards, which is what a two-section card was already doing *inside* one rail, only less space-efficient — and the point of lifting Jots out was to give it a whole rail when it is the one you are looking at. The design that lost persisted a seam split and a per-card vertical order; the one that shipped persists neither, because front-to-back has nothing to remember but each card's side. This matters out of the box rather than in a corner: the shipped default stands Cards, Arcs and Layout on the right, so the stacked rail is the deck a user meets. **When imposition takes or grants rail space, every visible rail lands on ONE shared width** — never a per-rail solve, so the deck cannot answer with two rails at two widths; sidebars are a uniform class the way content cards are. The shared width may grow to the **slim** content width (675) and no further, whatever Card Width the deck is set to — a rail is a reading surface, and a comfy- or wide-sized sidebar is absurd on its face — and may shrink to a fifth under the width the user chose, floored by the tightest `sizePolicy.min` among each rail's stack members. The chosen width the shrink allowance hangs under is the card's **durable** stored width (`sidebarWidthStore`, per card), never the live pane width: the live width is where the allocator writes its own answers, and an allocator that reads its output back as the user's preference re-anchors on every solve and ratchets one rail wide. The fifth-under shrink is the SOFT allowance and the refusal on a failed fit is no longer absolute — [D136] grades the licence: crowding deepens the shrink to the hard floor, and an untileable solve still moves the rails as far as visibly helps. **Every Layout card click re-solves**, Card Width included — restamping the content panes moves every seam, so `setContentWidth` runs the same retune the kind rows run — and so does a card JOINING the chain ([D136], narrowed by [D140]: a card already in the chain moving between slots does not). **The widths are slim 675 / comfy 800 / wide 1230**, named constants in `layout-imposer.ts`. Slim is the reading width the Session card's chrome was put on a diet to reach and is therefore also its floor — a card that could go narrower than the narrowest width the deck offers would have a floor no gesture can reach. A preset is applied by a command through `movePane` and held between the pane's own `sizePolicy.min` and `max`, because `movePane` writes the rect it is handed without clamping and a card can beat a preset in either direction (Settings' 720 floor, About's 320 lock). The stamp (`TugPaneState.widthPreset`) records what the user chose even where the card's bounds won, and **any manual resize clears it** — a settled control shows what the geometry holds. Two doors: the **title-bar popup** sets one pane, and **Card Width** on the Layout card sets the deck's default and puts every content pane on it at once, overwriting per-pane deviations. That asymmetry is the design — you say how wide content reads here, then dissent on the one card you want narrow — and it is why re-choosing the width the deck is already at is a real gesture rather than a no-op. A card registered with `takesContentWidth` opens at the deck's width instead of a number frozen into its registration. The **window-shade collapse was removed outright** to make room for the popup in the title bar: it was pointer-only — no command, no chord, no menu item — and old blobs carrying `collapsed: true` deserialize with the field dropped and the pane expanded. `card-registry.ts` (`layoutRole` / `takesContentWidth` / `isSidebarCard`), `lib/layout-imposer.ts` (`sidebars` / `contentWidth` / `maxRailWidth` / `CONTENT_WIDTH_*_PX` / `resolveContentWidthPx` / `allocateSidebarWidths` / `imposeSidebarStyle`), `deck-manager.ts` (`setSidebarSide` / `setContentWidth` / `setPaneWidth` / `retuneSidebarAllocation`), `components/layout/layout-card.tsx` (the three axes, sidebar controls built from the registry), `components/chrome/tug-pane.tsx` (the width popup). Full doctrine: [pane-model.md § Three geometry modes](pane-model.md). [D121], [L02], [L06], [L09], [L23]

---

## Snap Sets

**D32.** Snap requires Option/Alt modifier during drag (`altKey`).

**D33.** Set-move is always active once a snap set is formed — dragging one member moves all.

**D53.** Set members get squared corners via CSS `data-in-set` attribute.

**D54.** Set perimeter flash via SVG hull polygon (`computeSetHullPolygon()`).

**D55.** Break-out restores rounded corners (CSS-driven) and flashes individual card perimeter.

**D56.** Border collapse: snap positions offset by border width so adjacent cards share a single visual line.

**D57.** Interior set shadows hidden via `clip-path: inset()` on `.tug-pane-chrome`. Exterior edges extend by `SHADOW_EXTEND_PX`.

**D58.** Active/inactive shadow tokens: `--tug-card-shadow-active` (focused) and `--tug-card-shadow-inactive` (unfocused).

**D59.** Command-key (`metaKey`) suppresses card activation on click, allowing multi-card operations.

**D60.** Resize click activates the card (brings to front).

## Selection

**D34.** Three-layer selection containment: CSS `user-select: none` baseline, `SelectionGuard` runtime clipping, `data-tug-select` developer API. [L12]

**D35.** `SelectionGuard` is a module-level singleton, not React state. [L01, L12]

**D36.** Pointer-clamped selection uses `caretPositionFromPoint` (with `caretRangeFromPoint` fallback). [L12]

**D37.** Four select modes via `data-tug-select` attribute: `default`, `none`, `all`, `custom`. [L12]

**D38.** Cmd+A scoped to focused card via responder chain `selectAll` action. [L12]

**D122.** **The element marks; the container does not — and the element mark rides Accent while the selection fill rides Key.** Two cross-component rules, recorded together because the second is what keeps the first safe. (a) An **item-group container** — `TugListView`, `TugAccordion`, `TugRadioGroup`, `TugChoiceGroup`, `TugOptionGroup`, `TugTabBar`, `TugTabView`'s sidebar — marks keyboard focus with **nothing on the container**. The whole treatment is the mark on the **cursor item** inside it: a **ring** at full accent on an inline chip, a **leading-edge bar** at full accent on a full-width row. The container is `data-key-view-kbd`, so the global leaf-ring rule would stroke it; each component suppresses that with `outline: none` on its own root at one-class-plus-one-attribute specificity, and names `data-key-within` in the same selector. **Three container marks were tried first, and the record is the point.** (1) A full-accent perimeter: a bright rectangle around a bright ring, two questions in identical vocabulary at two scales, and the eye reads the big rectangle before the small one — backwards, since the cursor is what the next keystroke acts on. (2) A background wash, the two-*kinds* answer, so neither question competed; better on paper and it lost to the surfaces, because an area mark is a colour laid over a whole region and at any strength that reads it also tints every pixel of content sitting on it — brown over a mid-tone sheet, grey-tan over a pale one, each correction toward one surface degrading the next. (3) A toned, thicker stroke against a brighter, tighter element one, separating by *degree*; it shipped and still read as two rings, because tone is not enough separation between two closed rectangles a few pixels apart. The fourth answer is to stop drawing it: what all three were buying was "which container holds the keyboard", and an element mark *inside* the container already answers that. Retiring it also retired the machinery — `TugListView`'s perimeter needed a sticky overlay child, a JS-measured scrollport height and scrollbar width, a two-layer band split by the scroller's clip, a z-index lift, an inner-overlap offset the cursor caret had to read, and a carry-suppression rule, none of which has a subject now. The shape of an element mark follows the shape of the item: full-width row containers (list, accordion) mark the cursor with the bar (`--tugx-focus-cursor-bar-*`, a language-level knob precisely because two components draw it — one component reading the other's slot would breach [L20]); inline chip groups mark it with a ring. The bar is the loudest mark on the surface by design, and its trailing edge comes to a shallow point at the item's mid-height (`-point`) so it reads as an indicator aimed at the row. **The element mark is now the sole focus indicator an item-group has, which makes one registration rule load-bearing:** a group with nothing to cursor must be refused the focus group (the rail cards gate theirs on `navigable`, the post-filter row count), or it takes the keyboard and paints nothing — a WCAG 1.4.11 failure. That constraint got stricter with this decision, not looser. A selected row still paints its own opaque ground (`--tugx-list-row-ground`) even though nothing paints behind the rows any more — the rule that a selection wins the pixels it covers is general, and the next thing to paint behind a row will find it settled. (b) The focus mark resolves from the **Accent** axis and a committed selection fill from the **Key** axis, and any new theme must keep the two distinguishable. This was already true everywhere — accent is orange in all six themes, the "on" selection token is cobalt in `brio` and blue in `harmony` — but it needed writing down, because the natural move for someone tuning a focus mark is to reach for the selection hue "so it matches." A role-bearing group overrides both from its own role token, keeping them distinct within the role. Full doctrine and the engine-attribute contract: [focus-language.md](focus-language.md). records the superseded wash design. [L06], [L20]

## Motion & Animation

**D23.** Motion tokens are CSS custom properties: `--tug-timing` (duration scalar), `--tug-motion` (binary toggle).

**D24.** Reduced motion via `--tug-motion: 0` and `data-tug-motion` attribute. Durations scale; motion doesn't simply disappear. [L13]

**D76.** TugAnimator wraps WAAPI. Named animation slots (WeakMap-based), three cancellation modes (snap-to-end, hold-at-current, reverse), animation groups, reduced-motion awareness. [L13, L14]

**D77.** Inactive selection uses CSS Custom Highlight API for dimmed highlight in unfocused cards.

**D78.** Child-driven ready callback: parent triggers child `setState`, child signals DOM commit via its own `useLayoutEffect`. No inline measurement of child DOM from parent. [L04]

**D79.** `requestAnimationFrame` is never used for operations depending on React state commits. RAF timing relative to React's commit cycle is not a contract. [L05]

## Scroll Behavior

**D93.** Smart auto-scroll for dynamic content uses a six-phase state machine — not scroll-event guessing. `SmartScroll` (a standalone class, not a React hook) owns a scroll container element and tracks six mutually exclusive phases: `idle`, `tracking`, `dragging`, `settling`, `decelerating`, and `programmatic`. The phase is the guard: if the phase is `dragging`, `settling`, or `decelerating` and scroll direction is up, a user is scrolling — disengage follow-bottom. A scroll change is ours only while the phase is `programmatic`, or in `idle` with the post-programmatic one-shot suppression armed for that event; any other `idle` scroll belongs to the **user** — chiefly the native scrollbar, whose thumb drag delivers no pointer or wheel events — and an upward one outside the at-bottom band disengages follow-bottom, while one landing inside the band re-engages it. The full attribution inventory and the supersede rules live in [scroll-intent.md](scroll-intent.md).

**Amended: "any other `idle` scroll belongs to the user" excludes machine-authored displacement that the owning component has identified as its own.** The browser clamps `scrollTop` when the scroll maximum shrinks, and a clamp is shaped exactly like a user scrubbing up — one `scroll` event, no pointer, no wheel, phase `idle`. Where a component can *prove* it caused the movement, treating it as intent converts machine noise into a user decision, and intent is sticky: a single clamp used to end follow-bottom for the remainder of a session. `TugListView` brackets its own commits, and a displacement it detects is attributed via `SmartScroll.noteExternalWrite()` — which syncs the baseline and advances the write counter, so the still-undelivered `scroll` event arrives explained and both disengage rules pass it over. Where no component can prove authorship, the original rule is unchanged and the user still wins.

**Amended again: the clamp is prevented, not repaired, and the bracket is a pure witness.** The clamp fires synchronously at **renderer removal, inside React's mutation phase** — no reader forces it and no scroll API can witness it — which supersedes the earlier "forced layout in the commit-phase gap" model and with it the earlier remedy. The defense is constructive: an extent floor (`.tug-list-view-floor`, written by the commit bracket to the settled extent every commit, up or down) holds the scrollable extent across the mutation gap so there is nothing to clamp against, and a lowering is a *declared rebase* that pins its own clamp and records it. The bracket no longer repairs: an unexplained displacement is counted, traced, and logged at **warn**, and the position is left exactly where the browser put it — a record means the floor has a hole, which is a bug to fix rather than a symptom to counter-write. `notifyRepair`, the repair suppression, and the `repaired` / `priorRepairHeld` fields are deleted. See [scroll-intent.md](scroll-intent.md) for the evidence, the floor's rules, and the witness contract.

Growth pins remain **phase-invisible** by design — `pinToBottom` writes `scrollTop` directly and deliberately stays in `idle`, so the phase machine still reports the user's state rather than the pin's. They are now **counter-visible**: `pinToBottom` advances `programmaticWriteSeq` on the branch that actually assigns, which is what lets the displacement bracket exempt a pin whose deferred `scroll` event has not been delivered yet. The counter counts writes that moved the scroller, not calls — advancing it on `pinToBottom`'s already-at-max early return would hold the exemption open across most streaming commits and blind the bracket exactly when the transcript is most active.

Phase transitions are driven by six DOM listeners: (1) **`scroll`** on container — fires callbacks and drives re-engagement check; (2) **`scrollend`** on container — terminal signal for deceleration and programmatic animation, with 150ms timer fallback for browsers without native support; (3) **`pointerdown`** on container, **capture phase** — enters `tracking`; (4) **`pointerup`/`pointercancel`** on document — exits `dragging` into `settling` (a 50ms window to detect momentum); if scrolls arrive within 50ms, transitions to `decelerating`; if not, transitions to `idle`; (5) **`wheel`** on container, **capture phase** — skips `tracking`, enters `dragging` immediately, starts scrollend fallback timer, disengages follow-bottom on `deltaY < 0`; (6) **`keydown`** on container — all scroll keys (PageUp, PageDown, Home, End, ArrowUp, ArrowDown, Space) enter `dragging` immediately and start the scrollend fallback timer; only scroll-up keys (PageUp, Home, ArrowUp, Shift+Space) disengage follow-bottom.

The two intent listeners run at **capture** so a user disengage is recorded synchronously ahead of every descendant listener of any phase — one that stops propagation (the Cmd-wheel inner-scroller route, [D94]) or one that flushes a commit whose layout effects pin to the bottom. `keydown` deliberately stays at the bubble phase: a key a descendant control consumes is that control's key, not scroll intent, and bubble semantics are exactly that filter. Capture registrations take capture-matched removals in `dispose()` — an options mismatch leaves the listener attached.

The `settling` phase is explicit state: `isUserScrolling` returns true during `settling` (pointer released, momentum outcome pending). The `_scrolledAfterPointerUp` flag pattern is replaced by an actual phase.

Content auto-scroll is **controller-driven**: `TugMarkdownView.doSetRegion` checks `isFollowingBottom` and calls `scrollToBottom()` after content updates. `SmartScroll` has no `ResizeObserver` — it does not observe content changes. Architecture studied from `use-stick-to-bottom` (StackBlitz Labs, MIT — see THIRD_PARTY_NOTICES.md). [L03, L06, L07]

**Scroll anchoring is gated by follow-bottom.** (This replaces "`overflow-anchor` is not used" — WebKit does honor it, and the transcript's reading-position hold depends on it.) Anchoring holds mid-viewport content still when heights change above it, which is definitionally opposed to the pin: while following, the live edge *is* the position and the pin is the only writer entitled to hold the viewport anywhere. `SmartScroll` stamps `overflow-anchor: none` on its container at the follow-bottom transition chokepoint and clears it on disengage, so descendants' opt-in goes quiet while engaged and resumes the moment the user parks to read. An inline style, not a class — the gate belongs to following-the-bottom itself, so every consumer gets it without per-surface CSS. [L06]

**Follow-bottom's polarity**: engaged is the resting state; disengaging requires a direct user action or a user-requested machine scroll that declares itself; re-engaging requires attributed arrival at the bottom. A machine scope that disengaged (an inline dialog's) may not simply restore the flag it suspended at close — it consults `isSettledAtBottom` (the user's position judged against the live geometry *or* the geometry they were last shown, the same judgment behind `growth-at-bottom-reengage`) and engages only if the user never left.

**D94.** **Cmd/Ctrl-wheel bypasses inner block scrollers and routes the wheel delta to the outer scrollport.** Inner block scrollers (FileBlock's CM6 scrollport, DiffBlock's hunks region, TerminalBlock's virtualized scroller, future code-fence viewers, future JSON-tree blocks, future structured-result panels) capture wheel events while the cursor sits over them, which stutters the outer card-scrollport skim across long transcripts. The escape hatch is surface-wide: **holding Cmd (macOS) or Ctrl (Win/Linux) while wheeling routes the delta straight to the outer scrollport, regardless of cursor position.** Plain wheel (no modifier) preserves current behavior — inner captures until exhausted, then bubbles to outer.

Mechanism: `useOuterScrollOnModifierWheel({ innerRef, outerScrollportRef })` (and its imperative twin `attachOuterScrollOnModifierWheel(inner, getOuter)`) attaches a capture-phase, non-passive `wheel` listener on the inner scrollport. On a Cmd/Ctrl-modifier hit, the handler calls `preventDefault` + `stopPropagation` and forwards `event.deltaY` to the outer scrollport via `scrollBy({ top, behavior: "auto" })`. Outer scrollport is read on every event so context-driven node swaps land without re-attaching. The hook is registered in `useLayoutEffect` so wheel routing is live before the first paint a user could plausibly wheel against.

The contract is surface-wide, not body-kind-specific: any new inner scroller a body kind introduces opts in by calling the hook with its inner-scroller ref. The same mechanism applies to pane-scoped scrollers and modal scrollers when those grow into the pattern. Cmd-click (new-tab), Cmd-+/- (browser zoom), Shift-wheel (browser-native horizontal pan), and Alt-wheel are unaffected — the listener is wheel-modifier-specific and intercepts only the Cmd/Ctrl axis.

**Bash render-on-scroll-into-view recovery.** TerminalBlock's virtualized scroller occasionally paints empty when the outer card scrolls it into view — a WebKit layer-invalidation hiccup or an IntersectionObserver-style virtualizer missing the first paint while the outer's clip rect is settling. The fix is a symptom-targeted refit: TerminalBlock subscribes to its outer scrollport via `IntersectionObserver` (root = outer scrollport, threshold 0) and re-runs the virtualizer's `applyUpdate(scrollTop)` on every entering-view transition. A `scroll` listener on the outer (gated on `inView`) covers sub-IO-threshold deltas. Refit is idempotent when no enter/exit ranges change, so spurious fires are free. [L03, L05, L06, L22]

## Feed & Transport

**D19.** Transport is tugcast binary frame protocol, not a separate HTTP server. `FeedId` enum routes frames.

**D21.** Interface-first development: define TypeScript interfaces, mock backend, then implement frontend.

**D109.** `TugConnection.onFrame` returns an unsubscribe closure (mirroring `onDisconnectState`); short-lived subscribers (per-card stores) capture it and release on `dispose()`. The `_disposed` guard is retained as a secondary safety, not as the leak fix. [L27]

## Tugbank

**D46.** Tugbank is a SQLite-backed typed defaults store. `DefaultsStore` wraps `rusqlite::Connection`. Typed `Value` enum: `Null`, `Bool`, `I64`, `F64`, `String`, `Bytes`, `Json`.

**D47.** Per-domain key-value storage with CAS (compare-and-swap) concurrency via monotonic generation counters. `set_if_generation()` returns `Written` or `Conflict`.

**D48.** Frontend reads/writes tugbank via HTTP bridge endpoints.

**D67.** `PropertyStore`: typed key-path property store per card. Schema defined via `PropertyDescriptor[]`.

**D68.** `PropertyStore.observe()` is directly compatible with `useSyncExternalStore`'s subscribe contract. [L02]

**D69.** Inspector panels participate in the responder chain.

**D74.** Dev cascade inspector: `Ctrl+Option + hover` shows token resolution chain for any element.

## Code Session & Transcript

**D96.** **Any code path that lands a `TurnEntry` on `state.transcript` must also seed the per-turn `streamingDocument` paths (`turn.${turnKey}.{assistant,thinking,tools}`) from the entry's payload.** This is the write-side counterpart to [L26]'s post-unification render contract: the assistant row's `AssistantTurnCell` observes `turn.${turnKey}.${channel}` exclusively (no fallback to `TurnEntry.*` fields), so a turn that exists on the snapshot but whose per-turn paths are empty renders blank — a textbook [L23] violation. Today the only such code path is `code-session-store/reducer.ts`'s `append-transcript` effect, paired with the `write-inflight` effects emitted by `handleTextDelta` / `handleToolUse` / `handleToolResult` / `handleToolUseStructured` for both live and replay events (the live↔replay symmetry restored by Step 18.9). Any future analogue — out-of-band ingestion, server-pushed transcript snapshot, debug-tool import, hot-reload state restore — must replicate the seeding using `serializeToolCalls` (`reducer.ts`) for the tools channel and the raw string for assistant/thinking. The reducer's pattern is the reference implementation; deviating from it without seeding strips the corresponding content from every rendered cell that comes through the alternate path. [L23], [L26]

**D97.** The session card is partitioned into **six placement zones, `Z0`-`Z5`, numbered spatially top-to-bottom**, with `Z1` subdivided into per-turn `Z1A` / `Z1B` and a per-row `Z1C` indicator zone, and `Z4` subdivided into two prompt-entry toolbar sub-slots, `Z4A` and `Z4B`. Each zone is an addressable region the telemetry renderers and the assistant-rendering registry target by ID. Most are `ReactNode` slot props on a host component; `Z5` is a state-coordinated interactive area rather than a content slot. `Z4A` and `Z4B` are *layout positions* — a leading-fixed slot and a centred-floating slot — whose occupants the toolbar assigns and is free to swap (below). Spatial numbering means any zone is findable by intuition, and a future zone inserts at its position with a clean downstream renumber — `Z0` is unambiguously "what you see first."

```
┌─ session card · transcript pane ────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Z0   top of card — reserved (empty until its slot is filled)                 │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│                                                                              │
│ SessionTranscriptHost  →  TugListView          scrolls · flex 1 1 auto          │
│                                                                              │
│   ┌─ user row ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│   │ "count lines of code..."                                Z1 user half │   │
│   │                                                           (reserved) │   │
│   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
│   ┌─ assistant row ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
│   │ [ markdown body · tool-call blocks · thinking ]                      │   │
│   │                                               Z1A  model · timestamp │   │
│   │ ◌◌◌                                          Z1C  in-flight          │   │
│   │                                                    indicator         │   │
│   │                                                    (in-flight-tip    │   │
│   │                                                    row only;         │   │
│   │                                                    collapsed         │   │
│   │                                                    otherwise)        │   │
│   │ [copy]                                       Z1B  end-state (when    │   │
│   │                                                    a committed turn  │   │
│   │                                                    has assistant-    │   │
│   │                                                    side content;     │   │
│   │                                                    collapsed         │   │
│   │                                                    otherwise)        │   │
│   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
│ Z2  STATE · TIME · CONTEXT · TASKS · JOBS [⌄]                                │
│            (TASKS per [D100], JOBS per [D102]; each reads a dimmed           │
│             None when its feed is empty)                                     │
│            ([⌄] is the fold control, [D185] — one seat at the row's          │

│      flex 0 0 auto · never scrolls · sits outside TugListView                │

┌─ session card · prompt-entry pane ──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Z3   prompt-entry status row — reserved (collapses to zero height)           │
│                                                                              │
│   Ask Tug to build, fix, or explain                                          │
│                                                                              │

│ Z4A leading-fixed · Z4B centred-floating · Z5 trailing-fixed                 │
```

The find bar ([D122]) is a fourth row in the transcript pane when it is open, inserted between the view slot and `Z2`: transcript / find bar / `Z2`. It is a flow sibling at `flex 0 0 auto`, so the view slot cedes the height exactly as it does for `Z2` telemetry growth and the scroller's `scrollTop` is untouched — a mid-scroll transcript keeps its top edge and only loses viewport height from the bottom. It is deliberately NOT a shade: it lives outside `ShadeViewController`'s `"none" | "changes" | "history"` union, and the History shade rises from the top of the *bar* while it is open. Being outside the union is a claim about **structure**, not about coexistence — ⌘F and Changes do exclude each other ([D122]), because both are modes that take over the bottom of the card. A commit message in progress is never lost to that: the exclusion routes through `CommitModeController.leave()`, which persists it first.

| Zone | Host · slot prop | Current occupant | Status |
|------|------------------|------------------|--------|
| `Z0` | `SessionCard` · `headerContent` | — | reserved — empty |
| `Z1` | transcript row · `renderTurnTrailing`, keyed by `half` | user half: —; assistant half: `Z1A` model · timestamp + `Z1B` end-state aggregate | user half reserved; assistant half shipped |
| `Z1B` | per-turn end-state aggregate in `TugTranscriptEntry`'s `controls` slot — the OK / interrupted / error / transport_lost badge, per-turn token count, per-turn wall-clock, whole-turn COPY. Rendered by `SessionZ1B`. The slot collapses to zero height (no `min-height`) when there is no end-state to show (in-flight rows). | rendered when the committed turn has an assistant-side Message (per the session-wake work's Decision F). NOT rendered for a turn that committed with only a `user_message` (interrupted-before-response) — the user_message row's own static "OK" footer is the only chrome. NEVER multiplexes the in-flight indicator (Z1C owns that) | shipped under Step 5.6 |
| `Z1C` | per-row in-flight indicator zone in `TugTranscriptEntry`'s new `inflightFooter` slot — sandwiched between the body and the `controls` slot. *Every* `TugTranscriptEntry` carries the slot uniformly; the slot collapses to zero height when `inflightFooter={null}`. NOT a list row; adds nothing to `totalRows`; no scroll-to-row helper needs to skip it; no React key churn. | `SessionZ1C` — only the **in-flight-tip row** mounts an active Z1C with a `useSyncExternalStore` subscription to a memoized `{phase, interruptInFlight}` selector; all other rows pass `null` to the slot and stay subscription-free. Indicator content (visible glyph is the three-bar `TugThinkingIndicator` with `labelPosition="hidden"`; phase label rides on `aria-label`) resolves via `sessionZ1CContent(phase, interruptInFlight)` — returns the indicator for `submitting` / `awaiting_first_token` / `streaming` / `tool_work` / `waking`; returns `null` (slot collapses) for `awaiting_approval` (the pending dialog *is* the affordance), `interruptInFlight === true` (Z1B paints the end-state once the turn commits), and `idle` / `replaying` / `errored`. The `interruptInFlight` clause rested on "interrupt is instant from the user's POV", and that holds only while the interrupt's receipt lands: before the network-resilience arc the deck dropped `turn_cancelled` at the ingest guard, so a stop that escalated to tugcode's kill ladder collapsed this slot for a turn that then never ended. `[L33]` is the general rule the arc raised from it — no wait without a horizon. In Step 5.8 the in-flight tip is the in-flight code row (`!isCommitted`); in Step 5.9 it is the row carrying the Message flagged `isLastInflightMessage`. Position inherits from the row body's indent and footer placement — no absolute positioning. | shipped under Step 5.8 |
| `Z2` | `SessionCard` · `statusBarContent` | `SessionTelemetryStatusRow` — five cells, STATE · TIME · CONTEXT · TASKS · JOBS, and nothing flanking them (the sash grip and maximize toggle the diagram once showed are long gone; the BTW cell went with the Z2 diet that made the slim width preset fit — the `/btw` answer is a placard popping from the strip's top-right now, [D128]). Measurements sit left, work sits right, and the two work cells are neighbors so the split reads as one story. `TASKS` (per [D100]) renders a pulsing-dot + `done/total` fraction — dimmed `None` when the list is empty — driving a popover of ordinal-numbered checklist rows with a count summary and COPY, and no clear affordance (the list is transcript-derived, so there is nothing deck-local to clear). `JOBS` (per [D102]) renders a pulsing-dot + the active count of running rows, scheduled rows and one active goal, driving a grouped popover — Goal / Running / Scheduled / Finished — with stop / cancel / stop-loop / clear-goal actions and a footer Clear for terminal rows. Beneath the row once sat a one-line commentary strip (per [D103]); it is retired and nothing stands under `Z2` now — the session's account reads on the masthead's lower run ([D185], [D187]), and the beat in the history that run opens ([D186]). The row's **trailing edge** is the Session card's fold control ([D185]) — one seat, two glyphs, standing there whether the card is folded or not, and authored as a sibling of the refusing status content so it is a real focus stop while the cells and the gaps keep refusing. The five cells paid for that seat out of their own budgets rather than out of `TIME`: `CONTEXT` and the `TASKS` / `JOBS` pair each gave back characters, the row's own inset halved, and the widths and `@container` rungs live in `tug-status-cell.css`. All five value wraps share one declared height, so the label rules line up by construction across both cell constructions rather than by a nudge that goes wrong at the next face change. | shipped |
| `Z3` | `TugPromptEntry` · `statusContent` + `cautionContent` | — (the session card no longer fills it) | reserved — collapsed |
| `Z4A` | `TugPromptEntry` toolbar — leading, fixed | route choice-group — two routes, `Prompt` / `Changes`, per [D122]. Controlled by `CommitModeController`, so it holds no state of its own; absent entirely when the host passes no controller (the Component Gallery) | shipped |
| `Z4B` | `TugPromptEntry` toolbar — centred, floating | `indicatorsContent` — the Claude Code identity badge and the AI settings chip (`model · effort · mode`). The Project badge left with the Z4B diet that made the slim width preset fit, [D128] | shipped |
| `Z5` | `TugPromptEntry` submit button (no slot) | lifecycle-driven Submit / Stop / Awaiting / Stopping / Reconnecting | shipped |

The transcript pane (the upper `TugSplitPanel`) is a flex column — `Z0` header, `TugListView` (`flex 1 1 auto`, scrolls), with `SessionJumpToBottomButton` as floating chrome anchored at the list's bottom edge, and `Z2` status bar — so a telemetry update in `Z2` grows into space the list cedes without repositioning the scroll, and `Z2`, living *outside* `TugListView`, never scrolls with the transcript. `Z1` is per-turn rather than card-level: one trailing slot wired twice per turn (`half: "user" | "assistant"`), spatially inside the transcript pane between `Z0` and the `Z2` row. `Z1B` (the per-turn end-state aggregate) and `Z1C` (the per-row in-flight indicator zone) are intentionally separate sub-zones — `Z1B` lives in `TugTranscriptEntry`'s `controls` slot (collapses to zero when empty); `Z1C` lives in `TugTranscriptEntry`'s new `inflightFooter` slot, sandwiched between the body and `controls` (every row carries the slot uniformly; only the in-flight-tip row mounts a Z1C that subscribes to phase and paints the indicator; all other rows pass `null` and the slot collapses). The pre-Step-5.6 `Z1B` multiplexed both responsibilities ("indicator ↔ end-state display") inside one component; under per-Message rows the multiplex is structurally awkward, so the two responsibilities split into two `TugTranscriptEntry` slots with disjoint domains and independent collapse rules. An earlier revision of `Z1C` placed it as transcript-level chrome below `TugListView`; that ended up marooned at the bottom of the pane instead of at the in-flight row's footer, so the design moved to a per-row slot that inherits position from the row body's indent for free. `Z5` is the submit button — a single DOM node whose label / `disabled` / `data-mode` are driven by the lifecycle state machine and which is never swapped across mode transitions, per [L26].

The prompt-entry toolbar lays out three zones in one flex row. `Z4A` sits at the leading edge and `Z5` (the submit button) at the trailing edge — both fixed and content-sized; `Z4B` floats centred between them, two equal flex spacers splitting the free width so `Z4B`’s centre lands at the midpoint of the gap between the route group and `Z5`. A `Z4-lead` occupant once rendered inside that leading slot ahead of `Z4A`, holding the Session card's fold button; it retired with the button when the control moved to `Z2`'s trailing edge ([D185]), the seat was not repurposed, and `TugPromptEntry` carries no `leadingContent` prop at all — so the leading edge is `Z4A` alone, empty in a host that passes no controller (the Component Gallery) exactly as before. `Z4A` and `Z4B` are *positions*, not component assignments: the toolbar fills them with the route choice-group and the indicator cluster (the `indicatorsContent` slot — the Claude Code identity badge and the AI settings chip, the identity chrome naming the prompt entry’s target), and which occupant takes which position is a layout decision free to change. Today the two-route choice-group sits in `Z4A` and the indicator cluster in `Z4B`. Both of `Z4A`'s segments are always mounted, so the group's width is constant and a route switch never shifts the centred `Z4B` occupant. The maximize toggle and the prompt-area sash grip were card chrome rather than zone occupants, and both are long gone from the `Z2` status bar, whose trailing edge is now the fold control's seat — which is why the `Z3` prompt-entry status row, once home to the badges and the toggle, is empty and collapsed.

A zone's *location* is contract; its *occupant* is not — every zone is a generic addressable slot. `Z0`, the `Z1` user half, and `Z3` are reserved: the slot exists in the API, default content is `null`, and the row collapses to zero height until something fills it (the session card leaves `Z3` empty today, so its row has no height). `Z1C` is a per-row `inflightFooter` content slot on `TugTranscriptEntry`: every row carries it uniformly and the slot collapses to zero when passed `null`; only the in-flight-tip row passes a live `SessionZ1C` instance. Established by the Step 20.x turn-surface work (archived as); `Z3`'s `cautionContent` slot was added by the assistant-rendering drift-detection work; the `Z4A` / `Z4B` split and the `Z2` chrome relocations came from the prompt-entry-zones work; the `Z1B` / `Z1C` split (separating per-turn end-state from per-row in-flight indicator) came from Step 5.6 of the tide-session-wake work under the per-Message render-layout refactor and was reshaped in Step 5.8 from "transcript-level chrome below `TugListView`" into the per-row `inflightFooter` slot after the transcript-level placement marooned the indicator at the pane's bottom edge. [L06], [L26]

**D100.** The active task list surfaces as a `TASKS` cell on the [D97] `Z2` status bar — not inline in the assistant turn(s) that issued each call, and not as a separate sub-zone. Anthropic's `claude` ≥ `v2.1.148` replaces the old `TodoWrite` tool (single call carrying the canonical list) with a per-item CRUD family — `TaskCreate` (one call per task; the server assigns a monotonic `taskId`) and `TaskUpdate` (`{ taskId, status }` flips one task's status). A typical session produces ~3× more events than the old `TodoWrite` did (4 × `TaskCreate` up front + 8 × `TaskUpdate` interleaved for a 4-item list is representative), so inline-per-turn rendering would litter the transcript with a status-flip per row; the status-bar cell keeps the live picture one fixed glance away without claiming separate vertical real estate. The cell renders a `TugProgressIndicator` pulsing-dot + `N/M` label (`done` over `total`) inside `SessionTelemetryStatusRow` alongside `STATE` / `TIME` / `CONTEXT` / `JOBS`; clicking it opens a popover whose body is a vertical stack of `TugPopupListItem` rows (one per task, numbered `1.`–`N.` in source order, status-driven indicator + `subject`, with the optional `description` surfacing in a `TugTooltip` on hover). `/tasks` is a Tug-local command whose surface is this popover (`SessionTelemetryStatusRowHandle.openTasks`, the `/context` imperative-open precedent). When no task list is active the cell still occupies its reserved width but reads `None` (dimmed) and the ring is suppressed — the layout stays anchored so the surrounding cells never reflow. When the session is `idle`, the ring renders in its `stopped` state (closed outlined circle, no animation) in both the cell and the popover, so motion implies ongoing work and stillness implies it is over. The cell is fed by a pure reducer — `reduceTaskListState(toolCalls): TaskListState` in `select-task-list.ts` — that folds the event stream by walking `transcript[].toolCalls[]` then the in-flight `toolCallMap`'s values in order, appending one `TaskItem` per `TaskCreate` (with `status: "pending"` and `taskId` parsed from the `tool_result.content` "Task #N created successfully:" echo, falling back to monotonic count) and mutating-by-id for each `TaskUpdate`; only terminal (`status: "done"`) calls fold so an in-flight `TaskCreate` with no assigned id yet is skipped until its `tool_result` lands. The cell renderer reads through `useTaskListState` (a `useSyncExternalStore` hook per [L02]) and the popover composes `TugTaskItem` primitives directly, with role-driven accent colors (in-progress rows use `TugProgress role="action"` + `TugLabel role="action"`; completed rows use `--tugx-task-item-success-color` on the check glyph) so the surface picks up standard tone tokens instead of bespoke aliases. No wire-level clear event exists; the list lives for the session, and the active rule (non-empty list with at least one non-completed item) governs the `N/M` vs `None` reading. Session clear (`/clear`) resets the cell by way of resetting the store. The TASKS cell is the canonical surface for *current state* — the assembled list, one fixed glance away. The transcript carries per-call inline markers via `TaskInlineToolBlock` — a tiny `<ListChecks size=14>` + `<TugLabel size="sm" emphasis="calm">` row for each `TaskCreate` and `TaskUpdate` event (`"Created: <subject>"` / `"Started: <subject>"` / `"Completed: <subject>"`), so a reader scrolling the transcript can see *when in the conversation flow* each task action happened. The two surfaces never duplicate work: the cell answers "what's on the list now?"; the marker answers "when did this happen?". The marker uses the `calm` emphasis (italic + muted gray) so every row reads as ambient annotation subordinate to the TASKS cell — color is reserved for the rare error case (`role="danger"`, `emphasis="normal"`). The marker intentionally carries no `BlockChrome` — no frame, no status stripe, no error band — so it stays inline-flow, not another tool-call card competing with the cell for attention. The dead `registerToolBlock("todowrite", …)` entry is removed; the original Step 24's inline `TodoWriteToolBlock` was already silent on every `v2.1.148+` session — `TodoWrite` is no longer emitted — so the broader rework is also a correctness fix, not just a UX one. **Amended: a run of consecutive same-verb markers folds to ONE row.** The per-event grain assumed the volume of a short list; the volume a long session reaches differs in kind, because the task store is scoped to the *session* while the model writes a checklist per *turn* and the wire carries no clear event — the only cleanup available is `TaskUpdate status:"deleted"`, one call per task, so tidying nineteen finished tasks emitted nineteen `Deleted` markers and the transcript painted a wall of them. The cleanup was the mess. `groupTaskMarkerRuns` (`session-transcript-task-runs.ts`) is a pure fold over the run's `Message[]` marking each member `lead` or `member`; the lead renders a `TaskInlineRunBlock` — the marker's own grammar, same glyph and tone — reading `Deleted 19 tasks` with every member's subject on the row, clipped to one line with the untruncated list in the tooltip (the error row's pattern). Nothing is dropped and no row grows taller. A member must be a settled, top-level, non-error Task* call whose verb is resolved (a streaming placeholder never folds — its identity would change under the reader as the input arrives), and ANY other message ends a run: the rule is deliberately conservative rather than mirroring the render loop's skip list, which would rot the moment that list changed. Runs shorter than three are left alone, since two rows show both subjects in full and are not a wall — so the interleaved start/complete rhythm this decision originally described never folds at all, no two adjacent markers sharing a verb. [D101]'s classification is unchanged: both names stay bespoke.

**History.** D100 originally specified a separate pinned sub-zone — `Z2A` (leading-fixed) paired with a renamed `Z2B` (trailing). That iteration shipped briefly (a `SessionCardPinnedTodo` renderer + `statusBarLeadingContent` prop), but produced too much vertical-layout shift and broke the cell-row rhythm of `Z2`. The decision was reverted to single `Z2` + the `TASKS` cell described above; the `Z2A` / `Z2B` split is no longer present in the code, and D97's zone table reflects single-`Z2`. The original artifacts (`SessionCardPinnedTodo`, `statusBarLeadingContent`, `--tugx-pinned-todo-*` tokens, `:has()`-based collapse) were removed. [D97], [L02], [L06], [L20]

**D101.** Tool visibility in the Dev transcript is classified by a single editable policy table — `TOOL_VISIBILITY_POLICY` in `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/cards/session-tool-visibility-policy.ts` — with three buckets and one source-of-truth rule per bucket. **Bespoke** is implicit: a tool has a bespoke wrapper iff it is registered in `TOOL_BLOCK_REGISTRY` via the dispatch's bottom-of-file `registerToolBlock(...)` calls (the `BESPOKE_REGISTRATIONS` array exported as `BESPOKE_TOOL_NAMES`). Bespoke names MUST NOT appear in the policy table — that would be a double-classification, caught by the governance test. **Hidden** is explicit: the policy table's `hidden` entries map to a shared exported `NullToolBlock` via two short-circuits — `resolveToolBlock` returns `NullToolBlock` for any hidden name ahead of the registry lookup, and `detectToolCallDrift` exempts hidden names from the `unknown_tool` check. Hidden is for control-channel machinery the user does not need to see (`ToolSearch`, `ScheduleWakeup`, `EnterPlanMode`, `ExitPlanMode`, `PushNotification`) or for tools whose surface lives elsewhere (`TaskCreate` / `TaskUpdate` per [D100] — the TASKS status-bar cell is the sole surface). **Default-intent** is explicit: the policy table's `default-intent` entries become `AUDIT_CONFIRMED_DEFAULT_TOOLS`, routing through `DefaultToolBlock` (the JsonTree fallback) with no `unknown_tool` caution. Every `default-intent` entry's `rationale` MUST cite the follow-on step's `#step-` anchor — the governance test enforces it, so a default-intent entry is always an *explicit TODO* with a planned bespoke wrapper, never a forever-bucket. The governance test pins the two invariants that catch real mistakes code review would miss: (c) every `default-intent` entry's rationale contains `"Awaiting"` and `"#step-"` — the only mechanism preventing `default-intent` from becoming a forever-bucket; and (d) the union of bespoke + policy covers the v2.1.148 canonical tool set — so a new Claude Code tool in a future release fails CI until it is explicitly classified. Shape / parse / no-double-classification checks were considered and intentionally not landed: the entry shape is enforced by the `ToolVisibilityEntry` interface, and double-classification is rare-and-obvious enough that code review is the right gate. MCP (`mcp__*`) is intentionally excluded from the policy per the project's MCP non-goal — `mcp__*` names route through `DefaultToolBlock` and produce `unknown_tool` cautions; the caution count is the deferral's signal. Moving a tool between buckets is a one-line policy edit; promoting to bespoke means a new `registerToolBlock` call + removing the policy entry. [D04], [D11], [D100]

**D102.** AI-initiated background jobs surface as a `JOBS` cell on the [D97] `Z2` status bar, mirroring the `TASKS` grammar ([D100]) so the row reads as one instrument: a `TugProgressIndicator` pulsing-dot + a label (dimmed `None` when empty), a popover behind a click. The cell counts what is outstanding — running rows, scheduled rows, and one active goal — and lingers its recently-finished rows for five minutes rather than snapping to `None` the instant the last one ends. The cell's pose is a pure function of the ledger only — `jobsCellPose` in `select-jobs.ts`: any running job → `running`; else any failed → `aborted` (the danger pose [D100] reserved gets its first consumer; it holds until the user clears it, a new job starts, or the session clears); else `completed`. **No idle demotion** — the one deliberate semantic divergence from TASKS: a background job genuinely runs *between* turns, so an idle session must not quiet a running dot. The feed is a **session-lifetime jobs ledger** held in `CodeSessionState.jobs` (not a pure fold over `toolCalls` — the lifecycle events are not all tool calls, and Clear must *forget* rows): claude ≥ 2.1.173 emits `system/task_started` / `system/task_updated` frames for background `Bash` / `Agent` (`run_in_background: true`), which tugcode forwards verbatim as `task_started` / `task_updated` IPC frames from **both** routing tiers (the terminal flip arrives mid-turn for a fast failure, inter-turn for an idle completion). Ledger updates are idempotent upserts/flips keyed by claude's `task_id`: inserts from the `task_started` frame AND the launch `tool_result` echo (`backgroundTaskId` / `agentId`), terminal flips from `task_updated.patch.status` (`completed` / `failed` / `killed`→stopped), the `wake_started` trigger fold, and a defensive `TaskStop`-tool fold — first terminal flip wins. **The wire carries no backgrounded discriminant**: a foreground subagent's `task_started` is shape-identical (the empirical contract lives in `stream-json-catalog/v2.1.173-jobs-spike/`), so inserts gate on the launching tool call via `tool_use_id` — `isJobLaunch`: explicitly backgrounded (`input.run_in_background === true`) **or a `Monitor` watcher**, which is background activity by nature (a watcher's `task_type` reads `local_bash`, so its `kind: "monitor"` derives from the tool name). The gate also keeps an async subagent's internal jobs out (their launching calls aren't in the deck's stream). **Monitor rows have their own flip rule**: a watcher reaches terminal only via `task_updated` — the wake-trigger fold exempts `kind: "monitor"`. On the captured wire this is defense in depth (mid-life monitor events wake claude via task-id-less re-inits, and a monitor's only `task_notification` is terminal with a status agreeing with `task_updated`: completed/completed on natural exit, killed/stopped on timeout or kill), but the exemption pins the invariant against future per-event notifications. Replay never populates the ledger (a replayed launch would pulse forever — its lifecycle frames don't replay), and a fresh `session_init` stale-marks `running` rows to `stopped` (a respawned claude cannot carry jobs across). The popover (`JobsPopoverContent`) groups Goal / Running / Scheduled / Finished, each section rendered only when non-empty, and is the surface of the Tug-local `/bashes` command (`SessionTelemetryStatusRowHandle.openJobs`). The `/goal` rides here rather than with the checklist because it is a standing condition with an evaluator loop — background work, not a checklist item; it is its own snapshot field (`CodeSessionSnapshot.goal`, reduced in `select-goal.ts` from `/goal` submissions, `goal_feedback` frames, and the goal cycle's commit — a goal run is ONE result cycle, probe: `tugcode/probes/goal-loop/FINDINGS.md#q01-goal`), and its row carries an idle-gated clear (`clearGoal` sends `/goal clear` — a live goal run is stopped via the interrupt control). Job rows carry a status dot, end-truncated description (tooltip for the full text), elapsed (live 1Hz leaf that mounts only while the popover is open), and a stop button on running rows that fires the pre-existing `stop_task` verb (`CodeSessionStore.stopJob` → tugcode `handleStopTask` → control request); no optimistic flip — the capture confirmed claude answers with `task_updated{killed}` + a `stopped` notification. The footer carries the zero-bucket-drop summary — live watchers split out of the running bucket as "watching" (`1 running, 1 watching, 2 done`), while the cell's count and pose treat all rows identically — and a **Clear** button: a deck-local wipe of terminal rows only — running rows always survive — disabled when nothing is clearable. Terminology guard: despite the wire's `task_*` spelling these frames are the *jobs* vocabulary, unrelated to the `TaskCreate`/`TaskUpdate` tool calls behind TASKS; deck-side names use the job vocabulary throughout. Layout: the cell budgets are measured in `tug-status-cell.css` against the slim preset's strip content box; container-query collapse order TIME → TASKS → JOBS (a running job is live signal; the task list is recoverable from the transcript). Originating plan: **History.** As first shipped, the gate was flag-only (`run_in_background === true`) and monitors were invisible — an armed watcher had no surface for its entire lifetime until it fired, and no kill switch. The `Monitor` extension (gate, kind, flip exemption, the "watching" summary bucket) came from, pinned against `test-monitor-lifecycle-raw.jsonl`. (The `session_init` stale-mark now flips `running` rows only — scheduled rows survive, since every tugcode respawn resumes and claude ≥ 2.1.204 re-fires pending scheduled work; probe: `tugcode/probes/goal-loop/FINDINGS.md#q02-loop`.) [D97], [D100], [L02], [L06], [L26]


**D103.** *(Superseded by [D186] — one Rust digester taps the wire once and every account of what a session is doing reads its lines. Kept for what it settled, and for what it cost.)* PULSE — app-wide color commentary — **watched the wire**: the commentator narrated from the same session-tagged frames every deck received, never from a hand-phrased re-telling, displayed as a one-line strip beneath the [D97] `Z2` status row. Three findings in it are durable, and are why the entry stays rather than going quietly. **(1) Generality was the cause of the first failure.** The v1 architecture — a route-agnostic `pulse_fact` note-card bus fed by a tugcode producer observing outbound IPC — was retired because every lossy hand-off was a place truth thinned out, and when the untyped observer silently mismatched the wire shape the speak-pressured model fabricated. A narrator kept dumb about every route can only eat pre-chewed prose; a truthful Claude-Code-only account beats a general one that lies, and a future route integrates through its own tap into the one digester rather than through a bus that flattens them all. **(2) There is no niche for a model-written instant beat.** The model-based commentator was built, walked, and retired across nine prompt versions, all recorded in the spike README: when it spoke often it restated the visible transcript, and when it spoke rarely it was unpredictable. Color commentary has no stable niche beside a transcript the reader follows natively. The beat is deterministic, sub-second and free, and *a model in that loop is not to be re-proposed*. **(3) A second language on the wire bought a type check and cost a daemon.** The line machine was `tugpulse`, a tugcode-codebase sibling binary that tugcast lazily spawned and supervised, chosen so the frames were parsed with the real `OutboundMessage` types and shape drift was a compile error rather than a silent starve. [D186] pays for that check with a fixture test over a recorded frame stream instead, and deletes the binary, its spawn contract, its build and signing steps, its capped `pulse_lines` ledger table, and its `dev.tugapp.pulse/enabled` switch. **What the entry described that never shipped in the form it describes:** a deck-side strip component standing under `Z2`, and a per-scope ticker module of its own — both named here and nowhere in the code, which is what made superseding this entry cheaper than amending it. Originating plans: (v1), (the wire-tap rework). [D97], [D102], [D186], [D187], [L02], [L06], [L19], [L20], [L26]
