Enablement pulls from a new `stackDepth` on the menu-state payload — panes sharing the *focused* pane's slot, 0 when it holds no slot or nothing is selected — and the item validates enabled iff that exceeds 1, deliberately without the deselected-deck escape hatch its neighbours take, because Reveal Stack acts on a specific pane's stack and picking one for the user is the non-obvious-target failure. **The handle toggles, and that is ordering rather than a race:** an open `TugPopupMenu` closes on any chain dispatch that is not its own selection blink, `REVEAL_STACK` travels the chain, and `sendToFirstResponder` runs the responder action *before* notifying observers — while a closed menu has no observer registered at all. So open → the toggle queues false and the observer queues false again; closed → the toggle simply opens. Both single-valued. What this decision does **not** do: cycle the stack (⌃\` stays deck-wide; the picker plus arrow keys is the keyboard path — superseded by [D124], which adds a no-look cycle and makes ⌘R's owner a preference), give free panes any behavior (no slot, no stack, no badge, no chord, and no "most recently active stack" fallback), repurpose Cmd-click in a pane's *content* area, revive buried panes' DOM, change how panes enter or leave a slot, or add a tab strip. `deck-store-selectors.ts` (`slotStackOf` / `SlotStackEntry`), `lib/pane-title.ts` (`composePaneTitleBarText` / `paneTitleBarTextFor`), `lib/card-title-store.ts` (`version`), `components/chrome/deck-canvas.tsx` (the derivation and the raise), `components/chrome/tug-pane.tsx` (badge, picker, `revealStack`, the `REVEAL_STACK` responder entry, the meta-click branch), `components/tugways/internal/tug-popup-menu.tsx` (controlled open), `components/tugways/action-vocabulary.ts` + `action-dispatch.ts` (`reveal-stack`), `lib/host-menu-state.ts` (`stackDepth`), `tugapp/Sources/AppDelegate.swift` (the item, its selector, the parse, the validation, the Reload move). A Cmd-click reveals the stack and a Cmd-drag still drags: `dragMoved` latches only inside a rAF callback, so the two never contend. Originating plan: Full doctrine: [pane-model.md § Three geometry modes](pane-model.md). [D121], [L02], [L06], [L09], [L10], [L11], [L24]

**D124.** **A slot's stack gets a second, no-look switching command, and ⌘R's owner becomes a preference rather than a verdict.** [D123] gave the stack a picker and put ⌘R on it. That is the right gesture while you are still learning what is in a slot and the wrong one once you know: the picker has to be read, so switching costs a look even when the user already knows the card they want is the one behind. `Go ▸ Cycle Stack` is the counterpart — it brings a buried pane forward and puts **nothing** on screen, which is the entire point. Nothing to read means nothing to aim at, which means the press can be repeated blind. **Cycling raises the bottom-most pane in the slot, not the one below the front, and that is what makes it a ring.** Each raise sends the outgoing front pane exactly one place back, so a depth-N slot returns to its starting order after N presses and the user can count instead of look. Raising the next-one-down is the obvious rule and it is broken: with three panes it ping-pongs between the top two forever and the third is unreachable, because every raise rewrites the order the "next" was computed from. Raising the buried-longest is also what ⌘-backtick does with windows, so the behavior is borrowed rather than invented. It needs no new store mutation either — it is the raise [D123] already defined, aimed at the last `SlotStackEntry` — and the pane answers `CYCLE_STACK` itself rather than delegating to its title bar, because there is no transient UI to own; the title bar is [D123]'s route only because the picker's open bit lives there. The raise moves the first responder to the pane that came up, so the *next* press is answered by that pane reading its own freshly-ordered stack, which is why one press is stateless and N presses still compose. **Neither command is right for everyone, so the chord is a setting.** Both items always exist in the Window menu and both gate identically on `stackDepth > 1`; the preference (Settings ▸ General, `stack-chord-store.ts`, persisted at `stackChord` in the app's own tugbank domain, like the theme) moves only the key equivalent. It has to move on the host side — AppKit resolves a menu key equivalent before the `WKWebView` sees the keydown, so the frontend cannot claim or release ⌘R by any route — which is why the value rides the menu-state payload and `applyStackChordKeyEquivalent()` assigns it in `updateMenuState`, outside AppKit's key-equivalent scan, exactly as the dynamic ⇧⌘S for Save As… already does. The preference is not deck state, so `initHostMenuState` subscribes to its store directly and re-posts, the same way it subscribes to `cardTitleStore`. The default is **cycle**: the picker keeps two doors that need no chord at all (the badge, and Cmd-click on the title bar), while the cycle has none, so defaulting the chord to the surface that already has doors would leave the new gesture unreachable without a settings trip. What this decision does **not** do: add a reverse direction (a ring returns home on its own, and ⇧⌘R is `Maker ▸ Reload`), show any HUD or transient overlay during a cycle (the raised pane's own title bar is the feedback — a HUD would put back the thing the user is trying to skip), cycle anything but the focused pane's slot, or give free panes and the rail cards any behavior. `stack-chord-store.ts`, `settings-api.ts` (`readStackChord` / `putStackChord`), `main.tsx` (boot seed + DEFAULTS push), `components/chrome/tug-pane.tsx` (the `CYCLE_STACK` responder entry), `components/tugways/action-vocabulary.ts` + `action-dispatch.ts` (`cycle-stack`), `lib/host-menu-state.ts` (`stackChord`), `components/tugways/cards/settings-general-body.tsx` (the choice), `tugapp/Sources/AppDelegate.swift` (the item, its selector, the parse, the shared validation, `applyStackChordKeyEquivalent`). Handing the chord to one item takes it from the other — there is one chord, not two. [D123], [D121], [L02], [L11], [L24]

**D125.** **A card's own name replaces its type name in the title bar; it does not extend it.** [D123] put a Pane's name in one place (`lib/pane-title.ts`) and made every naming surface — title bar, slot-stack picker, Window menu — go through it. That fixed the disagreement between them but preserved the composition each had inherited, which joined the registry title to the live override: a Text card holding `changes-rework.md` was called **`File : changes-rework.md`**, on its title bar and in every list. The first word is redundant twice over. The filename already says it is a file, and the row draws that card's own `FileText` icon immediately to its left — [D123]'s own "a row is a miniature of the title bar" is what makes the word provably surplus, because the glyph carries the type and the text no longer has to. **So an override REPLACES the registry title.** The registry title is the FALLBACK — what a card of that type is called before it has a name of its own, which is what an unopened Text card ("File") or a Diff card ("Diff") legitimately shows — rather than a category stamped in front of every name. A **group name still prefixes**, because it is redundant with nothing: it is the name the user gave a multi-tab Pane, and no other part of the title bar carries it, so a named group of open files reads `Notes : draft.md`. This also retires a workaround: a card whose identity is entirely dynamic used to declare an EMPTY registry title so that its override would stand alone (the About and Session cards both do this), and that trick is now simply the rule — the empty declarations are harmless but no longer load-bearing, and a new dynamic card needs no such trick. One function moved (`composePaneTitleBarText`), and because [D123] had already collapsed the three surfaces onto it, the title bar, the picker, and the Window menu all changed together with no second edit — which is the argument for consolidating a rule before changing it. `lib/pane-title.ts`. [D123], [L10]

## Commands & Chords

**D126.** **Which chord a command gets is derived from a modifier algebra, not chosen from what happens to be free.** [L30] made every chord a registry fact, which settled *where* chords live and said nothing about *which* chord a command should get — so the chords accreted, each one taking whatever was near a mnemonic letter and climbing the modifier stack whenever plain ⌘ was already spoken for. That produces a keymap that is a list of memorized facts: unpredictable for a user, and offering an author no way to tell a good grant from a bad one. **Two base tiers and two operators now generate every modifier set in use** — ⌘ the universal tier (verbs any Mac user knows, plus the highest-frequency Tug verbs; digits are places), ⌃⌘ the Tug tier (surfaces, shades, modes, themes, app-specific machinery, anchored by the macOS-conventional ⌃⌘F Full Screen), ⇧ the counterpart operator (reverse/widen/undo), ⌥ the variant operator (same verb, altered object or form) — and every remaining set is a composition with a derived reading: ⌥⇧⌘ both twists ("…as Plain Text"), ⌃⇧⌘ the counterpart of a Tug-tier command, ⌃⌥⌘ the advanced form of one. Plain ⌥ letters (glyph typing) and plain ⌃ letters (the caret's emacs set) are **closed forever**, with ⌥⇥ and ⌃\` grandfathered as non-printing exceptions that would not be granted today. The argument for adopting this rather than inventing something is that it was already half-latent in shipped bindings — ⌘V → ⌥⌘V → ⌥⇧⌘V, ⌘Z → ⇧⌘Z, ⌘W → ⌥⌘W — so writing it down moved nine chords and explained the rest. Six rules ride with it, and the load-bearing two are **pairing** (a ⇧/⌥-composed chord shares its key with the base it twists; a chord with no base to vary is a plain-⌘ or ⌃⌘ candidate, never a taller stack chosen because the short one was taken) and **scarcity** (plain ⌘'s free slots go only to commands hit many times an hour, so the frequency claim is part of the grant). The rest: the arrows exemption (⌥⌘↑/↓ has no ⌘ base because ⌘↑/↓ is composer currency; ⇧ still means "to the extreme"), closed sets stay closed, ⌘. parity, and **menu placement is a chord decision** — a `menuEligible` chord preempts every scoped binding regardless of focus ([commands.md](commands.md), four-layer resolution), so one decision records both the tier and the `menuEligible`/`scope` choice.

**The algebra spends exactly the band [D124] cleared, and re-homes both chords [D124] placed.** That entry deleted ⌃⌘S/B/C/G/H "which is what leaves the ⌃⌘ band free", gave ⇧⌘P to the composer's Prompt route, and moved `cycle-permission-mode` to ⌃⌘P *in both doors* because either alone leaves the menu bar swallowing the chord. The continuity is the point rather than an accident: **Prompt Route moves ⇧⌘P → ⌃⌘P** (route selection is Tug machinery; ⇧ was carrying nothing, since there is no ⌘P base for Prompt to be the counterpart of) and **Cycle Permission Mode moves ⌃⌘P → ⌃⌥⌘P**, debuting the advanced tier — permission modes govern agent autonomy, the quintessential expert feature — and vacating ⌃⌘P is precisely what lets Prompt Route land on its own tier. Both doors move again, for [D124]'s reason. **Show/Hide Changes ⇧⌘C → ⌃⌘C** and **Show/Hide History ⇧⌘H → ⌃⌘H** (shade toggles are machinery; the twins move together), **Generate a Commit Message ⇧⌘M → ⌃⌘M** (still composer-scoped, now joining one mnemonic cluster: ⌃⌘C the shade, ⌃⌘M the message, ⌃⌘A the claim), **Next Theme ⌥⌘T → ⌃⌘T**, and **Insert File ⌘I → ⌃⌘I**, which frees ⌘I from its italic baggage under scarcity. Freed and returned to the pool: ⇧⌘P, ⇧⌘C, ⇧⌘H, ⇧⌘M, ⌥⌘T, ⌘I. Every move is a registry-default edit; **user overrides are untouched and no migration exists**, because an override is a per-command binding list in tugbank where *absent means registry default* — a moved default is exactly the case the override design already absorbs.

**Next Theme's chord enters the registry, because a chord that exists only as a Swift construction literal is invisible to the app that owns it.** `next-theme` carried `menuItemId: "view.nextTheme"` with **no bindings at all** — ⌥⌘T lived solely in `buildMenuBar()`, so the keymap pane could not show it, `resolveChord` could not reason about it, and no user could rebind it. It gains a real binding (⌃⌘T, `menuEligible`, `preventDefault`), which is sufficient on its own: `host-menu-state.ts` publishes a chord claim for a **non-`mirrored`** entry too, since presence in `menuChords()` is what the push tests. Construction literals for all four moved menu items are edited in the same change — they are pre-first-push defaults only, but a cold-start menu bar must never flash a chord the registry no longer holds.

**Claim All ⌃⌘A and Disclaim All ⌃⇧⌘A become registry commands rather than buttons the keyboard cannot reach.** Both are `routing: "first-responder"` with composer-responder-scoped bindings, registered in `tug-prompt-entry.tsx` beside the existing `commit-auto-message` registration under the *same* `commitActive` gate — because raising the Changes shade **is** entering commit mode, and the shade is a **passive** sheet that leaves the composer holding focus, so the composer's responder is where the chords are genuinely live. **Registration and handling live in different components, unlike `commit-auto-message`** (which does both in the composer): these two are handled in `session-card.tsx`, because the card is the only component holding `changesController`, and the chain makes the split work — the composer's responder sits below the card's, so a first-responder action the composer does not claim falls through. The gate is where the surface is; the semantics are where the controller is. **⌃⌘A is deliberately stronger than either shade button.** The shade wires two bulk buttons (claim-all-unattributed and claim-all-orphaned); the chord claims **both buckets at once**, because a single keyboard verb meaning "make everything claimable in front of me mine" is worth more than one that mirrors one button and sends the user to the mouse for the other. The buttons remain the granular path. **The pair is not an inverse on one set** — ⌃⌘A acts on what is *not yet* this session's, ⌃⇧⌘A on what *is* — so ⇧-as-counterpart is carrying "the opposite bulk verb of this shade", not "the same set, reversed"; the algebra promises a shared key and an opposite sense, never set inversion, and this pair is the reason to say so out loud. Both no-op on an empty path list or a pending claim/disclaim round-trip, read non-reactively from `changeset-verb-store`. Bindings are the door, so `lintCommandTable` passes with no `internal` declaration; the keymap pane shows them as scoped, non-rebindable rows.

**⌘. parity is a matcher change, not a routing change.** Five components hand-matched `metaKey && key === "."` even though `cancel-dialog` owns both ⎋ and ⌘. in the registry — `tug-alert.tsx`, `tug-confirm-popover.tsx`, `tug-sheet.tsx`, `tug-placard.tsx`, `tug-editor-context-menu.tsx`. They are **not naive bypasses**: each documents why chain routing is unreliable for its surface (a portaled popover whose buttons never promote; a placard outside any focus mode), so the [L30] defect is only the authored chord match — which also mis-matches by `event.key` label instead of `code` identity. One exported helper, `isCancelChordEvent(event)` in `keymap-registry.ts`, returns true when the event matches any `cancel-dialog` binding **except Escape**, and the five surfaces call it in place of their literals while keeping every dismissal path and propagation posture exactly as documented. **Excluding Escape is load-bearing**: the engine owns ⎋'s ladder for alerts, sheets, and context menus, and a helper that matched it would let five conversions quietly steal it. `chordMatchesEvent`'s parameter widens to a five-field `ChordEventFields` structural type rather than each call site casting — the cast would be a lie repeated five times, and the widening is what lets one predicate serve native listeners and React synthetic events alike. The context menu's extra `ctrlKey` alternative is dropped: the registry chord is the one truth. **Parity extends to dismissal, not to field-revert.** ⌘. mirrors ⎋ wherever ⎋ dismisses a surface or backs out of a mode, so the sweep adds it to the dev error overlay, both image-lightbox paths, and the editor's completion popup. It is deliberately withheld where ⎋ *reverts an in-field edit* — `tug-filter-field`, `tug-value-input`, `tug-slider`, and the gallery's in-flight hue scrub — because that ⎋ is form-control currency inside a typing surface, editing the value rather than leaving the surface, and ⌘. there would be both surprising and outside macOS convention.

The doctrine doc is [chord-tiers.md](chord-tiers.md) — the algebra, the six rules, the plain-⌘ free-pool ledger, the macOS never-bind list, and the known anomalies, including the deferred Cascade ⌃⌥C / Tile ⌃⌥T re-home and the shade toggles' un-detachable menu claim. `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/command-registry.ts`, `keymap-registry.ts`, `chord-format.ts`, `action-vocabulary.ts`, `tug-prompt-entry.tsx`, `cards/session-card.tsx`, the five converted surfaces; `tugapp/Sources/AppDelegate.swift` (`buildMenuBar` literals). Originating plan: This supersedes the chord spellings embedded in the prose of [D122]–[D125] without editing those entries, and most directly [D124], whose ⌃⌘ band it spends and whose two P-chord placements it re-homes; it also removes the stale comment left by [D125]'s retirement of the ⌃⌘C `!changes` alias, and makes [D117]'s ⇧⌘C collision note obsolete. [L30], [L02], [L11], [D117], [D122], [D124], [D125]

---

## Model Work

**D127.** **Auxiliary model work runs on SharedAgents over the user's own subscription, never on a model Tug ships.** Tug's non-conversational model work — pulse intent headlines, shell-command arbitration, and whatever comes next — runs on an app-scoped pool of persistent, job-constrained headless `claude` workers authenticated by the Claude Code subscription the app already requires. **Tug does not ship, download, host, or update model weights.** The retired arrangement was an on-device MLX pack (qwen3-4b): ~2.4 GB resident and 2.28 GB on disk to run a model markedly weaker than the one the user is already paying for, behind a catalog, a downloader, a progress UI, a Configure Tug wizard step, and a second Swift inference backend that existed only to feed it. The subscription is a hard prerequisite of the whole app, so the remote path adds no dependency that was not already load-bearing — which is the argument that makes this a subtraction rather than a trade. **Cost accepted, explicitly:** these features stop working offline, degrading to the absent posture they were already designed to have.

**A SharedAgent is job-constrained by construction, not by convention.** `AgentSpec` names a model and a fixed `&'static [JobSpec]` table; `JobSpec` carries a job's instructions, its timeout, and its slow-mark. **No API anywhere accepts an arbitrary prompt** — `run(job_name, input)` looks the job up in that table, and a caller holding the pool cannot ask it for anything the table does not already name. That is what keeps a shared pool from becoming a general-purpose model endpoint inside the app, and it is why adding a second agent (the Sonnet one) is a second `AgentSpec` over the same machinery rather than new machinery. **Workers are persistent because a cold turn cannot meet the latency contract**: measured against CLI 2.1.222, a cold turn is 2327 ms — more than classify's entire 2 s budget — against 867–989 ms warm. Warmth is the feature; the pool exists to hold it — so **nobody ever waits on a cold start**. A job whose ceiling is shorter than one is never handed a worker that has not answered yet: its turn becomes that worker's warmup and the caller degrades on the spot. Lanes are otherwise warmed ahead of any caller (`ensure_warm`, called on traffic that precedes the job — a card asking for the login-PATH set warms the classify lane), and stay warm across a recycle because the replacement is warmed before the old worker stops answering. Workers recycle at `MAX_TURNS_PER_WORKER` (40) so a transcript cannot grow without bound, reap on idle per class (30 min for classify, whose ceiling cannot pay a respawn; 5 min for summarize, whose can), and respawn no faster than `RESPAWN_MIN_INTERVAL` (5 s) so a persistently failing spawn cannot become a fork bomb.

**Workers carry a job class, and classify never queues behind summarize.** The two jobs have contracts an order of magnitude apart (`CLASSIFY_TIMEOUT` 2 s against a user waiting on a keystroke; `SUMMARIZE_TIMEOUT` 6 s against a background cadence), so a single FIFO would let one in-flight summarize spend a classify's whole budget before it was even dispatched. `JobClass` splits the pool: a classify arriving during a slow summarize gets a same-class worker, never the summarize worker's queue. The rule is one `match` and it is the one thing in the pool a unit test caught wrong — `classify_with_grammar` fell into the summarize lane, which is precisely the collision the affinity exists to prevent, and which nothing but a test would have surfaced.

**A worker is isolated because it runs `claude`, and `claude` is an agent.** `--disallowedTools '*'`, `--strict-mcp-config`, `MAX_THINKING_TOKENS=0`, and a neutral Tug-owned cwd at `base_data_dir()/shared-agent`. The isolation is not precautionary: the unisolated posture *actively misbehaved* in the bringup spike — the worker invoked Bash on a tool-baiting prompt, and with the cwd left in a repo it read that repo's `CLAUDE.md` and appended a decoy token to its answer. A summarizer that inherits project instructions is a summarizer whose output is a function of whatever directory it happened to start in. The neutral cwd also parks worker transcripts under one encoded directory rather than scattering them through the user's projects.

**Every failure shape produces one degraded answer.** No agent configured, dead worker, timeout, malformed or refused verdict — all of it collapses to the same thing at the boundary: classify emits `ok:false, verdict:null` and the line goes to Claude with no routing chips; summarize emits no headline and arms backoff. The deck never learns *which* failure happened, because there is no action it could take that differs. **And under app-test the pool refuses to spawn at all** (`TUGAPP_APP_TEST=1` ⇒ `run` returns `Err` before any process starts), which makes the absent posture the *only* posture an app-test can observe — so the absent posture needs no tugbank seeding to force it, and no app-test can cover the positive path by construction. That limitation is stated where the coverage lives rather than papered over; the positive path is covered by an on-demand real-`claude` test and by fake-spawner unit suites. Model prose is never asserted anywhere, per the scribe convention.

**The timeout triad is a cross-file invariant, so each of the three names the other two.** `CLASSIFY_TIMEOUT` (`shared_agent.rs`), `CLASSIFY_REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` (`shell-classify-store.ts`), and `VERDICT_SUBMIT_WAIT_MS` (`tug-prompt-entry.tsx`) are all 2000 ms and have to move together — a submit that gives up before the transport does would route a line to Claude while the verdict was still in flight. `CLASSIFY_SLOW` is **1500 ms, not the 1 s the plan proposed**: at a measured ~900 ms warm, a 1 s mark would fire on roughly half of all calls, which does not raise an alarm so much as retire one.

**Readiness is a tenant switch and a transport check, deliberately not a probe.** `useSharedAgentReady` is true when the `dev.tugapp.shared-agent` switch is enabled (absent reads as enabled) and the connection lifecycle is `open`. It does **not** ask the pool whether a worker is alive, because a probe would cost a turn to answer a question whose wrong answer is already free: the fallback lattice makes an unavailable agent indistinguishable from a slow one at every call site, so the only thing a probe could buy is suppressing a request that already degrades correctly.

What this decision does **not** do: put conversational model work on the pool (sessions are the user's own `claude`, unchanged), permit any caller to compose a prompt, promise these features work offline, or claim the pool is a general inference service. `tugrust/crates/tugcast/src/shared_agent.rs` (the pool, `HAIKU_AGENT_JOBS`, `AgentWorkerSpawner`, the `SHARED_AGENT_DOMAIN` reads), `feeds/session_synopsis.rs`, `feeds/shell.rs` (`shell_classify`), `actions.rs` (`shared_agent_*` observability verbs); `tugdeck/src/lib/shell-classify-store.ts`, `lib/shared-agent-store.ts`, `components/tugways/tug-prompt-entry.tsx`, `configure-tug.tsx` (the wizard is four steps with the on-device-AI step gone). Originating plan: [L02], [L22], [L24], [L27]

**D137.** **A model borrowed for one turn is live-only: borrow and release never write the card's persisted selector.** A card's model has two different kinds of change, and the code had only one path for both. A user's pick is durable — `useModel`'s `setModel` sends `model_change` *and* writes `dev.model/<cardId>` through tugbank ([D07]), which is exactly right for "this card runs Sonnet from now on". A **loan** is not that. When a gesture wants a specific model for one turn — the plan review's Opus turn is the first, a `/audit` on Opus or a cheap-model summarization is the next — routing it through `setModel` would write the borrowed model into the card's remembered selector, and a crash mid-turn would make that lie durable: the card wakes up claiming to run a model nobody chose. So the borrow is a **composition of what already exists rather than a new mechanism** — persistence lives in the hook, not the store, and `CodeSessionStore.setModel` already sends the frame without persisting. `borrowModel` / `releaseModel` (`tugdeck/src/lib/use-model.ts`) are `sessionMetadataStore.applyModel` + `codeSessionStore.setModel` and **nothing else**, with the no-persistence property stated at the definition as a requirement and pinned by a test whose fake tugbank client and fake `fetch` both throw — so a later refactor cannot quietly route them back through `setModel`. **Not persisting is what makes crash recovery free**: the persisted selector stays correct throughout, so `use-model.ts`'s existing mount-restore realigns the live session on the next mount with no recovery code of our own and no new failure mode. Two consequences are load-bearing. **The chip reflects the loan** — the user sees Opus while Opus is running, because a settled control shows what the session actually holds. And **the borrow and the mount-restore must be mutually exclusive**: `applyModel` moves `snapshot.model`, a dependency of the restore effect, so every borrow re-runs it — and on the path where readiness was never reached (`models.length === 0 && model === null`) that effect early-returns *without* arming `sentRef`, making the borrow's own `applyModel` the thing that first makes `model` non-null, at which point the restore would revert the loan mid-turn and persist the seed over it. A module-level set of cards-with-a-borrowed-model is what the effect checks, and the effect's whole decision is factored out as a pure `evaluateMountRestore` so that interaction is assertable without rendering anything. **"Different model" is decided on the resolved catalog row, never on the selector strings** (`resolvesToSameModel`): a Max account on `default` may already be running Opus, and comparing `"default"` to `"opus"` would report a difference that does not exist and flicker the chip through two pointless control requests. The captured value for the release is the **selector**, not the resolved id — that is what `model_change` carries and what tugcode records. `tugdeck/src/lib/use-model.ts` (`borrowModel` / `releaseModel` / `currentModelSelector` / `resolvesToSameModel` / `evaluateMountRestore`), `lib/dash-review-controller.ts` (the first caller). The stored `dev.model/<cardId>` is byte-identical across a full borrow cycle. [Q01]. [D03], [D07], [L02], [L28]

**SUPERSEDED (2026-08-14): there is no borrow. A review runs on whatever model the user has selected.** The mechanism below was sound and its no-persistence property was correct; what was wrong was the premise — that the app should pick a model and schedule a turn on the user's behalf. Because the request could only ever be fired from *inside* the devise turn, it had to wait for that turn to settle, which meant a queue, which meant a heuristic for "the user got ahead of me", which meant a review that silently never ran while the transcript had already promised it would. A gesture that announces work it does not do is worse than one that does less. **The rule now is a fork in `arc-devise` and nothing else:** devised on the review model → review inline, in the same turn, immediately; devised on anything else → stop and print a clickable `/arc-review <path>` chip, and let the user choose the model *before* they click. `borrowModel` / `releaseModel` / `hasBorrowedModel` / `ModelBorrow`, the `cardsWithBorrowedModel` set, `evaluateMountRestore`'s `modelIsBorrowed` input, `dash-review-controller.ts`, `dash-review-request-store.ts`, the `plan_review_request` CONTROL broadcast, `POST /api/dash-review`, and `tugtool plan review-request` are all deleted. `resolvesToSameModel` and `currentModelSelector` survive — the mount-restore still uses them. What remains of the lane is `lib/arc-review.ts`: bare-form target resolution and the last-reviewed record. The **negative** is what now holds: the chip says Sonnet before, during, and after a review turn. The lesson generalizes past this feature — **never schedule a turn on the user's behalf**; hand them a gesture instead. [D07], [L28]

**Amended: the release waits out the turn it was borrowed for.** The original released at every terminal outcome including unmount, and fired the `model_change` immediately in all of them. That is right for a settled session and wrong for the one case unmount actually names: `CodeSessionStore.setModel` carries no `canSubmit` gate of its own — the gate lives in `useModel`, which the borrow deliberately bypasses — so an unmount mid-review put the frame on the wire mid-flight and rested on claude honoring it *there*, which nothing in this codebase establishes. If it does not, the live session keeps the borrowed model, and the free crash-recovery above does not cover it: `resolveSeedModel` returns `null` for a card with neither a per-card selector nor a deck-wide default, so `evaluateMountRestore` waits and no later mount corrects it. So a release that arrives mid-turn rides a **one-shot subscription that fires on the turn's settle and then unregisters itself** ([L27], and [L28] again — the release acts on the lifecycle by observing it rather than reaching into it). The subscription is deliberately **detached from the controller**, because it has to outlive it; the session store outlives the card in both shapes that reach here, since a session swap leaves the old store running its turn and a disposed store makes `subscribe` and `setModel` alike no-ops. The **borrow registration is held until the release actually fires**, not until `dispose` returns, so a remount's mount-restore still stands down for a loan that is genuinely still out instead of racing it. `lib/dash-review-controller.ts` (`releaseWhenSettled`). Unmount mid-turn defers, the deferred release fires exactly once across further beats, and unmount after settle releases at once.

**D129.** **Card navigation is one relationship with two axes — lateral and depth — and the Window menu's ad-hoc arrangement commands are retired.** The Window menu accreted five movement commands with four unrelated shapes: Previous/Next Card walked one pane's tabs and stopped at its edges, Cycle Panes (⌃`) hopped panes with no inverse, Cycle Stack rotated a slot one way under a ⌘R it had to share by preference, and Cascade/Tile rearranged geometry the imposer had long since superseded. D129 replaces all of it with a quartet that reads as one sentence: **Previous/Next Card move side to side, Previous/Next Card in Stack move front to back, and together they reach every open card.** The **lateral pair keeps ⇧⌘[ / ⇧⌘]** — untouchable macOS tab convention — and now walks the deck's whole *lateral ring*: every tab of every **visible** pane (the front of each slot and rail, plus the free panes), so at a pane's last tab the step crosses into the next pane instead of wrapping. **The sidebars are on the ring, every one of them** — they are cards the user reads and types in, standing in plain sight, and a lateral walk that skipped them would leave the card that is always on screen as the one the keyboard could not reach. The first cut excluded the Cards card by analogy to `move-to-slot`; that exclusion is a fact about *slots* — a sidebar takes none — and reading it as a general rule about sidebars is what left it off the ring while Jots rode it. A rail is a place exactly as a slot is ([D128]), so a rail's front is a ring position exactly as a slot's front is. That is what retires Cycle Panes rather than merely deleting it, and it restores the deselected-deck keyboard escape too (both directions re-activate the topmost card when nothing is selected). The ring's order is **structural, never measured** — left rail, slotted fronts by slot number, free panes by stored position, right rail (`lib/card-ring.ts`) — so the same function gates the menu item (`visibleCardCount` on the menu-state projection) and computes the step, and the gate cannot disagree with the gesture. The handler moved from the pane to the **deck canvas**, the one responder that can see every pane; `activateCard` both raises the target's pane and makes it the active tab, so one call serves the within-pane and cross-pane steps alike, wrapped in `transferFocusForActivation` as every activation is. The **depth pair takes ⌥⌘[ / ⌥⌘]** — the ⌥-variant of the lateral pair on the same keys, exactly the composition [D126]'s pairing rule generates: same motion, other axis. NEXT is [D124]'s raise-the-buried-longest ring, renamed; **PREVIOUS is its exact inverse, and needed the one new store mutation in the change** (`sendPaneBehind`): the front pane goes all the way to the back and the one beneath it fronts, because the tempting rule — raise the second-from-top — is [D124]'s ping-pong failure wearing the other label. This supersedes [D124]'s "no reverse direction" posture and dissolves its chord preference whole: **⌘R belongs to Reveal Stack outright**, stated as an ordinary registry binding, and the arbitration machinery — `stack-chord-store.ts`, the `stackChord` tugbank key, the Settings ▸ General choice, `applyStackChordPreference`, the payload's `stackChord` field — is deleted, not parked. Reveal Stack keeps its depth gate and its `disabledChord: "detach"`. **Cascade and Tile are removed altogether**, command and code (`arrangeCards`, the `arrange-cards` wire, both menu items): the Layouts system and the imposer are how Tug arranges the deck, period — which also resolves [chord-tiers.md](chord-tiers.md)'s ⌃⌥ anomaly by deletion rather than re-homing. ⌃` returns to the closed ⌃ set, leaving **⌥⇥ the only grandfathered exception**. `tugdeck/src/lib/card-ring.ts` (the ring), `deck-manager.ts` (`sendPaneBehind`; `arrangeCards` gone), `components/chrome/deck-canvas.tsx` (the lateral pair), `components/chrome/tug-pane.tsx` (the depth pair), `components/tugways/command-registry.ts` + `action-vocabulary.ts` (`previous-stack-card` / `next-stack-card`; `cycle-card` / `cycle-stack` / `arrange-cards` retired), `lib/host-menu-state.ts` (`visibleCardCount`; `stackChord` gone), `tugapp/Sources/AppDelegate.swift` (the quartet, the removals). Both directions of the depth ring are inverses; the lateral ring's gate crosses panes, and shrinks when a stack buries a card. [D121], [D123], [D124], [D126], [L02], [L11], [L30]

**D130.** **A card's width is a keyboard gesture on the Tug tier, and the digit row means "index into an ordered set" rather than "place on the deck."** Width was reachable by exactly one door — the title bar's width popup — which is a mouse trip for a property you retune constantly while reading. D130 adds two more doors onto the same act: **⌃⌘1 / ⌃⌘2 / ⌃⌘3** for Slim / Comfy / Wide, and a three-row check-marked group in the **View** menu, both landing on the pane the selection is in. The proposal that started it was ⌥⌘1–3, and **R1 is why it was rejected**: ⌥ is the variant operator, so ⌥⌘n has to read as a variant of ⌘n — a variant of Move Card to Slot *n* — and width is a different verb on a different property, so the composed set had no base to compose from. Landing on ⌃⌘ digits instead required amending [chord-tiers.md](chord-tiers.md)'s "digits are places," which was only ever true of the tier that had digits and, stated flatly, made the ⌃⌘ digit row underivable. The wider rule — **a digit indexes an ordered set; the tier says which set** — keeps the old reading inside it (⌘n *is* a place) and comes with the constraint that makes it a rule rather than a pool: the family must be an order **the user already sees**, which slots (left to right) and `CONTENT_WIDTH_PRESETS` (narrow to wide, the order every picker offers) both are. **Three commands, not one cycling command**, though ⌃⌘T Next Theme would have licensed a cycle: the set is static and small, the gesture is meant to be no-look, and a cycle you must know your place in is one you have to look at — the same argument that makes Next Card in Stack a ring ([D129]) rather than a swap. It also gives the menu three check-markable rows instead of a verb whose current value is invisible until you fire it. **Promotion to the menu is the half of the grant R6 asks for**, and it is safe here exactly where it was not for the slot family: ⌘1–9 stay chord-only because surfaces like the PDF viewer decline them by hand to leave the digits with the deck, and a menu item would take that choice from every surface after — while nothing in the app claims ⌃⌘ digits. **No new state, and no second opinion about width.** The chord's handler lives on the **deck canvas** (the one responder that can name which pane the selection is in, as with ⌘1–9) and dispatches the existing `set-card-width`, so the clamp to the stack's bounds and the `widthPreset` stamp are identical on all three doors; the enablement and the radio mark ride one new menu fact, `cardWidth`, gated on the same two conditions the title bar hides its popup behind — a pane is selected, and it is not a rail. A pane dragged to a width of its own has no preset stamped, so **no row is checked** rather than the nearest one — the settled control shows what the geometry holds. The Swift items carry **empty key equivalents** so `applyCommandChords` writes the chords from the table and all three stay rebindable, the discipline the sidebar toggles follow and the shade toggles do not. `components/tugways/command-registry.ts` + `action-vocabulary.ts` (`set-pane-width`, the three parameterized entries, the `cardWidth` fact), `components/chrome/deck-canvas.tsx` (the handler), `action-dispatch.ts` (the wire bridge), `lib/host-menu-state.ts` (the projection), `tugapp/Sources/AppDelegate.swift` (the View group). [D126], [D128], [D129], [L11], [L30]

**D131.** **Bullseye is a temporary *presentation* layered over whichever geometry mode a pane already holds — never a fourth stored mode.** The deck could place a card four ways and size it four ways, and still had no gesture for the thing a writing app calls a focus mode: *put the thing I am working on in front of me, at a comfortable column, and push everything else back — for now.* **⌃⌘B** and **View ▸ Bullseye** are that gesture. The name is deliberately not "focus mode": *focus* already names the responder-chain concept in this codebase ([focus-language.md](focus-language.md), `transferFocusForActivation`, `data-focused`), and a second meaning for it would be a permanent tax on every reader. **The obvious implementation is a trap, and avoiding it is most of the decision.** "Remember the geometry, call `setPaneWidth` and `centerPane`, restore on exit" routes through `movePane`, which calls `scheduleSave()` unconditionally *and* rewrites the `widthPreset` stamp on any width change — so within one save debounce the temporary posture is in the persisted v4 blob and the user's stamp (or their hand-dragged width, which is recorded by the *absence* of a stamp) is gone, and a crash or quit mid-bullseye strands them in a stance they never asked to keep. That is a silent, durable [L23] violation. So **bullseye writes no geometry at all**, and exit therefore restores nothing: there is nothing to restore because nothing was disturbed, which is strictly stronger than restoring four fields correctly across every interleaving a window resize or an allocator retune could produce. The one state change is `bullseyePaneId` on `DeckState` — in the state because `DeckState` *is* the `useSyncExternalStore` snapshot ([L02]) and the input to `arrangementSignature`, and deliberately absent from `serialize()` for the reason above, following the precedent `hasFocus` sets on the same interface. **The frame is the imposer's existing one-up placement at comfy** — `imposeStyle({ slot: 0, count: 1 }, comfyWidth, pinnedFrame)` — because `travelFraction` already returns `0.5` for `count < 2`: "centred in the band, slack split evenly" *is* what one-up means, so writing centring math would be writing a second copy of it, and the width is `comfy` rather than a fourth number ([D130]'s "no second opinion about width"). It is the **first** branch of `TugPane`'s geometry ternary: precedence over imposed is the feature (a slotted card bullseyes to the centre and returns to its slot), precedence over pinned is defensive only, since a rail can never hold the posture. Entry and exit **cross rather than cut** for free, because every horizontal pin is a `left` calc ([D121]) and the FLIP settle interpolates width as well as position. **Every other content pane leaves the canvas; the rails stay standing and recede.** A card that is merely dimmed is still a card you can read, and the first cut of this decision — recede everything, move nothing — was wrong for exactly that reason: it produced a deck where nothing had got out of the way. So each other content pane slides off the canvas and back on exit, and **which side it leaves by is read against the bullseyed card, not against the canvas**: panes left of it go left, panes right of it go right. Sorting around the canvas centre was tried first and it let cards cross the bullseyed one — bullseye the leftmost card of a three-up and the middle card, still left of the canvas centre, slid left THROUGH the card arriving there. Sorting around the bullseyed card's own former place makes a crossing impossible by construction, because every pane keeps the side it was already on and relative order is preserved throughout the move. The reference is the bullseyed pane's PRE-bullseye centre, which is available for free: bullseye writes nothing, so the pane still carries the slot and position it will return to. The cost is that travel is no longer a short hop to the nearest edge — a pane just right of the bullseyed one but near the left of the deck crosses the whole canvas to leave rightward. It writes no geometry either: the displacement is one `left` override at render, so the pane returns to the pixel. **The edge is read from the pane's RESOLVED pin, never from its stored `position.x`** — for an imposed pane the stored position is a last-known value the imposer superseded, and a three-up deck can hold all three panes at nearly the same stored x while they sit left, centre and right on screen, so deciding from it sent every card out the same side. The comparison itself is then made in CSS rather than in JS: both terms are length expressions over the live insets, and resolving them at render would mean measuring, which is the observation [L06] keeps out of the geometry path; instead a clamp saturates the signed difference between the two centres into one bound or the other, and re-resolves on a window resize for free. **Both bounds park the pane just past the canvas edge rather than a whole canvas beyond it**, and that is about the motion rather than the resting place: the frames animate out on the existing FLIP settle, and a left bound of `-100%` — a full canvas-width out — made a left-leaving pane travel some 1400px further than a right-leaving one over the same 300ms. The settle's spring is critically damped, so it spends most of its distance early; over that much travel the card was gone inside ~50ms and read as having simply vanished, while the return, which decelerates *into* view, looked correct. Same tween, opposite direction, only one of them legible — and the failure is invisible to every resting-rect assertion. The motion is the meaning here: a card that disappears reads as closed, a card you watch slide off the edge reads as moved aside. **Rails are excluded and keep their pins** ([D128]): a rail that left would take the band's insets with it and the bullseyed card would jump the moment the posture began — and collapsing one would run the space allocator on both edges of a gesture meant to be instant and repeatable. They take the deep recede in place instead. **The recede is the deck's existing inactive-content dim, deepened** — the two `.tug-pane-chrome::before` / `::after` layers at deeper token values under a `data-bullseye` hook — and emphatically **not** a scrim: `.tug-pane-scrim` is the *pane-modal* layer and blocks pointer events, while bullseye must stay non-modal, because clicking another pane is one of its exit doors and a blocked click is a dead gesture instead of an exit. Nothing may put `filter`, `transform`, `backdrop-filter`, `contain`, or `will-change` on `.tug-pane` for this: any of them makes every receded frame a containing block for its `position: fixed` descendants, and `TugSheet` portals into the frame while completion popups and banners position from viewport coordinates. **The exits come in two shapes, and both are stated over the mechanism rather than over the caller.** The geometry-shaped exits say *any path that writes a pane's `position`, `size`, or `slot` releases that pane* — one helper, `_clearBullseyeFor`, called from the three paths that do (`movePane`, gated on the `positionChanged || sizeChanged` locals it already computes and **not** on `evictSlot`, which the width doors never pass; `setContentWidth` and `assignCardToSlot`, which bypass `movePane`). Stating it over the mutation is what makes the audit question checkable — *does this path write geometry?* — where a rule written over call sites goes stale the next time someone adds a mutator; it is the same rule the imposer already lives by, that a manual gesture releases a derived pane ([D121]). The focus-shaped exits are **one clear from `_flipFirstResponder`**, the single entry point for first-responder transitions, so the click, the ⌘R picker, both rings, every sidebar chord, and the canvas-background deselect are covered at no per-path cost — and so is every focus path added later, which is the property an event handler per door cannot offer. The read accessor derives on top of that (`bullseyePaneIdOf`: the id holds only while its pane exists *and* hosts the first responder), which makes a stale id unreadable in the window before a flip commits. **Deriving alone was tried first and is not sufficient**, and the correction is recorded rather than quietly applied: a hidden-but-present id starts matching again the moment focus returns to its pane, so clicking away and back resurrected a posture the user never re-asked for. Bullseye is meant to be left, not parked. The posture does survive a **tab switch inside** the bullseyed pane, because the question asked is whether the *pane* hosts the responder — the user is still working in the card they bullseyed. **⌃⌘B is derived, not chosen** ([chord-tiers.md](chord-tiers.md)): a card's posture on the deck is Tug's own layout machinery, so it takes the Tug tier beside ⌃⌘L, ⌃⌘T and the ⌃⌘1/2/3 width row; plain ⌘ is out under R3 (a deliberate posture change is not a many-times-an-hour verb) and the composed sets are out under R1 (there is no ⌘B base for it to be a variant of — ⌘B is held in reserve for bold). Promotion to the View menu is R6's half of the grant and preempts every scoped binding, which is intended: a deck-level posture is not a surface's to decline. One action, not [D130]'s selection-relative / pane-addressed pair, because bullseye's two doors mean the same pane; if a title-bar control is ever added it should introduce a pane-addressed `set-bullseye` and have `toggle-bullseye` dispatch to it. `layout-tree.ts` (the field, invariant 8), `deck-store-selectors.ts` (`bullseyePaneIdOf`), `deck-manager.ts` (`toggleBullseye`, `_clearBullseyeFor`, `_clearBullseyeOnFocusFlip`), `serialization.ts` (the documented omission), `components/chrome/tug-pane.tsx` (the branch, `derivedRef`, `data-bullseye`), `components/chrome/deck-canvas.tsx` (the handler, the signature term, the container hook), `components/tugways/command-registry.ts` + `action-vocabulary.ts` (`toggle-bullseye`, the `bullseye` fact), `lib/host-menu-state.ts` (the projection), `components/tugways/tug-pane.css` + the six themes (the deepened dim — and it has to be deep: the inactive dim it builds on moves the card surface about 4%, so a timid multiple of it is indistinguishable from nothing, which is how the first cut shipped a recede nobody could see), `tugapp/Sources/AppDelegate.swift` (`view.bullseye`). [D121], [D128], [D130], [L02], [L06], [L09], [L23], [L24], [L30]

**Amended: a rail bullseyes too, and its place on the edge is reserved while it does.** The original refused a sidebar Pane — in `toggleBullseye`, in the canvas handler, in the menu gate, and as invariant 8 — on the reading that a rail is the imposition's fixed end rather than a reading surface. That was true of the *arrangement* and false of the *user*: Cards, Jots, and the Overview are cards you sit and work in, and the rail is exactly the place where a card is too narrow to work in. What made the refusal look necessary was the fear of a rail that collapses — take the rail away and the band's insets change, the chain re-imposes wider, and the bullseyed card jumps the moment the posture begins. **But bullseye writes no geometry, so nothing takes the rail away.** The Pane record keeps its width and its side, `sidebarRailsOf` still finds it, the `--tug-imposer-inset-*` values it feeds are the same ones they were, and the rail's slot on the edge stands open and empty for the duration. The reserved place is the whole point: a bullseyed rail must never be re-imposed as if it had been *hidden*, because then exit would be a re-open rather than a return, and the deck would rearrange twice around a gesture the user meant as a look. Which is also why the existing "rails do not leave the canvas" rule needs no exception when the bullseyed Pane is itself a rail — the others keep their pins, and the line the content Panes sort around is the bullseyed rail's own **pinned** centre (written out as `gap + width/2` from its edge, with the live rail-width property as the term) rather than `imposeSidebarStyle`'s `left`, whose calc reads a `--tugx-rail-side` property each rail frame declares on *itself* and which resolves to nothing when read from any other Pane. Two consequences fall out. A bullseyed rail **drops its resize handle**, since that handle drags the rail's width measured from the deck edge and the frame is not standing there — a grip nowhere near what it would move. And the deferred title-bar control is now shipped, in the shape this decision specified for it: a `lucide` **`Target`** button immediately behind the stack badge on every Pane, dispatching a pane-addressed **`set-bullseye`** `{ paneId }`, with ⌃⌘B and View ▸ Bullseye resolving "the Pane I am in" and handing off to the same action. Two actions rather than one is now correct for the reason the original gave for one being enough, read the other way: a button can name a Pane the selection is not in, and a chord cannot. The button carries `data-on` / `aria-pressed` from the derived id, so it says what the geometry says.

**D187.** **The Observer is the session's narrator of record, and one Sonnet ask per wake writes both the Overview post and the session's standing sentence.** It is the only model cost in the narration chain — the tap, the digester and the beat under the line are deterministic and free ([D186]) — and it runs on the Overview pool of the arrangement [D127] settled: three workers, `sonnet` by alias rather than a pinned id, because this is prose work with a human reading the output. **It wakes five ways**, and the reason rides the job input because the model uses it well — it is a fact about the moment, not a hint about the answer. `turn-end` is a flush and the "ready for you to look in" signal. `sitrep-timer` is the dominant wake, and `DEFAULT_SITREP_SECS` is **60**: it was 90, chosen by reading three offline replays of one real session side by side, and it came down because the post is no longer only the channel's — it is the standing sentence and the masthead's account run too, and a line a reader watches should not be slower than the line it stands in for. (Amended 2026-09-13: the run UNDER the standing sentence, not the line over the beat — see [D185].) `session-end` produces a wrap-up. `token-threshold` is **off by default** (`DEFAULT_TOKEN_WAKE_TOKENS` is 0), because the timer and turn ends already cover the ground and a threshold that fires mid-thought is noise. **Amended 2026-09-14: `submission` is the fifth wake, and the envelope carries a third sentence.** A user's new ask re-arms that session's window short — `DEFAULT_SUBMISSION_ARM_SECS` is **6**, a `dev.tugapp.overview` knob beside the sitrep, and it rides the loop's one existing deadline rather than a timer of its own — so the line under a working session's name can answer the new ask seconds in rather than a sitrep later. A submission the digester does not read as an ask arms nothing: a slash command is a local act and not work to narrate, and `counts_as_ask` is the gate. After the short wake the window is the sitrep's again, so the arm buys one wake per ask rather than a faster cadence for the whole turn. **What it costs, measured rather than argued:** `overview-replay --submission-arm-secs` segments one recorded transcript both ways and prints the pair, and a 37-hour session of 1145 frames went from 117 wakes to 138 — 21 more, roughly one wake in six. The third field is `current`, the per-turn sentence: what this session is on in THIS turn, held to the same register and the same 72-character clip as the standing sentence, broadcast on `OVERVIEW` as a tagged `session_current` value rather than as a post, never persisted, and read by the deck as a fact about the turn it was written in ([D132]). A submission wake writes that field and nothing else — null post, null standing sentence — and `synopsis`'s through-line rubric is unchanged by any of it, which is what three independent fields are for. **What a post costs:** `OBSERVER_POST_TIMEOUT` is 120 s with a slow mark at 60 s — a quality bound rather than a latency budget, because nothing user-blocking waits on a post; the Operator's two jobs share the pool's one lane, which is why there are three workers rather than two. **`{"post": null}` is an answer, not a failure.** It is the model reading the work and judging it not worth telling: nothing is written, and nothing goes on the wire. A quiet channel is editorial judgement being exercised, and is meant to be reachable. **The post and the sentences are independent fields of one envelope.** `synopsis` is optional, and a missing, null, empty or whitespace field leaves the standing sentence exactly as it stood — a sentence that blanks on a bad turn is worse than one a minute out of date. Either field may arrive without the other. **Amended 2026-09-13: the Observer is shown the sentence it is revising.** Each wake's input carries `STANDING SENTENCE NOW:` with the sentence currently under the session's name, and a null `synopsis` is the instruction to leave it standing; the rubric asks for the through-line of the session's whole run rather than the newest ask, so the sentence moves when the subject moves and holds while the work under it does — on a thirteen-wake replay it held on six and moved on seven, each move following a change of subject. A session whose ledger row cannot be resolved is **skipped entirely** rather than written under a guessed key, for the reason [D132] records. **Refs validate against what the model saw**, never against the repository: `validate_refs` takes the wake's corpora as a **slice, never one concatenated string** — the rendered digest window and the settled-facts section — so a sha or a path the window never carried is dropped rather than linked, and search and narration read the same stored rendering of a fact. Dedup is the same shape: each wake shows the Observer `DEFAULT_LAST_K_POSTS` of its own recent posts about this session and it declines to repeat itself. Nothing compares text. **One switch, `dev.tugapp.overview/enabled`** (a bool; absent reads enabled), gates the wakes and nothing else, because the wakes are the only model cost. With it off the masthead's account run falls through to the turn's ask and then to the rest sentence, the standing sentence holds as it stood, the beat keeps running in the history the account run opens, and no model work happens anywhere in the chain. It replaces the retired `dev.tugapp.pulse/enabled` and the `dev.tugapp.shared-agent/synopsis` tenant, each of which gated something that no longer exists. **One-way isolation is structural:** no code path leads from here into any work session's stdin, and the Observer's own posts travel on `OVERVIEW`, a feed the tap does not read — so the narrator cannot narrate itself. `overview_replay` drives this same code against recorded transcripts, which is where a cadence or a window change is measured rather than guessed. `tugrust/crates/tugcast/src/feeds/observer.rs`, `feeds/observer_wake.rs`, `feeds/overview_agent.rs`, `feeds/overview_replay.rs`, `feeds/refs.rs`; `tugdeck/src/lib/overview-store.ts`. [D103], [D127], [D132], [D185], [D186], [L02]

---

## Session Identity

**D132.** **A session has one identity, produced by one resolver and rendered by one component family at a declared density tier — led by a live pulsing dot, titled `<name> : <callsign>`, and said in two lines beneath.** Tug named a session seven ways across fifteen surfaces, under five near-parallel precedence rules no two of which agreed: `sessionChipDisplay`, `sessionRowTitle`, `sessionEntryTitle` (a hash-equality *sniff*, which is what a rule looks like once nobody remembers what it was for), and two more in Rust. The 8-char short id was computed independently in three places and stored in none. D132 is [D123] — *a pane's name is one string produced in one place* — applied to the session, and the five rules are deleted rather than reconciled. **`resolveSessionIdentity(sessionId) → SessionIdentity`** is the one producer; **`TugSessionIdentity`** is the one renderer family — `chip` and `line` are its two tier values, and the row and masthead densities are compositions of them (`TugSessionRow`, `SessionMasthead`) rather than further tiers on the component; **no surface composes a `project/tag` string of its own**, which is a grep gate rather than a convention because that is the only form the discipline survives in.

**Two registers, not two formats.** A surface that *is* the session — the masthead, a picker row, a Cards row — renders the identity as typography: no enclosure. A surface that *refers* to a session from foreign context — a Overview ref, a History commit line, the Changes card's orphan hint — wraps the same identity in the **session atom**, a rounded pill. An enclosed chip reads as a link to the thing elsewhere; on a surface that *is* the thing, that reading is a lie. The tier implies the register, so a caller picks a density and never a format.

**The mark is a live pulsing dot, and it is the same mark on both registers.** It says what the session is *doing* rather than what kind of thing it is, which is the one thing a static glyph could never do. `useSessionPhase(sessionId)` is the session-keyed door onto it and returns the flattened phase **key** rather than the snapshot, which is what makes it affordable on a Changes card carrying one citation per row: React bails out unless the *reading* changed. **Unknown liveness reads `idle`, never danger** — no bound card, services not yet constructed, a closed or external row — because red is the error channel and spending it on "we do not know" makes every ordinary closed session look broken. A card that exists whose transport is genuinely offline still reads danger; that is a real failure rather than doubt. Retired with the dot, *do not re-propose*: the **chatbox icon** (`MessageSquareText`/`MessageSquareOff`), which spent a second mark on a job the dot does better, and the **`OFFLINE_PHASE_INPUT` fallback** that painted doubt as danger.

**The title is `<name> : <callsign>` in TWO runs, and which run gives way depends on the register.** A user-supplied name is the user explicitly saying what the session is called; it cannot rank below a callsign Tug minted for itself. The callsign stays visible beside it because it is the permanent citable handle a rename never changes. Under a width squeeze the two registers truncate **differently, by design**: on a *title* surface (the line tier — the masthead, the Cards rows, the picker rows) the callsign is the handle a reader scans a list by, so it survives and the user's name elides; on the *atom* (the chip tier) the user's own words survive and the minted handle elides — the tooltip and every copy path still carry it whole either way. An unnamed session renders the bare callsign, which may then elide because it is the only run there is. `sessionTitleParts` produces the pair; two runs rather than one joined string is what lets them be sized separately, and a filter highlight is painted **inside each run** — joining first would put the ` : ` separator inside a mark and defeat the truncation rule. **No `project/` prefix in title ink.** That prefix is `sessionIdentityLine`'s, and that string is a different channel: the pane-title channel the tab strip, the Window menu, and the slot-stack picker read, which is not a notification path and is documented as **constant for the life of a binding** — changing it would make the tab strip stale on rename. It survives unchanged, and so does the prefix, in the tooltip, the citation, and the telemetry popover. Retired: **`<project>/<callsign>` as one text node**, which was right while the two halves were a path and wrong the moment one of them became the user's own words. **Monospace still belongs to flat text only** — never to session chrome.

**The callsign is immutable and the UUID never leads.** A legacy tagless row degrades to its 8-char short id and **never** to the full UUID. Permanence is not aspirational: `sessions` rows are hard-`DELETE`d by trash, three cascade paths, and cap/age eviction, so a unique index over that table frees a tag when its row dies — and a recycled tag makes a two-year-old commit's citation resolve to a *different* session, which is a confidently wrong answer and strictly worse than an unresolvable one. So an **append-only `minted_tags` table is the all-time arbiter**: a callsign is claimed exactly where a line is born — `birth_line` for a card's first spawn or a plain `/new`, `ensure_scan_line` for a session the external scanner found — in the same transaction as the line it names, its `PRIMARY KEY` violation is the collision signal a mint retries against, and **nothing anywhere may delete from it** (`no DELETE FROM minted_tags` is a grep gate). `sessions_tag` survives as the live-row invariant only. On a genuine collision the server **rerolls a complete fresh `adjective-noun`** — the bare `-N` suffix backstop is retired, along with the silent NULL tag it landed on at exhaustion. *Do not re-propose either.* The guarantee is honestly **per-ledger** (`sessions.db` is per-instance): a trailer written on one machine and read against another's ledger simply misses, which renders as the slashed atom and is safe.

**The callsign belongs to the line, not to a session id.** A `sessions` row is a **segment** of a line of work and carries no identity of its own, so an id change — a stage rotation, a rewind-fork, a crash respawn — writes another segment against the same line and the callsign does not move, because there is nothing to move it from. Provenance lives in the `forked_from_session_id`/`fork_point` columns, never in the spelling ([D167]).

**The citation is the only sanctioned flat-text form**, and the only place monospace appears: `<tag> (<shortid8>)`, project-prefixed when the context does not supply the project, degrading to the bare short id for a legacy tagless row (a doubled hash is noise). Every Tug commit carries **two** trailers — `Tug-Session:` (the citation, for a human) and `Tug-Session-Id:` (the full uuid, for a machine, never displayed) — which tugcast parses server-side into typed `GitLogCommit` fields exactly as it already did for `Tug-Arc`, **and strips from the displayed body**, because `%b` retains trailer lines and that is why the History card used to show raw trailer ink. Reading a citation is two steps with a wire between them: `resolveCitedSession` is **pure grammar** over the trailer text — machine field first, then the parenthesized token (36 chars = a legacy one-line trailer, which therefore still resolves exactly; 8 hex = a short id) — and turning that id into a session is the **ledger's** job, over the `resolve_sessions` verb. Legacy commits live in history forever and had to keep working; they do. Transcript exports become `tug-session-<callsign>-<shortid>` — a file named after a hash is a file nobody finds again.

**Resolvability is a ledger answer, and a short id is expanded server-side.** *Do not re-propose* deciding either from the client's caches. The tag store and the binding store hold only the sessions some listing, push, or card binding happened to mention this run, so a chip judged against them is resolvable or slashed depending on whether the picker had been opened — which is not a fact about the reference. `resolve_sessions { ids }` answers `{ sessions: [{ queried, session }], unknown }`: keyed by the spelling asked (a short id and its row's full id are different strings), and **naming its misses**, which is what lets `session-citation-store.ts` cache a negative instead of re-asking on every repaint. Expansion belongs there for a second reason — only the ledger can see that two sessions share eight hex characters, and an ambiguous prefix therefore **resolves to nothing** rather than to the first row found, the same "wrong-but-resolvable is worse than unresolvable" rule the callsign's permanence rests on. A resolved row also seeds the name / callsign / description stores, which is the fourth such seed path beside the spawn ack, `list_sessions_ok`, and the card-binding rows, and it is how a citation names a session no card is bound to. The resolver answers from `sessions` first and falls back to `external_scan_cache`: cap and age eviction hard-delete `sessions` rows while the transcript stays on disk and the picker keeps listing it, and a citation must not go dark on a session the picker can still resume — the fallback row is synthesized exactly as the picker union synthesizes an external row.

**Liveness is not identity.** The record carries no phase field, and that survives the dot becoming the mark. Phase lives on a per-**card** `codeSessionStore` whose snapshot is the whole `CodeSessionSnapshot`, and an identity hook that subscribed to it would wake the chip, the line, the row, and the masthead on every transcript event; the dot is isolated in a leaf subscription so a reducer wake repaints the dot alone. `useSessionPhase` is the join — session → card → services → snapshot → key — and it lives beside the resolver rather than inside it precisely so the two stay separable. Identity and liveness are two subscriptions with two keys, and **the component is where they meet**: the dot arrives as a child, never as a field.

**The React entry point is the hook, and that is part of the spec rather than an implementation note.** `resolveSessionIdentity` reads three stores imperatively; called from a render body it hands back a snapshot with **no subscription**, so a `/rename` updates the stores while the surface repaints never. That failure passes every unit test and appears only as a stale name in the running app, so `useSessionIdentity` — `useSyncExternalStore` over each backing store **by id**, never by whole-store version token, which would wake every identity surface in the app on any session's rename — is the only door a component may use ([L02]). *No component calls the bare resolver*: grep gate.

**The Session card's chrome is a fixed 72px masthead, and the pane owns the slot while the session owns what is in it.** Three lines — the dot-led title with the pane controls, the description, the activity with its tape — plus a trailing telemetry **popover** carrying branch, stamps, the rendered atom, and the citation on a `TugCopyBadge`.

**One row shape, three mounts.** The masthead, the Cards rows, and the new-session picker rows show the same thing, so `TugSessionRow` authors the shape once and all three compose it — the component library is the consistency mechanism rather than a convention three files are asked to remember. The masthead is seated in chrome through the knobs the row's own primitive publishes (block and inline padding, the indicator gutter, the flush hover, the stack's block pad) rather than by reaching into its rules, and it raises the sub-line indent from the rail's to the dot's full advance because a card-wide tier can afford to hang its two lines off the *title* where a rail cannot. Retired with the shape, *do not re-propose*: the **three-level stack** (a standing goal beside the description read as a second goal-shaped line — the intent survives in the beat-history popover, where the Observer's post heads its run of beats), the **four-line row** (its metadata facts are the activity line's rest form now), and the four **audition fits** `gutter`/`reveal`/`wash`/`duplex` with their comparison meter — only `inset` ships. **The stack's type is stated once, on the row, and no mount restates it**: title, description, and activity are the ambient sans at the row size, one size down, one size down again, and the step off the title is small enough that the pair beneath still reads as one group. The activity line therefore **drops the beat component's condensed face** on this stack — condensed was right for a commentary bar of its own, where width is scarce, and is wrong for the third line of a paragraph whose other two lines are sans. Three mounts that each named their own sizes is exactly how the three surfaces came to differ from one another and from the gallery. Composing a list row into chrome also means **turning off the row's rail reflexes, the pointer poses included**: the flush hover wash and the `:active` press layer both answer a click that acts on a row, and a title bar's click activates or drags the *card* — left on, the press painted the entire chrome tier accent-blue on every press, the ones that begin a drag included. A pose that lives on a pseudo-element is invisible to a probe that reads `getComputedStyle(el)`, and `:active` cannot be reached by a synthetic event at all, so the assertion that guards this holds a real button down and reads the row's `::after`.

**The activity line says something true at rest.** `<turns> turns, <size>. Last updated: <stamp>. Ready.` — the turns segment **always prints** (a fresh session's line is `No turns, 8 KB. Ready.`; "no conversation yet" is a fact, not an absence, and the zero is spelled as prose rather than as a digit, which reads as a measurement that came back empty), an unknown size drops its segment, the labeled stamp appears only for a session with turns to have been updated by, and **`Ready.` always closes the line** — the session is on disk and one gesture from another turn wherever the row is read. `Last updated:` is labeled because a bare date-time beside a size and a count is ambiguous about which of the three it dates, which is also why the relative-age form (`3h ago`) went with the metadata line it opened. During a turn the live beat replaces the sentence — on every mount of the row, the picker's included; the picker's state lines survive only for rows this app cannot see into (a terminal's) and for failed rows. **The bare turn-end marker `Done` is not a beat and is never shown** (`sessionActivityBeat` filters it at every mount): it is the *absence* of one, it says a run ended and nothing about when, it sits there unchanged for however long the session then stays quiet, and it is precisely the state the rest sentence describes with the facts in it. The marker itself is untouched on the wire — the digest deque, the beat-history popover, and a copied line all still carry it; only this reading changes. That grammar is what makes [P10]'s server work load-bearing: both facts are scan-derived, so tugcast re-derives them **at turn end** from the same incremental entry points the picker scan uses, and `build_session_updated_frame` reads the `(file_size, turn_count)` pair from the scan cache on **every** push — because the client replaces its cached row wholesale, so a push that omitted a fact would blank a known size or knock a scan-derived count to a sparse `0`.

**`PULSE` was an internal name and never user-facing ink; it is now not a name at all.** The rule was that stores, modules, feeds, class names and constants kept the word while labels, legends and stand-ins lost it — because with the activity line always carrying something true to say nothing needs a placeholder word, and a band that names itself is furniture where a sentence belongs. [D186] finished the job on the internal half: the subsystem is the **digest**, its newest line is the **beat**, and the word survives in the codebase only as ordinary English for a thing that throbs. The ink half of the rule is unchanged and still guarded — `at0280-shared-agent-absent.test.ts` asserts that with the model work absent the masthead and the Cards row carry no stand-in word at all — and it reads now as the general rule it always was: a surface says what is happening, or says a true rest fact, and never says the name of the machinery that would have spoken for it.

**The controls cluster is the same on every pane, masthead-bearing ones included.** The width control was suppressed on a masthead for a while, on the reasoning that the first row is identity plus its own telemetry wave and width competes with the name for it. That was reverted (`1693707dc`): the Session card is the pane whose width is retuned most often, so the one surface that most needed the control was the one that did not have it, and a control that appears on some panes and not others is a cluster the eye cannot learn. It renders on every pane that *has* a width — rails excepted, and not because they bear a different tier but because a rail takes its width from the allocator and has no preset to set. The **slot-stack badge**, the **`…` section menu**, and the **close X** are likewise unconditional: the badge describes the *slot* rather than the card, and a Session card standing in a stack is entitled to say so — it is the only way into the panes behind it. Assertions about the cluster name the control they mean by testid, never an equality check on the cluster's list, which is legitimately longer or shorter depending on whether the card stands in a stack.

**The masthead generalizes past the Session card, and a rail gets a tier of its own.** Three tiers ship: a 36px tinted bar for a **utility** card, a 72px masthead for a **document** card — Text, File viewer, Diff, and the Session card, whose masthead is the one described here — and a 32px flush bar with racing stripes for a **rail**. A document masthead is the same object as the session's, mounting the same `TugSessionRow` on the same `masthead-frame.css` tier, differing only in what its three lines say: a name, where the document lives, and one quieter fact. **The description ladder generalizes with it**: a document card that can name no place publishes a *stand-in* rather than nothing — the tier is a fixed three lines, so an empty second line is a hole between two filled ones, not a shorter masthead — and a stand-in paints a step quieter, exactly as the session's own last rung does, plus **italic** on the document tier, where the line's real content is a path and the absence of one deserves to differ in type rather than only in gray. (Italic is safe *here* because IBM Plex Sans Italic is bundled at 400, the weight this line inherits; a `font-style: italic` landing on a weight with no bundled face paints nothing at all in the app's WKWebView, which is why the guard for it is a pixel count and not a computed-style read.) The wording rule that follows is that a line never claims an act that did not happen: an unwritten buffer's third line reads **Draft**, not "Saved". Rail-ness is read from the card registry's `layoutRole: "sidebar"` — what the card *is*, not `sidebarSide`, which says only where it currently stands, and would flip a released rail out of its livery mid-gesture. Both tiers stamp their role on the pane element as well as the bar, because the height must be declared where the scrim, the sheet clip, and the banner can see it and `:has()` will not carry it up. Racing stripes are settled — flush ground, three 1px stripes on a 3px pitch, neutral ink only, focus signalled by brightening and never by the accent color; *do not re-propose*. `pane-model.md` § "Chrome has three heights" is the doctrine; the frame a pinned rail stands in is [D181].

(The telemetry popover shipped as a `TugPopover`, not a `TugPlacard`: a hover-dismissed surface puts its own copy button out of the pointer's reach, and `TugPlacard` renders in place, which a clipping 72px chrome tier cannot host.) The height is **ratified and fixed, never content-driven**; the masthead never reflows and overflow truncates. Chrome remains the Pane's ([L09]), so `TugPane` opens the taller slot when its **active** card publishes a masthead sidecar and mounts a tugways `SessionMasthead` into it — which is what keeps the digest feeder, the dwell queue, the compaction pin and the telemetry popover out of chrome code. In a multi-tab pane the chrome follows the **frontmost** tab, swapping 36↔72 on tab switch: a pinned 72px chrome over a Text tab would caption one card with another card's identity. The height travels as a pane-scoped `--tugx-pane-chrome-height` so the four chrome-offset consumers (the title bar, the pane scrim, the sheet clip's top, the pane banner) follow it while the four that are really measuring *a tab row* or *a square button* stay on `--tug-chrome-height` — the masthead and the tab bar **stack** (72 + 36), they do not merge.
