Once the masthead carries the voice the Z2 commentary strip comes out entirely, with the dwell queue moved *as code* rather than rewritten; its focus stop is retired rather than re-homed, since the masthead is chrome and takes no card-cycle stop.

**The description is a rolling synopsis, and the incipit is retired as its source.** A first prompt describes where a session *started*; a description has to be current, which is the whole complaint. The standing line is composed from the session's own evidence — the newest ask as the subject, the arc of earlier asks behind it, the opening for context, and the newest activity lines as explicitly-labelled background — and persists to a `synopsis` column on the session's ledger row. The register is a summary's rather than a headline's: articles and conjunctions are allowed, and the line names an undertaking and its object. It shipped as a job of its own on the SharedAgent's Haiku sentence lane, paced at once a minute, composing from a session-lifetime digest it collected itself; [D187] retired that job and the module behind it, and the sentence comes back now as the second field of the Observer's own envelope, written from the same digest window the post is written from. One ask, two answers — which is what stops the session's name and the session's narration from describing the same minute differently.

**The name and the description are two fields on two lines, and the rename freeze is gone.** `SessionIdentity` carries `customName` (the user's `/rename`) and `description` (the agent's synopsis) as independent fields — the merged `title` is **removed**, not deprecated, so the compiler enumerated every reader. The freeze existed because they competed for one line of chrome and a generated line must not speak over the user's word; they occupy different lines now, so both enforcement points are gone — the `AND name_user_set = 0` clause in `record_synopsis` and the pre-model early return in the synopsis feed. Leaving it would have given every renamed session a permanently dead description line on its most-visible surface. *Do not restore it as a "fix".* The description's own precedence is its rungs, and **amended 2026-09-14** the top two of them are live-turn rungs: while a turn is in flight, the Observer's **current line** for that turn ([D187]), else the **turn's own ask**. At rest neither exists and the ladder is the standing one it always was, which is the property the split protects — the line a list of sessions is scanned by must not move because a turn started, and the session picker opts out of both live rungs for the same reason. The ask rung is a floor, not a feature: a written line has a latency tail, and for the seconds before one lands the ask is the one thing that cannot be wrong, so it is marked `data-stamp` like the other stand-ins and yields the moment a line is written. The current line is cleared by the turn rather than by a message — the store holds one line per session with the stamp it was written at, and a line older than the turn in flight belongs to a turn that is over — because the turn is a fact the digest holds and the channel does not. Then the standing rungs: the synopsis, else the session's **own first prompt** (load-bearing at the picker, where a freshly-scanned external session has never been summarized and its first prompt is the only human-meaningful text the row holds), else `Created <stamp>`. The lower two are marked `data-stamp` and painted a step quieter, to say they are facts standing in. **The stamp rung has to work on a session with no turns**, which is the only state in which it is the *only* rung available — and both of the deck's sources were structurally blind to exactly that session: the card's replay anchor reads the first turn's timestamp out of a JSONL a zero-turn session has not written, and the ledger's workspace listing deliberately omits a content-empty row so nobody is offered an abandoned session to resume into. The listing rule stays; the spawn-time `session_updated` push is **kept rather than discarded** (`SessionLedgerStore.detachedRows`) and answered by id through `findRow` / `useSessionLedgerRow`. A card asking about *its own* session is not the picker browsing a workspace, and only one of those two questions the empty-row filter was written for. The line **always occupies its height**, so a description arriving does not move the line below it.

**The description shipped riding a headline's machinery, and the headline is now deleted rather than renamed.** It is worth recording how that came about, because every step of it was locally correct. built two renderers for a per-stretch **headline** — the card strip's bright leading run and a Cards intent line — and the description was then added as a second job beside it, taking the headline's emit completion as its trigger and the headline's text as one line of its digest. replaced the Cards line with the description, and this decision retired the Z2 commentary strip and had `tug-session-row.tsx` deliberately withhold the beat component's `headline` level. Nobody swept the producer. The headline's whole output chain was dead while its emit loop went on running — a second Haiku call a minute, writing to a ledger cache with no reader — because two of its functions had become load-bearing **by accident**: a completion nobody watched was the description's clock, and a line nobody read was a line of its evidence. So the consolidation re-homes both — the trigger onto the beats directly, the digest line onto the newest activity lines — and deletes the rest: the `summarize`/`summarize_done` jobs, that cache and its restore chain end to end, and the eval's retrospective lane. **Do not rebuild the headline chain**; a second per-stretch line is a new proposal that must argue for itself, not a restoration.

**The description used to ride the commentary strip's kill switch, and rides no switch of that kind now.** It gated on `pulse/enabled` only because it was composed inside that subsystem's emit loop, which is machinery rather than intent: a user who turns off the commentary did not ask for their session rows to go nameless. It then read a tenant switch of its own, and it reads [D187]'s one switch now along with everything else a model writes — and with that off the row falls through to the ladder's lower rungs and says something true rather than nothing. What stays rejected, *do not re-propose*: a **second** collection pass — the digest is one cut of evidence the subsystem already holds, and a parallel JSONL reader on its own schedule is the shape that was refused. **And the write still has no fallback row key:** ledger rows are keyed by *claude's* session id, the resolver answers it, and a resolver that cannot answer means the ask is **skipped entirely** rather than aimed at the tug id. The resolver returns `None` both for "no such entry" and for a contended lock, and after a rewind fork the tug id names the *parent's* row — so guessing it would write one session's description onto another's, and the session that waits a minute for its line has lost nothing.

**Due-ness is derived and defers rather than being swallowed.** A session is due when it has moved since its last ask, or when a settled stretch has a refresh pending; the once-a-minute pacer only decides *when*, never *whether*, because the beats that made a session due are consumed at spawn and not before. The predecessor swallowed the post-settle refresh outright whenever it fell inside the window, which is why a session that went quiet could sit under a stale line indefinitely. Burst and idle thresholds are gone with it: under a one-minute pacer they were arithmetic that could not change an outcome. Originating plan: 

**The atom paints in text ink, and the dot is its only color channel.** The pill keeps its rounded shape and drops the theme's session tone: the run takes the ordinary text color and the border a `currentcolor` mix. A colored pill around a colored dot was two tints saying one thing, and the dot's tint is the one carrying information. The theme-authored `session` tone on the `atom` slot — four `--tug-color(violet, …)` recipes per theme, aliased in one hop to `--tugx-session-identity-*` ([L17]) with `ELEMENT_SURFACE_PAIRING_MAP` entries — **stays authored** and is simply not consumed by this component; retiring the tokens is a separate question. Still rejected, *do not re-propose*: a **per-session hashed tint** (color is a semantic channel; a hashed hue reads as meaning while meaning nothing), and a bespoke `--tug-session-color` role token with the component deriving three of four values by `color-mix` — not a `--tug7-*` target, moves per-theme tunability into component CSS against [L20], and is invisible to the contrast audit, which cannot see through `color-mix`.

**A DOM paste has to ask the pasteboard, because the event cannot see the atom.** Inside Tug.app an atom copy is written natively to the Tug-private `dev.tugapp.prompt-atoms` type — the whole reason that path exists is that WebKit's pasteboard normalization swallows custom MIME types. The unnoticed consequence was symmetrical: a DOM `paste` event's `clipboardData` cannot see that type either, so `getData(TUG_ATOMS_MIME)` came back empty for a clipboard demonstrably carrying an atom, and the paste fell through to plain text — a session atom copied from the Session Summary panel landing in the composer as its citation *string*. The editor's own ⌘V responder action always read the bridge and did the right thing; every other route into a paste (a menu Paste, a keystroke WebKit handled before the chain saw it) reached the DOM event instead and lost the chip. So the DOM handler now consults the native bridge as its last branch before plain text: only inside Tug.app, only after the DOM has had every chance, with the bridge's own plain text as the fallback — a clipboard with no sidecar still pastes exactly what it would have, one async hop later. Both routes insert through one `insertSidecar`, because two implementations of one paste is one edit away from two behaviors.

**Wherever an atom is a mounted React chip, it is a subscribed one.** Its dot reads the session's phase this second and its name tracks renames — the Overview ref, the History line, the Changes list row (one chip *per row*), and the masthead's telemetry popover, all four for the price of the hook, because all four compose `TugSessionCitation`. **The composer's pasted atom is explicitly excluded and stays baked ink**: an atom in the prompt editor is a CodeMirror `AtomWidget` whose `toDOM` returns an `HTMLImageElement`, and the bake is not a styling choice but how an atom survives being *editor ink* — one `root.render` for the app's life ([L01]), a replaced inline element whose caret and selection semantics CodeMirror owns, and a copy/paste round trip through document text. A pasted atom's dot is a snapshot at paste time and its name the name as of paste, which is honest for a thing that is now text in a draft. *Do not propose mounting a live subtree there* without first solving the second-React-root problem.

**An unresolvable citation keeps its shape and says so.** Same pill, dashed border, muted ink, a forced idle dot, fully inert, tooltip *Session not found* — and it still shows whatever callsign the commit recorded, because a reference that cannot be followed is more useful naming what it named than showing a bare hash. The **slashed mark is retired** with the icon it slashed: there is nothing left to slash, and shape alone states the failure, which it already did alongside the slash. **Liveness is never a property of a reference**: sessions are never dead, only unfindable.

**Right-clicking the masthead's title copies the atom.** The full flavor set, through the same `useCopyableText` every other Tug chip uses — the atom should be reachable without opening anything, and right-click is the idiom already in place. The telemetry popover additionally *displays* the rendered atom above the flat citation, so the two copyable forms sit side by side, each labeled by what a paste of it yields.

**That panel is titled `Session Summary`, and it is a KEYED table, not a log.** Six rows in the gallery's order — `STATE` (the ledger's word and what the session is doing, `live · working`), `TURNS` (count and size), `CREATED` (or `COMPACTED`), `BRANCH`, `ATOM`, `CITATION` — with the keys small, letter-spaced, and in the ambient sans, and monospace reserved for the citation exactly as everywhere else in identity. There is no `LAST USED` row: the activity line a few pixels above the panel says `Last updated: …` at rest, on the same card. Two things the shipped panel got wrong are worth naming because both are structural rather than cosmetic. It composed `TugPopupListFrame`, **which paints everything but its own titlebar in mono** — right for the tabular logs it was built for, and it silently made this panel the one identity surface set in monospace, so the panel takes its type back explicitly. And its rows clipped: a value cell with `overflow: hidden` elides a run of text correctly and **cuts a bordered chip off mid-curve**, which is what happened to the atom's pill and the citation's badge. An **enclosed** value — anything with a border of its own — gets a cell that does not clip and is made shrinkable instead, so the chip elides its own run inside its own border, which is the same two-register rule the title already follows. **Exactly one of the pair is enclosed, and it is the atom.** The citation row is bare monospace text, never a `TugCopyBadge`: a bordered, tinted, icon-bearing chip directly under the atom's pill put the louder box on the quieter fact and made two enclosures where the distinction between the rows *is* the enclosure — the atom is the chip, the citation is the string you would paste. Both rows additionally carry a **trailing icon COPY** — the suite's `BlockCopyButton`, in the shape a popup already uses for it — because right-click is a gesture the reader has to know about and these are the two facts most worth taking away. The atom's button and the atom's right-click write the **same** thing, through the same two-step: `writeSessionAtomToClipboard` first (the citation on `text/plain`, the atom sidecar on `dev.tugapp.prompt-atoms`, so a paste back into a Tug surface re-materializes the live chip), falling back to a plain-text write when the native bridge is absent. The action cell is declared on **every** row, empty on four of them, so one value column runs the height of the panel. The panel is **400px, not the mock's 320**, and the citation sits one type step below the sans values: the mock draws no project prefix and no trailing copy, and at 320 the real string — `<project>/<callsign> (<short-id>)`, a fixed grammar rather than open-ended prose — ended in an ellipsis mid-id, which is a panel truncating the one fact a reader opened it to take away. Monospace also reads larger than sans at a shared nominal size, so the quietest fact was set in the loudest run.

Those two halves fight, and the record is where they are kept apart. A `recordedTag` — the callsign inside a commit's own trailer — fills `SessionIdentity.tag`, so a reader of `tag` alone cannot tell the ledger's word from the commit's copy of it, and one that tried would call every foreign commit's session findable. So resolvability is **its own field**, `SessionIdentity.resolved`, true only on ledger-fed evidence (a callsign in the tag store, a card binding supplying the project, or a `resolve_sessions` hit) and conspicuously indifferent to `recordedTag`. A third rendering falls out and is required: while the round trip is in flight the chip is **inert but not slashed** — claiming *not found* before asking is the same lie pointing the other way. And the click belongs to `TugSessionCitation` rather than to each surface, offered only when a card is actually bound (a subscribed read, so it appears and disappears with the card): every citation means one thing by a click, and *looking clickable while doing nothing* is the failure the Overview's disabled refs were written to avoid.

Also retired, *do not re-propose*: the **`(branch)` title suffix** — the branch left identity and lives only in the telemetry popover; and **`/retag`** — the callsign is immutable, which is the property every commit trailer now rests on.

**And what was KEPT, so it is not re-litigated as an oversight.** The slot-stack badge and the `…` section menu stay in a masthead pane's title bar; the masthead's **two popovers** stay — the tape's expanded Activity card and the activity run's recent-pulses history, whose intent groupings are where the retired standing-goal line survives; the composer's pasted atom stays baked ink; `sessionIdentityLine` and `sessionCitation` are unchanged, as are the atom's clipboard flavors and the commit trailer pair. The atom's *face* changed; its *payloads* did not.

Two open questions were deliberately deferred rather than answered: whether the atom's sidecar segment should carry the short id (nothing needs exact re-resolution yet — the callsign is already a unique permanent key), and whether the Changes card's server-side `session_row_title` should adopt this grammar (it is a sixth surface naming sessions and currently gives a different answer; the change is independent of everything here).

`tugdeck/src/lib/session-identity.ts` (the resolver, the hook, `sessionTitleParts`, the citation and line formatters, `resolveCitedSession`), `lib/code-session-store/use-session-phase.ts` (the session-keyed phase door and the doubt rule), `lib/session-activity-line.ts` (the rest grammar), `lib/session-created-at.ts` (the shared creation-date resolver), `lib/session-tag-store.ts` (the by-id cache and the reverse `tag → session_id` index behind `/resume <callsign>`), `lib/session-synopsis-store.ts`, `lib/session-atom.ts` (the clipboard flavors; the chip's raise dispatches the registry's `focus-session-card`, the same funnel the Cards rows use), `lib/pane-title.ts` (`cardTitleTextFor`), `components/tugways/tug-session-identity.tsx` (the family and `TugSessionCitation`), `components/tugways/session-phase-dot.tsx`, `components/tugways/tug-session-row.tsx` (the one row shape), `components/tugways/tug-activity-line.tsx`, `components/tugways/session-masthead.tsx`, `components/cards/cards-session-cell.tsx`, `components/tugways/cards/session-picker-cells.tsx`, `components/chrome/tug-pane.tsx` (the chrome tier and the width control's suppression); `tugrust/crates/tugcast/src/session_ledger.rs` (`minted_tags`, the mint reroll, `record_synopsis`, `scan_metrics_for`), `external_sessions.rs` (`refresh_session_metrics`), `feeds/agent_supervisor.rs` (`build_session_updated_frame`, the turn-end refresh), `feeds/observer.rs` (the one ask that writes both the post and the sentence, per [D187]), `feeds/changeset.rs` (the tag arm), `feeds/git.rs` + `tugcast-core/src/types.rs` (the typed trailers), `tugarc-core/src/ops.rs` (`session_trailer`). Gates: `at0280-shared-agent-absent.test.ts` on the ink, and the no-bare-resolver-in-components grep gate. Originating plans:, then (the content rollout). [D121], [D123], [D127], [L01], [L02], [L06], [L09], [L13], [L17], [L19], [L20], [L25]

**D133.** *(Superseded by [D198] — the agreement machinery this decision hardened is deleted outright; the picture is now a pure function of the store's bins and the clock, with no origin, no registration and no acknowledgement for anything to disagree across. Kept for the four defects it named, which are the case against the model rather than against its tuning.)* **The sparkline's one animation is stopped and started by REGISTRATION, never by replay, and its ack window is a freeze, never a blank.** The registration-hardening round ([D-series predecessor: ]) replaced the tear-down-and-recreate scroll with one WAAPI animation paused and resumed across dormancy — and shipped four defects of that model, which together were the whole "truncates, hops, clears, judders, sometimes vanishes" complaint. All four are corrections to the *protocol*, not to the drawing. **One: no paint during the ack window.** An ordinary committed-origin paint issued while a rebase's acknowledgement was in flight landed after the proposal in the surface's queue, so the origin move put the viewport over stale-origin pixels — blank for a tick, or until the *next event* when that tick was the settle burst's last, which is a tape that vanishes at idle and stays vanished. Ordinary paints now hold their fire while a rebase is pending (the tape keeps appending; nothing is lost), and the commit flushes one clear paint at the new origin. **Two: an origin-moving proposal paints `clear: false`.** The old proposal wiped the whole canvas and inked only the proposed region, so the transform still in force looked at blank canvas for the round-trip — the doc promised "the correct pixels, simply not moving" and the code delivered a flash. The proposal now draws into its own region (origins differ by whole epochs — several viewport-widths) and the committed picture survives untouched; only a FORCED same-origin re-registration clears, because there an unclear overdraw would double the translucent area fill. **Three: there is no `resume`.** `Animation.play` resumes from the held position while the clock ran on, so every same-epoch wake came back mis-registered by the whole dormant spell — the viewport on older ink or past the ink entirely — until the self-check snapped it a tick later: the judder. The surface's resume verb is deleted; a stopped scroll restarts only by writing the committed origin back as its start time (`setEpochStart`, whose hold-clearing side effect is the restart), and the tape tracks `scrollStopped` so a born-live tape — whose animation was created running and registered — writes nothing. **Four: non-live tapes park.** A born-idle tape now pauses its scroll at birth instead of running a compositor animation to its first epoch end; a flat re-entry inside the same epoch, and a canvas-reclaim repaint of any resting tape, re-register the paused position onto the clock before re-pausing — without which a repaint that assumes the clock under a transform held at an older reading is a truncated or vanished resting flatline. Colour work obeys the same window: a dominant-channel stamp mid-rebase moves the DOM attribute immediately (it is not canvas) and defers the canvas repaint to the commit, and theme swaps route through the tape (`SparklineTape.refreshColors`) rather than straight to the painter, for the same reason. The invariant the unit harness now asserts is correspondingly sharpened: a `setEpochStart` carrying the CURRENT committed origin is a re-registration and needs no proposal; only an origin MOVE must ride an acknowledged paint. *Do not re-propose*: a `resume`/`play` verb on the surface (any hold-relative restart reintroduces defect three); a clearing proposal paint ("the canvas holds one origin at a time" was the old doc's argument, and it is exactly what blanked the pre-move viewport); ordinary paints continuing through the ack window ("the tape must not miss a sample" — it doesn't; the commit flush carries them). `tugdeck/src/lib/sparkline-tape.ts` (the protocol: `paintProposal`, the paint hold, `registerScroll`/`stopScroll`/`parkScroll`, `colorsStale`), `lib/sparkline-geometry.ts` (`drawSparkline`'s `clear` flag), `lib/workers/sparkline-render-worker.ts` (the `clear` wire field), `components/tugways/tug-sparkline.tsx` (the surface: no resume, nullable stamp `t0`, `refreshColors`). [L06], [L13], [L26], [L27]

**D134.** *(Superseded by [D183] — a rail is always divided, there is no mode to choose, and the measurement pipeline this decision's follow-on arc built is deleted. Kept for the two verdicts it records and for the history of how the rail got here.)* **A shared sidebar rail is a stack by default, and splitting it is a choice the user makes per side — never a rule the deck applies.** This surface has now returned two lived verdicts, and D134 is their synthesis rather than a third opinion. The **first** build divided a shared rail automatically, and it was rejected: it spent a rail's height to show two half-cards, which is what a two-section card was already doing inside one rail, only less space-efficient — the whole point of lifting Jots out was to give it a full rail when it is the one you are reading. The **occlusion stack** that replaced it ([D121], [D123]) shipped, was lived on, and was found wanting the other way: it hides content the user wants visible at once. What both verdicts point at is not a better default but a **decision the deck cannot make** — whether two cards on one edge are one thing you switch between or two things you watch. So the division becomes state: `imposition.rails[side] = { mode?, order?, shares? }`, additive-optional on the v4 blob ([D121]'s widening precedent, no version bump), with **stack as the default and absent reading as stack** — every existing blob restores to exactly the deck it described. The doors are the title bar's **stack badge** (whose glyph now states the mode, and whose menu carries *Split Vertically* / *Stack* / *Equalize Heights* below the member rows), a per-shared-side **Stack | Split row on the Layout card**, a **draggable seam** between members, and a **drag on a member's title bar**, which inside the rail's corridor reorders instead of unpinning. **The split is a division of the RUN and nothing else** — one width per side, allocator untouched, every horizontal pin exactly as it was — and it is expressed as `calc` over per-side seam custom properties, so a window resize re-resolves a split rail in the browser's own reflow and costs zero JavaScript, the same discipline the rail width already rides ([L06]). **Order and heights are stored, and neither may follow z-order.** `shares` are weights keyed by componentId — never index-positional fractions — so membership churn can never misalign a height with the wrong card and a reorder is a pure translate; the effective order is the stored one filtered to the members standing, then any unnamed ones in **registration** order. That last clause is a correction, not a nicety: `sidebarStackOrder`'s docstring claimed registration order while its one caller passed `findSidebarPanes`, which walks `state.panes` — the array `activateCard` reorders — so the enumeration was z-derived. Harmless while every member drew the same rect (though it already armed an empty settle window on every rail activation, since the arrangement signature reads that enumeration); under a split it would have made **clicking the lower card visibly swap the two**. The fix is two-sided and both halves ship: the caller sorts into registration order, and `setRailMode(side, "split")` materializes an explicit `order` in the same commit, so vertical order becomes stored state at the instant it becomes visible. **Nothing ever deletes a rail arrangement** ([L23]): a side that drops to one member renders identically in both modes and re-applies its mode, order, and shares when the member returns — a card closing is an internal operation and may not destroy what the user arranged. Even *Stack* keeps the order and shares, so a re-split lands where they left it. **The settle had to learn height.** `flipDelta` returned `{dx, dy, sx}` and the Last pass skipped any frame with no move and no width change — which on a stack→split flip is the top member exactly, whose left, top, and width are all unchanged. It gains `sy`, and `springKeyframes` a `scaleY` term emitted only when it is not 1, so mode flips and churn re-flows cross instead of cutting while every everyday gesture keeps byte-identical keyframes. **A split shows one badge per member**, because nothing there is occluded — falsifying the "a same-width stack shows exactly one" sentence that both [D123] and the code's own comment carried, which is corrected in place rather than left standing; and the picker's rows list in rail order with the check on the **focused** member rather than the topmost, since checking the topmost when every member is visible is a claim about z-order dressed up as a claim about what you are looking at (`SlotStackEntry.topmost` is renamed `selected` for that reason). **Not done, deliberately:** auto-split on a second card (stack stays the default until the shipped feature earns otherwise), pairwise sub-groups or stack-within-split (the complexity cliff, and two or three cards do not need a tree), any width change, and a keyboard nudge on the seam — the seam ships pointer-first, with *Equalize Heights* in the menu keeping every outcome but a custom ratio keyboard-reachable, until the tabled focus-nav subsystem reopens. `lib/layout-imposer.ts` (`RailArrangement`, `railModeOf` / `withRail*`, `effectiveRailOrder`, `railSeamFractions` and its inverse `railSharesFromFractions`, `railSeamProperty`, `imposeSidebarStyle`'s `member` placement), `lib/pane-flip.ts` (`sy`), `serialization.ts` (`parseRails`, defensive and field-by-field), `deck-manager.ts` (`setRailMode` / `setRailOrder` / `setRailShares` / `equalizeRail`), `components/chrome/deck-canvas.tsx` (the seam properties and their stale-index sweep, the registration-ordered enumeration, the arrangement-signature terms, the seam element), `components/chrome/tug-pane.tsx` (the split frame, the mode-aware badge, the corridor drag), `components/layout/layout-card.tsx` + `layout-miniature.tsx` (the rail rows and the divided drawing), `components/tugways/action-vocabulary.ts` + `action-dispatch.ts` + `command-registry.ts` (`set-rail-mode`, `equalize-rail`, no chords). Full doctrine: [pane-model.md § Three geometry modes](pane-model.md). [D121], [D123], [D128], [D129], [L02], [L03], [L06], [L09], [L13], [L23], [L30]

**D135.** **A settle tween may smear a frame's raster only within a capped distortion; past the cap the frame crosses by real geometry, and a rail mode flip is a fade plus one true height change.** The FLIP settle's acceleration has a price the deck had stopped naming: the compositor rasterizes a frame once and animates the *texture*, so a translation is pixel-identical to moving the element but a **scale is a screenshot smear** — every border, corner radius, and glyph stretched rather than re-laid out, correct at no intermediate size. That was tolerable exactly as long as the only scale in the settle was small: the adjacent width-preset step (675↔800) deforms by 18.5% for 300ms and reads as motion. [D134]'s `sy` put a ~100% vertical stretch on the frame densest with small type and thin rules, and it read as what it was. **The policy is a numeric cap, not a judgment call per gesture**: distortion is `max(s, 1/s) − 1` — symmetric, a halving and a doubling both read 1.0 — and a scale term may ride in the transform tween only while distortion ≤ `MAX_FLIP_SCALE_DISTORTION` (0.2). The cap convicts what it convicts: both Wide width jumps (800↔1230 at 53.8%, 675↔1230 at 82.2%) are over it and now cross by a **real width tween**, not by a smear tuned to acquit them. **Over-cap motion is real geometry through TugAnimator, never rAF** ([L13] draws exactly this line: rAF is for gesture loops that read input per frame — the seam drag; a fire-and-forget settle tween is programmatic motion). `springSettleKeyframes` walks the actual `width` or `height` on the same damped spring the move rides, spring-in-the-offsets under a keyword easing as ever; the effect is main-thread by construction — the frame's subtree lays out truthfully on every frame, the same work the seam drag's live path already does, bounded to the settle window. **A frame carries ONE KIND of geometry term at a time, and a settle that carries a size runs as up to three beats in a fixed order — shrink, move, grow — each beat one effect on one clock.** Every frame that gets smaller shrinks in place; then every frame that changes position translates, transform-only; then every frame that gets larger grows in place; a beat no frame has a term in is skipped, and every frame's beat of a kind runs together. The order reads as *make room, move, close up*: a card arriving in a split column sees the sitting member shrink, then slides into the room that opened; a card leaving slides away, then the member left behind grows over the room. The seam concern this rule once answered with a single effect is met by the sequencing instead. An edge pinned by the *sum* of a translate and a size is only honest while both advance on one clock — and no beat ever carries the sum, so there is no sum to keep honest: the concern is a constraint on a frame carrying two terms at once, and it does not reach a frame that carries them in turn. Air opening between members during the shrink is the make-room beat being legible, not a seam failing, and it is not to be closed by collapsing the beats back into one effect. What the one-effect reading was written to protect still holds, per beat: a move beat is always transform-only and accelerated, a resize beat is main-thread by construction and re-lays out its subtree every frame regardless, and a frame that only moves — every everyday gesture, the stack move the whole design is measured against — plans to exactly one move beat carrying the same transform-only list it always has. The fade stays an effect of its own because a faded frame never moves. A frame interrupted mid-beat is held where the eye has it and re-planned from its current rect, and the interrupted beat's velocity is inherited only by the replacement's beat of the same kind — a shrink's into a shrink, a move's into a move — because the kinds are different motions in different units. **Both axes are floored at half a pixel**, so a measurement that came back a hair different is not a size change: without the floor a rail mode flip, which does not touch width at all, still reads `sx !== 1` off sub-pixel rounding and picks up a `scaleX` term for a deformation of one ten-millionth — invisible, and still a frame being told to do something it does not have to. **Height never smears at all**: the gestures that change a frame's height halve or double it, which no cap admits, so `FlipDelta` stays `{dx, dy, sx}` and any height delta is a real tween — [D134]'s `sy`/`scaleY` mechanism is removed, its problem statement kept. **A rail mode flip is choreographed, concurrently, in one settle window, and exactly one frame moves**: the member that survives the flip on screen crosses by real geometry — a translate plus a real height change — sweeping up off the run it kept (split) or growing back over it (stack). Every other member **fades and holds still**. It is arriving somewhere it has never been, or leaving for somewhere it will not be seen; there is no path between those places worth watching, and animating one is a card sliding across the rail for no reason. Fading in, the held pose is the final tile the commit already put it at. Fading out, holding takes a **static inverse** — one transform keyframe repeated so nothing interpolates, plus the first rect's height — because the commit has already moved the frame to the full run it will occupy invisibly behind the survivor, and letting that landing show is the distracting translate this rule exists to forbid. **The survivor is the rail's Z-FRONTMOST member, not its top tile.** A stack's members all draw the same rect and z-order alone decides which of them you see, so the frontmost is the card the flip leaves the user looking at, and it is therefore the card the growth belongs to; picking the top tile instead grows a frame that ends up hidden while the visible card arrives by a cut. The survivor is consequently not always at the top of the run — a frontmost bottom member has to travel up as it grows — and that is fine, because with `sy` gone the travel is a true translate plus a true height tween and costs nothing but motion the eye was already following. (Two earlier cuts were wrong in opposite directions and both are instructive: z-order deciding which frame moved *while faded frames were still being translated too* read as the departing card being flung across the rail — the defect was the flinging, not the basis; correcting it to rail order then quietly grew the wrong card.) **The animator's commit is residue, and each settle hands back what it displaced**: TugAnimator commits an animation's final value into `el.style` on completion, and a baked pixel length where React rendered `auto` or a `calc()` would freeze a member against the very expressions its geometry keeps reading (the seam property, a window resize). Each settle captures the inline values it is about to displace and restores them in its completion handler — which TugAnimator guarantees runs on the far side of the commit, because `finished` resolves after `commitStyles()`. *Do not re-propose*: a counter-scale on the content (two smears cancelling is still not one true pixel), a `clip-path` reveal (not transform-family; demotes the whole effect), one effect carrying a translate and a size together so that a seam stays pinned through the settle (the seam opening during the shrink is the make-room beat, and the one-clock argument constrains only a frame carrying both terms at once — which no beat does), or animating the shared seam custom property for a mode flip (the arriving member must stand at its final tile from the first frame, and the seam property would drag its edges through poses the design just rejected). `tugdeck/src/lib/pane-flip.ts` (`MAX_FLIP_SCALE_DISTORTION`, `scaleDistortion`, `SettleTerms` / `springSettleKeyframes`, `planSettleBeats` and `beatLaunchVelocity`; `sy`, `springKeyframes`, and `springSizeKeyframes` all removed — the one builder cuts every beat's list), `lib/imposer-motion.ts` (the `shrink` and `grow` recipes beside the crossing), `components/chrome/deck-canvas.tsx` (the cap check and the half-pixel floors, the beats and the holds between them, the fade, the fade plan armed against the previous rail modes, `railFrontmostPaneId`, `inlineRestorer`). The cap's verdict is settled for every preset pair. Amends [D134] (the settle sentence) and the smear tolerance [D134] argued from. [D133], [D134], [L06], [L09], [L13]

**D136.** **The space allocator's licence is graded by what the picture suffers, and assigning a card to a slot is one of the moments that runs it.** The allocator's original licence was all-or-nothing: move the rails only when the clamped solve tiles every seam within tolerance, otherwise `null` — leave everything exactly where it stands. Lived with, that discipline turned out to be the deck refusing to help precisely when help was most visible: pick a Layout whose chain overlaps and the rails would hold their chosen widths while the cards occluded one another, because the width that would have relieved the crowd missed perfection by a few pixels. The refusal read as timidity, and it was — a refused perfect answer had come to mean a preserved bad picture. **The moments widen by one.** A rail's width is still spent only when the user has just asked the deck to arrange itself, but `assignCardToSlot` now counts: it is the imposer's own verb — the Cards card's slot picker and the ⌘1..⌘9 chords both land on it — and a user who just told the deck what to arrange is owed a deck that makes room for it. The old carve-out ("the user moved a CARD") described the drag-out and the close, which remain non-moments; it never honestly described the assign, whose whole meaning is "arrange this". Dragging a card out of the chain, closing one, and seeding a deck still leave the rails alone. **The licence has three grades, each asked of the RESULT.** Grade one is the classic solve: one shared width per the least-squares fit, clamped between the tightest soft floor (a fifth under each chosen width, `LENS_FLEX_SHRINK_FRACTION`) and the slim ceiling, taken when every seam lands within `ALLOCATOR_RESIDUAL_TOLERANCE_PX` of the gap. Grade two: when the SOFT floor is what refused — the fit wants the rails narrower still — the licence deepens to each rail's hard `sizePolicy.min`: cards crowding or occluding one another is the deck failing to show the user's cards, and that outranks holding a rail's chosen width, so a fit reachable inside the deepened range tiles exactly and is taken. A rail holding its chosen width while the cards under it overlap is hoarding. Grade three: when even the widest licence cannot tile, the rails still move to the best width they may stand at, IF that visibly beats the standing picture — worst seam better by more than the tolerance — and introduces no overlap the standing picture does not have. **Overlap is never an acceptable purchase**: `seamPicture` reads both quantities off the same seams (`worstError`, how ragged; `worstOverlap`, how deep any neighbours occlude) because the licence asks two different questions of them — raggedness is what a move must visibly improve, overlap is what a move may never introduce. Without that second reading, a floor-clamped solve on a slack deck would "improve" a 20px gap into a 5px occlusion, a smaller number and a worse picture. **In the slack direction the fallback is capped at the widest CHOSEN width.** An untileable arrangement never conscripts width past the user's preference — it only gives back what past allocations took. This is the correction of a conflation the old doctrine carried: "never snap to the remembered preference" treated the live width as the user's, but the live width diverges from the durable one only when the allocator itself wrote it (a hand-drag writes the durable store as it goes), so returning a crushed rail to its chosen width when holding the crush buys nothing is returning the user's width to them, not taking it. Growth beyond the preference toward untileable slack is still refused — that half of "a resize that closes no gap is the deck taking the user's width for nothing" was right and stands. **Idempotent by construction**: every answer is a pure function of the canvas, chain, and durable policies — the standing widths enter only the is-it-worth-moving comparison — so re-running any moment after a move changes nothing, and `RailPolicy.currentWidth` is documented as never being the anchor. `null` still means *leave every rail exactly where it is*, and the move is still all-or-nothing across the rails at one shared width ([D128]). `tugdeck/src/lib/layout-imposer.ts` (`allocateSidebarWidths`'s three grades, `seamPicture`, `RailPolicy.currentWidth`, the module-header moments), `deck-manager.ts` (`assignCardToSlot` committing through `_commitImposition`, `_sidebarRails` folding `currentWidth`, `retuneSidebarAllocation`'s moments). The deepened floor, the floor-clamped improvement, the give-back and its cap, the overlap refusal, and the assign re-tuning in its own gesture are all this decision's. Amends [D128] (the shrink allowance and the failed-fit refusal) and the "two moments" doctrine in `retuneSidebarAllocation`'s and the imposer module's headers. [D121], [D128], [L02], [L09], [L23]

**D138.** **An arc's facts split cleanly in two: anything git can see is derived on every read and never stored; anything it cannot is declared once, in the arc log, by a verb.** An arc is a branch, a worktree, branch config, and an append-only log — there is no arc database, and this is the rule that keeps it that way. Rounds past base, worktree dirt, and the join journal are all visible to git, so `derive_stage` recomputes them on every read and they cannot go stale; the same call in `arc_detail_entries_in` (`tugarc-core/src/ops.rs`) serves `tugtool arc list` and the Changes card's snapshot, so the CLI and the card cannot disagree about an arc's base, rounds, or dirt. "This build succeeded", "this arc was audited", and "I am on step 4 of 9" are invisible to git, so `arc mark` and `arc step` write them to the log and `derive_stage` reads the latest declaration back. **A stage is never written to a config key** — that would give the derived half something to go stale against and duplicate the declared half. The precedence is `joining > declared > draft-ready > working > created`, with `joining` on top because a join journal means an interrupted teardown, the one state that actively needs a person. Two corollaries the code depends on. **Read paths never mint**: `arc_owner_key` returns the bare branch ref when an arc has no `tugid`, and only write-path verbs call `ensure_arc_id`, because a read that wrote git config would be a side-effecting read and a multi-process race on every feed recompute. And **a path handed outward is absolute, resolved where the main repository root is known**: every arc op normalizes through `main_repo_root` first, since a card's project directory may itself be a linked worktree and the receiver has no way to find the main root — so `ArcDetail.worktree_abs` and the changeset entry's `worktree` are absolute and no consumer composes one. The full model — identity, the seven stages, and what a binding is — is [arc-lifecycle.md](arc-lifecycle.md); how an agent behaves once working on one is [arc-work-doctrine.md](arc-work-doctrine.md). [D112], [D113], [D116]

**D139.** **An arc's documents live under the arc, at `<repo>/.tug/arcs/<name>/`, and the arc's name is their address.** The brief and the plan exist to carry decisions across the arc's cold session boundaries; nothing in the machinery ever needed them to be tracked files, and being tracked files is what made them a chore. Their residence in the working tree was the source of every mechanism that managed them — adoption at birth, the mid-run transplant, base-copy divergence detection, the restore that kept a discard from destroying the only copy, the join-time sweep into an archive, and a `[tugtool.arc].docs` declaration each project had to make before any of it could run. All of it is deleted rather than adapted, because **the document has one copy for the reason it has one home**: there is no residence to choose, so there is nothing to record, transplant, reconcile, or clean up. "This arc has a plan" is `plan.md` existing, read fresh on every composition ([D138]) — no config key remembers it. The name is what the verbs take: `arc run|step|documents <name>` take names only, and `plan lint|status|stamp` take a name or a path with the argument's *shape* deciding, so a name is an exact address rather than a search. Paths handed outward are absolute, resolved where the main repository root is known, and every path function normalizes through that root itself — a verb run inside an arc worktree must resolve the same file the base checkout would, or a run writes a second ledger nothing reads. The ledger is rewritten in place and never committed, so a run's whole walk leaves the arc worktree byte-for-byte clean; a replay's undo and redo move the ledger's commit cells along the replay's own recorded mapping, since moving the branch no longer moves them. **The asymmetry at the end is the point**: a join removes the directory and names the removal in its receipt, because the squash commit is the durable record of landed work and the documents have nothing to add to it; a discard *keeps* them and says where, because a discarded arc's documents are the only trace of decisions the user may return to, and `arc run <name>` reopens on them ([L23]). And because `.tug/` is gitignored, none of this is visible to `git status` — `ensure_tug_excluded` writes an anchored `/.tug/` into the repository's shared `info/exclude` the first time a verb needs it, so a project with no `.gitignore` at all stays clean. [D138], [L23]

**D140.** **The allocator's assign-moment is about chain MEMBERSHIP, not about the assign verb.** [D136] widened the space allocator's moments to include `assignCardToSlot`, on the reading that a user who just told the deck what to arrange is owed a deck that makes room for it. That reading is right for the gesture it was written against — a loose card, or one pulled out of a tab group, *entering* the chain — and wrong for the other gesture the same verb serves. A card that already holds a slot taking a different one changes nothing about who is in the chain: nothing new needs room, and the only width a re-solve finds is the width freed when the chain packs into fewer columns. Handing that to the rails grows a sidebar by most of a card on a ⌘-digit that named one card, which reads as the deck rearranging itself behind the user's back. So `assignCardsToSlots` reads chain membership *before* it mutates and commits with `retuneRails` set only when some target pane was not in the chain already. **The line that matters is card-addressed versus deck-addressed**, and it is the same line `setCardWidths` already stands on: a ⌃⌘-digit that drained a rail to its floor is the identical fault from the other side, and `retuneRails: false` was the identical answer. A rail's width is the user's; the Layouts click, the settled canvas resize, and a change to who stands in the chain are what license spending it. Everything else in [D136] — the three graded licences, the overlap refusal, the capped give-back, idempotence — is untouched; only the membership test in front of the assign is new. `deck-manager.ts` (`assignCardsToSlots`'s `chainBefore` set and the `joinsChain` commit, `_commitImposition`'s `retuneRails` option). A move inside the chain holds both rails, a licensed retune afterwards can still take the width, and a card JOINING re-tunes in its own gesture. Narrows [D136]. [D128], [D136], [L23]

**D141.** **Arc progress is a glyph, not a line: the compact register rides the title run beside the arc's name, the row's own indicator stays the bare phase dot, and no surface changes height to carry any of it.** The arc had grown a fourth line on the Cards card's bound session rows, which broke consistency with the 72px masthead and made row height a function of binding state. The fix turns progress into marks that ride ink the rows already have. **The title grammar** is `{name}^{arc}` followed by `ArcLifecycleMark` — the phase glyph, one pill, and the `i/N` of the declared run — composed as `SessionIdentityRow`'s progress cluster (`arc-lifecycle-mark.tsx`, `arc-phase-mark.tsx`, `tug-step-fraction.tsx`). The phase word rides the mark's one tooltip and its `aria-label` rather than the line, because `implementing` as text spends ~90px the glyph carries in 13. **The row's indicator stays the bare phase dot**, whatever the arc is doing. It used to become a segmented step ring once counters existed, and that was two marks drawing one step count in two geometries, free to disagree whenever either lagged; an arc says its progress in one place per surface, and the segmented ring now uniquely means a task list that is not an arc. **The width comes from the identity rule**: a custom name REMOVES the callsign from the title (`sessionTitleParts`), unconditionally — the callsign stays the citable handle in tooltips, citations, and every copy path, but never rides the title. It is unconditional because a custom name is unique: a user-set name is unique across lines at the write, and a name another line already wears is TAKEN from it by the newer `/rename` ([D170]), so there is no collision left for a callsign to disambiguate. The exception this rule originally carried — the callsign returning under a shared name — is retired along with the client-side apparatus that computed it. The two changes land together because the cluster fits the title line only in the width the callsign freed. **The Arcs card is always on** — every arc in every state, no presence hook, the band as a fixed address with a one-line empty state — because a card that comes and goes cannot be glanced at and no other rail card worked that way; its `kind` stays `"dashes"` (the tugbank persistence key), only the title moved. Each arc is a two-line block in ONE grammar both collapsed arc surfaces wear: the EYEBROW holds identities only (arc atom — hairline — worker mini-atom or Bind|Discard; no review tint, that yellow means waiting), and beneath it `ArcLifecycleLine` holds the work — the lifecycle track, the fraction while a step is current, the note, and the tone-colored divergence facts — the identical element the Changes shade's collapsed arc row renders under its worker atom. The fourth line, `ArcFactsRun`, and the doc+clock review glyphs on these rows are retired rather than unused; review still speaks through the identity run's tint and the join gates. `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/arc-lifecycle-mark.tsx`, `arc-phase-mark.tsx`, `arc-lifecycle-line.tsx`, `arc-lifecycle-block.tsx`, `tug-arc-track.tsx`, `session-identity-row.tsx` (the dot indicator and the cluster), `lib/session-identity.ts` (`sessionTitleParts`), `components/arcs/arcs-card.tsx`, `cards/session-changes/session-changes-arc-lane.tsx`. Amends [D132] (a custom-named title is the name alone). [D123], [D132], [D138], [D167], [D168], [L06], [L13], [L19], [L20]

**D142.** **The join is an arc, not a console: the machine works first, the user decides once, and every act lives in Z5 or in a prompt that summoned itself. Status is never a control.** The shade's arc face had become a panel of buttons — `RESOLVE` / `RESOLVE AGAIN`, `VERIFY`, `Resume teardown`, `JOIN ANYWAY` — each of which asked the user to drive a step the server was perfectly able to take on its own, and none of which could be pressed until the user had gone looking for the surface that held it. **The machine takes every step it can take unattended.** A `built` arc is reconciled and Tier-0-checked by a server-side **join pilot** with no gesture at all, exactly once per `(base_sha, arc_head)` pair — the mark is what bounds the re-runs, and it is read inside the dispatched task under the occupancy guard rather than in the changeset recompute's predicate, because the recompute is a hot path and a mark checked there but acted on a hop later is a time-of-check/time-of-use window a second recompute walks straight through. **The one decision left is the user's**, and it comes to them: a durable `join.prompt` fact raises an ask on the bound session offering *Join now* / *Review first* / *Not yet* (a card-hosted modal as written here; [D150] moves it inline to the transcript's live edge — presentation, not machinery), and the re-ask policy is written on the branch (`branch.tugarc/<n>.tugjoinprompted` = `clean`|`red`) so a base that moves and re-reconciles to the **same decision** raises nothing, while a decision that *changes* asks again. A prompt yields to a composer already in a landing mode — a modal over a half-typed commit is precisely the interruption this design exists to delete — but a running turn is not a reason to hold. **A red no longer refuses; it arms.** The Z5 land button takes `role="danger"` with a confirm naming the failing checks, and the answer rides the press as `anyway: true` rather than being remembered on the mode, so a decision made about one candidate never carries onto the next. **Status wears the transcript's own chrome.** `arcJoinRegister` is one pure derivation — no store reads, no clock, no `components/` imports — mounted on the Arcs card row, the shade's arc row and the composer's status row, and it returns a `ToolCallPhase` so `BlockHeader`'s pulsing dot means here exactly what it means over every tool call; inventing a second status vocabulary for the same kind of fact is how two surfaces come to disagree about one arc. **The arm order is the derivation** and is stated in the module: wire-drop, blocker, live join, question, stated refusal, run, terminal result, verdict, nothing. **The rare verbs go behind a `⋯`.** Bind/Unbind and Discard are real, reachable and almost never pressed; standing on the row they read as peers of constant acts, and behind a menu they keep working and stop shouting. A disabled menu item takes no pointer events, so its refusal cannot ride a `title` or a tooltip and rides the **item's own label** instead (`Discard — Wait for the turn to finish`) — which is what let the join face's entire refusal list be deleted without weakening [L31]. The join itself is deliberately *not* in that menu: it is a decision made in the composer, and a second door to a one-door gesture is the thicket rebuilt. **Two defects this arc's tests surfaced are doctrine-shaped and closed here.** The land-beat subscription was keyed on `project_dir` while the beat frames echo `workspace_key`, so two surfaces were subscribed to a cell nothing writes — the [L29] spelling class, one field over, and the reason the key now travels as its own `workspaceKey` prop rather than being re-derived per mount. And the join narrated itself invisibly, for two compounding reasons: the store deleted its beat row on the terminal frame, which on a fast join arrives in the same batch as every beat it should have followed, and landing *exits* the mode, which cleared the composer's target at the exact moment the landing had the most to say. **The terminal frame now SETTLES the narration** — `joined` / `failed`, resting the way a `BlockHeader` rests rather than vanishing — and the mode keeps a `narration` past its own exit that `LandingSnapshot.narrating` carries to the composer, superseded by any mode that genuinely becomes active. A minimum-dwell timer was the alternative and was rejected: it would make the register say "joining" for N ms after the join was over, which is a resting lie, and it would not survive the batching that caused the problem. **Retired by this decision:** the four controls above, Tier 1 verification at join time (`do_changeset_join_verify` — it is not run at join time by anything and is not rescheduled elsewhere), and the resolve-idle silence deadline (`RESOLVE_IDLE_DEADLINE_MS`), which existed to notice a stall the beats now report directly. `tugrust/tugcast/src/feeds/` (the pilot and its dispatch), `tugarc-core/src/ops.rs` (`join_in`'s beat callback), `tugdeck/src/lib/arc-join-register.ts`, `changeset-join-store.ts` (the settle, `clearLand`), `join-mode-controller.ts` (`narration`), `landing-mode.ts` (`narrating`, `landRole`, `landConfirm`), `components/tugways/cards/session-changes/session-changes-arc-join.tsx`, `session-changes-arc-lane.tsx`, `arc-row-menu.tsx`. The whole arc stays unpressed until the one press, the red arms the button and the confirm lands it, a still resolver narrates for fifteen seconds, and the prompt carries the re-ask policy. Amends [D141] (the row grammar it draws on). [D138], [D141], [L02], [L11], [L19], [L20], [L29], [L31]

**D143.** **The shade's arc face is three lines and a fold: identity, work, join state stand; evidence and content fold; and everything that stands is one scale.** The [D142] rollout shipped the face as a pile — seven lines, each a different species at a different size, plus a `clean` chip and a standing "Wait for the turn to finish". Sorting the pile: an arc's facts are five species — **identity, work state, join state, evidence, content** — plus acts, and **only the first three stand** as the collapsed block's lines: the eyebrow (the worker's atom with the arc inside it, the hairline, the `⋯`), the work line, `ArcJoinRegister` — one left margin, the atom's own text inset. A line renders nothing it cannot say: no join → two lines, no register stub. **The outcome chip and the standing refusal are banished**, not restyled — the chip restated the register's word one surface over, and a standing refusal is a sentence about a press nobody made; a refusal rides the control that refuses (the composer's ⬆, the ⋯ item's own label), which is [D142]'s rule finishing its work. **Evidence and content live in the fold**, on the fronted row only, as three `TugSectionLabel`-headed sections ranked **report** (the join's evidence: the tier verdict as a shield line, the resolver's account, failures, and notes wearing a caution-bar advisory — a green with notes is a green *with exclusions*) · **rounds** (subjects and files, the count as the label's qualifier) · **draft** (the message the join lands with, quoted in muted mono) — the shade's own eyebrow grammar, not a third dialect, and the report section returns null rather than mounting an eyebrow over silence. **All three standing lines are one scale.** The shade is a surface you came to read, not a rail you glance at: the eyebrow's atom wears the chip tier's own `sm` size, the work line is `ArcLifecycleLine` inside `ArcLifecycleBlock size="read"` — the reading scale, where the track and its fraction sit on an `sm × 1.6` line box — the register spans the block instead of floating as a pill, and the stack indent follows the full `--tugx-session-atom-text-inset`. The Arcs card keeps `ArcLifecycleBlock size="rail"` and the `2xs` atom — that is the one place the two surfaces differ, and the difference is the point: the card glances, the shade reads. `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/arc-lifecycle-block.tsx` (`size`), `arc-lifecycle-line.tsx`, `tug-arc-track.tsx`, `tug-arc-atom.tsx` (the atom's size), `cards/session-changes/session-changes-arc-lane.tsx`/`.css` (the block, the fold), `session-changes-arc-join.tsx`/`.css` (the report section; `deriveJoinFace` slimmed to outcome + resolve — the sentence is `arcJoinRegister`'s). Amends [D142] (the face it disarmed) and [D141] (the two-surface grammar, now two scales). [D141], [D142], [D168], [L06], [L19], [L20], [L31]

**D144.** **A join lands exactly one commit on the base, carrying the authored draft. The resolution ladder supplies bytes; the strategy supplies shape; and neither may be inferred from the other.** On 2026-08-20 a clean arc joined and arrived on `main` as five linear commits with the draft unread — no squash was ever composed, so there was nothing to put it in. Two independent defects compounded, and both are the same mistake in different clothes: **an internal mechanism was allowed to decide a user-visible outcome.** First, the ladder's **rung 1 (the replay probe) stood above the check for whether anything conflicted at all**. A replay is trivially clean whenever the base has not moved, so *every ordinary join* exited as `JoinShape::Replay` with the round chain as its candidate, and the clean-squash arm below it was unreachable for very nearly every arc that ever joined. The ladder is a **conflict** ladder: no rung may decide the shape of a join that had no conflict for it to decide, so the `stages.is_empty()` arm now stands ahead of every rung. Second, `join_in`'s candidate path **short-circuited the strategy match entirely**, landing with `git merge --ff-only` and reporting the candidate's own message — so the ladder's internal shape became the base's history, and `strategy: Squash` (which every route passes) was inert. The candidate now supplies the resolved **bytes** only; `strategy` decides the shape, `Squash` squash-lands it under `integrate_message`, and `Rebase` stays the one explicit opt-in to a linear land that keeps the rounds' own messages. Staleness is now **stated** — an ancestry test, the same one `resolve::candidate_status` applies — rather than inferred from `--ff-only` failing, which conflated "the base moved" with "this strategy declines to fast-forward" and is what made the fast-forward look load-bearing. **The procedural failure is the one worth remembering:** the draft contract was pinned at the message-*composition* layer (`integrate_message` returns the draft — six tests) and never at the *landing* layer, so the whole suite stayed green across a change that stopped landing the draft at all. A contract is pinned where the user meets it. The two new pins assert what arrives **on the base** — one commit, that subject, both rounds' bytes — and were each verified to fail against the old behavior before being kept. `tugrust/crates/tugarc-core/src/resolve.rs` (rung order, module doctrine), `ops.rs` (`JoinOptions::candidate`, the candidate land). Amends [D142] (every join rides a candidate — it does, and the candidate is not the shape). [D142], [L31]

**D145.** **The callsign is carried everywhere and shown only where it is the name.** [D141] made a custom name replace the callsign in a session's title, and landed it in `sessionTitleParts` — which is the title tier, not the whole app. Every surface that composes `TugSessionIdentity` inherited the rule for free (the masthead, the Cards monitor rows, the Arcs rows, the picker, the Overview's ref atoms, the transcript's session chips), and the two that do not compose it kept showing a handle for a session the user had already named. Both are closed here, and the shape of each is worth having written down. **The composer's atom chip** is a Canvas bake inside an `<img>`, so it draws its segment's stored `label` — and that label is the *reference*, `<project>/<callsign>`, which the wire marker and the clipboard sidecar both depend on. So the chip resolves its own display text at bake time (`sessionChipLabel` in `tug-atom-img.ts`), beside the phase dot, which was already the other fact about a session chip that is not in its segment; the segment, the `data-atom-value`, and the `title` hover are untouched. A bake can neither subscribe nor cascade ([P14]), so a `/rename` reaches an already-drawn chip through the editor's widget regeneration — the **same door a theme switch comes through**, subscribed to `sessionNameStore` in `tug-text-editor.tsx`. The name store only: a callsign is minted before any atom of it can exist, so the tag store cannot change a chip's text out from under it, and subscribing to it would regenerate every chip in every editor on every session spawn. **The Cards card's one-line rows** — a session card standing as a tab in a multi-card pane, or as a stacked pane row — draw `CardIdentity.title`, which was the Line verbatim while the three-line monitor row beside them obeyed the rule. The projection now resolves the display title, and the Line moves to a `callsignLine` field that is **matched but never drawn**: hiding the handle must not make it unfindable, which is the promise the whole rule rests on, and the filter is where that promise is kept. The flat join lives in `sessionDisplayTitle` rather than at each call site, so a flat row, a chip, and a label cannot come to disagree about whether a collision shows a `:` or a space. **What deliberately still shows a callsign:** the citation and every copy path (a reference a reader cannot resolve is a name, not a reference), the tooltip (which exists to carry what the run cannot), the pane title in the tab strip and Window menu (constant for the life of a binding, and not a notification path), and export filenames. `tugdeck/src/lib/session-identity.ts` (`sessionDisplayTitle`, `sessionDisplayTitleForBinding`), `lib/tug-atom-img.ts` (`sessionChipLabel`), `components/tugways/tug-text-editor.tsx` (the name-store regeneration), `components/cards/cards-data-source.ts` (`title` / `callsignLine`). The chip draws the name, a rename repaints it, and the atom's value does not move. Amends [D141] (which stated the rule, at one tier). [D123], [D141], [L02], [L20]

**D147.** **The join arms itself from recorded facts. Skills narrate; verbs record; the server derives.** On 2026-08-20 a `dash-implement` run walked three steps of a plan on the `imposer-polish` arc. Every step committed, every ledger row read `done`, the join draft was written — and no join was ever offered, because the run never reached the phase of the skill that types `tugtool arc mark <name> built`, and the pilot's first gate was `stage == "built"`. The entire [D142] arc sat dark behind one declaration a skill had to remember to make. **Nothing the user experiences may depend on a skill executing a chore.** The fix is to derive readiness from what the machine already records. **A run declares where it ends.** `tugtool arc step <name> start <n>` now requires `--through <m>`, naming the final step of the selection; the value is written to the arc log as a `run-through` marker and folded back by `read_declarations`, so `run_complete` is `last step-done >= run_through` — a recorded fact, not a guess. There is deliberately **no timer, quiet window, or quiescence heuristic** anywhere in this: the machine knows which steps were asked for and which are done, and inferring "probably finished" from silence would be a worse answer to a question that has an exact one. A run that stops early stays incomplete, which is correct — it *is* incomplete. **A plan-less arc arms on every committed round** with a clean tracked worktree, because there is no selection to finish and a landed round is all the intent there is. **`mark built` / `audited` still arm and now gate nothing** — telemetry that stamps a stage word, and the manual unblock for anything the derivation misses. **Readiness is computed once**, in `tugarc_core::log::join_ready`, stamped on `ArcDetail`, and read by both the pilot and the standing prompt; a word test would not do, because an arc declared `built` reads stage `built` and is equally joinable. One discovery reshaped the predicate: `arc step done` rewrites the plan's ledger row *after* the round it describes has committed, so **every finished run ends with its own plan tracked-dirty** — read literally, a tracked-dirt disqualifier would have left every completed selection permanently unready. `unfinished_tracked_dirt` excludes the arc's own plan path, and that exclusion is load-bearing rather than a convenience. **A joinable arc reads `ready`** — a derived `derive_stage` arm above the step declaration (a finished run's latest declaration is a `step-done`, which would otherwise say `implementing` forever) and below `built`/`audited`. **The client carried its own copy of the server's gate, in four places**, and that is the durable lesson here: removing a server-side condition is not a one-line change when the faces have re-implemented it. `arc-join-register.ts` returned `null` unless the stage was `built`, so the register would have gone dark on all three [D143] surfaces while the modal fired; its no-verdict beat keyed on `built` again; `arc-meta-line.tsx`'s `STAGES_PAST_THE_WALK` decides whether `implementing (8/8)` reads as a finished walk; `arc-stage-mark.tsx` had no glyph. All four now read the joinable set (`ready` · `built` · `audited`) rather than one word. **The pilot works only for arcs bound to a live session.** Derived readiness widens its population from "explicitly marked" to "every arc with a round", and its attempt mark is keyed on the head pair — so one commit on the base would re-qualify every arc at once and turn an ordinary landing into N workspace builds, each spent on nobody, since the prompt raises only on a bound card. Two consequences follow and both are pinned: **`arc create` does not bind**, so the skills' Setup phase must (`tugtool arc bind <name>`) or a run ends with the work done and no join in sight; and an unbound joinable arc must **say nothing** on the Arcs card rather than rest at "Building the joined tree" forever, promising a check the pilot has already declined to run. **The prompt says what it would land, and where the words came from.** The message precedence is silent by construction — a forgotten draft lands the branch description, an arc with neither lands `Arc work` — so `ArcJoinPrompt` carries `message` + `message_source` (`draft` | `description` | `fallback`), composed by `landing_message_preview`, which shares its composition with `integrate_message` so the preview and the act cannot drift. It is display, not identity: the id is untouched, so editing a draft while the ask stands cannot orphan the answer being given, and the sheet's quote is a snapshot of the question it was raised for rather than a live view. **A log-only write reaches no watcher**, because the arc log lives under the data dir rather than the workspace, so the aggregate feed's 2 s probe now stats each open project's `arc log.md` mtime beside the drafts version — and that half is deliberately not under the probe's `ledger.is_some` guard, which would have taken the mark path down with it. `tugrust/crates/tugarc-core/src/arc.rs` (`run_through`, `run_complete`, `join_ready`, `unfinished_tracked_dirt`), `ops.rs` (`step_in`'s `--through`, `derive_stage`, `landing_message_preview`), `tugtool/src/arc.rs` (the refusal), `tugcast/src/feeds/join_pilot.rs` (`bound`), `join_board.rs` (the prompt's readiness gate and its provenance), `changeset.rs` (the pilot decided on the async side, where boundness already is), `changeset_all.rs` (the arc log probe), `tugcast-core/src/types.rs`, `tugdeck/src/lib/arc-join-register.ts` (`JOINABLE_STAGES`, `bound`), `changeset-types.ts`, `components/tugways/arc-meta-line.tsx`, `arc-stage-mark.tsx`, `cards/join-prompt-sheet.tsx`/`.css`, `components/arcs/arcs-card.tsx`, `tugplug/skills/arc-implement/SKILL.md` and `tugplug/skills/arc/SKILL.md` (the bind, the declared run, the build demoted to an offer). The no-mark guarantee is held by an absence rather than an assertion — nothing marks at all, so a `mark built` reintroduced anywhere would hide the regression the moment it returned. Amends [D142] (the trigger) and [D143] (the stage vocabulary the faces key on), and cites [D144] as the same failure class: an internal mechanism deciding a user-visible outcome. [D142], [D143], [D144], [L02], [L20], [L31]

**D148.** **An arc's numerals count the declared run; the track draws one tick per plan step and lights the run across it.** The two are different questions and one row was answering only the second. `arc step start 1 --through 3` records both facts — a `run-through 3` line and a `step-start 1/10` whose denominator is the plan's whole ledger — but only the plan pair reached the wire, so an invocation of `Steps 1–3` produced a masthead reading `1/10` beside a task list reading `0/3` and a description saying "steps 1–3". Both numbers were honest; nothing composed them. The unit somebody *asked for* is the declared run: it is what the task list mirrors, what the invocation names, and what the join arms from ([D147], where `run_complete` is `done >= run_through`), so it is what a glanceable counter should answer. `tugarc_core::log::run_fraction` derives the run's `(position, length)` from the arc log fold — `run_first` latches on the first step declaration after a `run-through` line and is cleared by the next one, which works because the writer appends the run's declaration *before* its opening step and only when the value changes — and `ArcDetail`/`ArcStatus`/`ArcChangesetEntry` carry the pair beside the plan's, one derivation for every surface as in [D147]. **Where the run sits inside the larger plan is said graphically, never in words.** The first draft of this hung a sentence ("step 1 of 10 in the plan") on the roomiest rows and was rejected on the grounds these surfaces exist to be concise; the answer is the track. `TugArcTrack`'s implement cell draws one tick per *plan* step, and `tickState` gives each tick one of four readings — `done`, `withdrawn`, `active`, `pending` — so one strip carries the plan's size, which steps have closed, which is open, and which were decided against, and a run of steps 5–7 shows its walk two-thirds of the way along. The implement cell is three cells wide at every plan length, so the strip is the same graphic at three steps and at twenty-four; a cell that sized to its ticks made the strip's width a function of the plan's length, and on a narrow row it grew into whatever was set beside it. It costs one tick state painted from `data-state` ([L06]), no words on any row, and no pixel of height. A generation that declared no run keeps the plan pair as an explicit degraded case, which is every arc log written before runs were declared. Words go only into the `aria-label`, where they cost nothing and can afford to say both. Extends [D147] from what the machine derives to what the faces show. [D142], [D147], [D168], [L06], [L13], [L20]

**D149.** **The join verifies nothing; the run ends on the tree the join will land.** An arc is *make these changes and fit them back onto main*, and until this, nothing in a run ever verified the fit. Two mechanisms were spending real minutes each and neither one tested the deliverable. The devise skeleton's Integration Checkpoint pattern told plan authors to close with a terminal step re-listing the full sweep — `cargo nextest run`, `tsc`, `vite build`, `bun test`, `app-test-changed` — against the **sandbox**, the arc's own tree frozen at branch time: every command in it had already run, on those exact bytes, inside the step that changed them. Then the join paid a second time for the right tree: the pilot built the joined tree and ran the project's Tier 0 over it, **cold**, because `Workshop` is warm-by-design only from the second check on and most arcs are checked once and die — so a finished run met "Building the joined tree" standing in front of the dialog, which is what the field reported. Both are deleted. The ending becomes one procedure, stated in [devise-skeleton.md](devise-skeleton.md#execution-steps) and enforced by the doctrine's *a checkpoint that passed is spent*: `tugtool arc replay <name>` puts the rounds on the live base, and the scoped verification runs **only** when the replay moved the tree (`Replayed`/`Recorded`); `Current` means the last step's checkpoint already verified these bytes by identity and nothing re-runs; `Conflicted` is work arriving at the right desk, resolved in the warm worktree with the model present rather than by a ladder at join time in front of the user. What lands is what was tested, once, and the join inherits tested bytes with nothing left to check — so **the join gate is reconcile-clean alone**, and the prompt raises the moment a candidate stands. Gone with the tiers: `verdict_gate`, the `Verification` fact and its branch-config keys, `--anyway` and the durable override it answered, `PilotAction::CheckTier0`, `ArcJoinVerification` on the wire, the register's `checking` states, and the `verify_tier*` config keys. `verify-tier0.sh`'s command shape survives, renamed `scripts/verify-fit.sh`, as the ending's scoped check. Two alternatives were rejected and should not be re-proposed: a **terminal sandbox sweep** (the shape being deleted — it tests the wrong tree at full price), and **join-time Tier 0 kept as advisory rather than blocking** (which keeps the cold multi-minute build and merely stops it from gating, so it still stands between a finished run and the dialog, now without even a gate to justify it). The accepted cost is a short window between the run's ending and the join in which the base may move again; it is bounded by the replay's own compare-and-swap and cheap to re-run. Supersedes the join-time verification of [D142] and completes [D147]'s demotion of the mark to telemetry. [D142], [D144], [D147]

**D150.** **The join speaks inline, in one place, and stops speaking when it is done.** On 2026-08-22 a real join proved the [D147] arc end to end and failed on every part of how it *spoke*. Four defects, one shape: the arc reported itself on surfaces that were wrong, stale, or both. **The decision mounts inline at the transcript's live edge, not as a pane modal.** The modal's recorded justification was that the ask must not be missed — but an inline surface at the live edge is summoned by the same fact, on the same bound card, directly above the composer where the user is already reading; what the modal added beyond that was *blocking*, and its scrim greyed out the run's own ending narration, which is the exact context the decision is about.
