The gates are unchanged and travel with the hook: answered-ids per mount, yield to a composer already landing, close when the fact clears, supersede on a new `request_id`, dismissal-is-"not-yet". **The landing morphs in place on the same element** ([D13]'s precedent): the sheet kept a frozen `showSheet({title: "Join?"})` header asking a question the user had already answered while the join ran, and inline there is no separate header to go stale. **The terminal record is the transcript's receipt row, and the composer register rests then clears.** The register's indefinite rest was right when it was the only surface that could report a landed join; with the receipt row ([D111]) and the inline surface's settled line both present, a third copy resting forever on an *input* surface is furniture. A **failed** join keeps the register — failure is the outcome that still wants an act. **The lands-as is a live view, and its two lies had two independent causes, both real.** The read side of the arc draft lookup used bare `std::fs::canonicalize` while the write side keyed through the [L29] gateway, so two spellings of one base root could read as two projects; and `POST /api/draft` fired no feed bump, so a standing ask served the message it was raised with forever. Fixing the first required hoisting `resolve_to_claude_form` into `tugcore::pathform` — `tugarc-core` cannot reach `tugcast`, and the *layering* was part of the defect, not an excuse for a tolerate-both-spellings shim. **A landing outlives its arc**, and that is the trap the new surface fell into first: the instant the join lands the arc's feed entry is gone, so a body reading the card's live `arcName` loses the store key it was watching at the moment the outcome arrives, reverts to "no progress yet", and never settles — so it never departs. The hook captures the arc name at the press. **The beats were never broken.** Driven under a pin, the surface paints all four in order; the 2026-08-22 reading of "no progress at all" was the pre-first-beat gap plus a join fast enough to outrun a sampler, which is why the pin records the DOM's own history with a `MutationObserver` rather than polling. `tugrust/crates/tugcore/src/pathform.rs` (the hoisted gateway), `tugcast/src/path_resolver.rs` (delegation), `tugarc-core/src/ops.rs` (`project_spellings`), `tugcast/src/server.rs` (`draft_handler`'s bump), `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/cards/join-prompt-inline.tsx`/`.css` (replacing `join-prompt-sheet.tsx`), `session-card-transcript.tsx` (`liveEdgeContent`), `session-card.tsx`, `lib/join-mode-controller.ts` (narration retirement), `lib/arc-join-register.ts` (`SETTLED_REST_MS`). Amends [D142] (its "card-hosted modal" is now this inline surface) and [D147] (whose "the sheet's quote is a snapshot" limitation this deletes). [D13], [D111], [D142], [D147], [D149], [L02], [L20], [L29]

**D151.** **A project declares how its own tree is hydrated, checked, and built in `[tugtool.arc]`, and every absence has a stated sentence rather than an invention.** tugplug ships inside the app bundle, so the skills are the onboarding voice for whatever repository Tug is pointed at — and they had been speaking fluent Tug: `bun install` as *the* hydration, `cargo nextest` as *the* baseline, `scripts/verify-fit.sh` as *the* fit check, `just app-debug` as *the* build, `tests/app-test/` and `tuglaws/` as though every project had signed them. `tugtool init` wrote `post_create = ["bun install --cwd tugdeck"]` into any repository, so the front door itself spoke the dialect. The engine was never the problem: `tugarc-core` knows no Justfile and the verbs are proven against scratch repositories. **The seam is the one [D149] left standing.** [D149] deleted `verify_tier0`/`verify_tier1`, the only per-project declaration the workflow had, leaving `post_create` as the surviving pattern — a declaration in the project's own committed config, read at the moment it is needed, absent by default. Two optional keys join it: `verify`, the run-ending's scoped fit check, and `build`, the command that produces an inspectable instance. Committed config means they ride the arc branch into every worktree. `tugtool arc config` is the single reader — JSON and text, absent keys as `null`, a missing config file exit 0 rather than an error — so no skill parses TOML in prose and absence reads the same everywhere. `verify` may carry `{base}`/`{head}`, which the consumer substitutes with the replayed range before running via `sh -c` from the worktree root; a command carrying neither runs unscoped, which is a project's right to say. **There is deliberately no test-selection key**: a plan's per-step checkpoints already name the commands for the work they verify, and the ending runs only the fit check, so a selection key would have no consumer moment that is not already spoken for — it would be a sweep-shaped key waiting to be misused. **This serves the RUN's ending, under [D149]'s own doctrine, and nothing else.** `verify` is consumed exactly where the ending already acts — after `arc replay` reports `Replayed` or `Recorded`, and after a resolved `Conflicted`; `Current` still re-runs nothing, declared or not. A red verify is ordinary work: fix it in the warm worktree, commit the fix as a round, re-run. **No declared command is ever run at join time.** The join gate remains reconcile-clean alone, and [D149]'s two rejected shapes — the terminal sandbox sweep and the join-time advisory — stay rejected. A portable verify declaration is not a reopened verification-at-the-join debate, and this sentence exists so it cannot be read as one. **The degradation doctrine generalizes `arc-review`'s missing-rubric clause**, which was already the right shape: *proceed on the criteria above and say so*. Every absence gets a sentence, the work proceeds at a named lower fidelity, nothing is invented, and the run **says so in the transcript** rather than only in the skill file. No `verify` → the ending checks what the replay moved with the plan's own checkpoint commands, never one the agent made up. No `build` → no build is offered, and the report says the work is inspectable at the worktree. No `tuglaws/` → the review or audit names its criteria inline, and `arc-devise` writes against the format summary its own prose carries with `tugtool plan lint` as the enforcement (the linter ships with the product and is project-agnostic; a plugin-shipped copy of the skeleton would be a second format contract, free to drift). No real-app harness → real-app claims are stated as unverified at that layer, and the fake-DOM/RTL bans are named as Tug doctrine binding where `arc-work-doctrine.md` exists rather than imposed on a project that never adopted them. **A hook acts only on its subject.** A gate that enforces one project's contract resolves the subject from the payload's `cwd` (then `$PWD`, then the git toplevel) and stays out of the way when that project is not what the command runs in. An inapplicable gate must never fire, alongside the standing rule that a broken gate must never block work. (The worked example was `gate-app-test-output.sh`, the app-test report gate, removed 2026-08-25 — see [D162].) `tugtool init`'s default is now project-neutral: an empty `post_create` and commented examples for all three keys. Proven on a repository with no Justfile, no package manager, no app-tests and no `tuglaws/`, walked create → step → commit → draft → replay → join twice — once undeclared to observe each degradation, once declaring `verify` with the base moved mid-arc so the replay legitimately reported `Replayed` and the declaration was consumed on a real range. `tugtool-core/src/config.rs` (`ArcConfig`), `tugtool/src/commands/init.rs` (`DEFAULT_CONFIG`), `tugtool/src/cli.rs` + `src/arc.rs` (`arc config`), `.tugtool/config.toml` (this repository as first consumer), `tuglaws/devise-skeleton.md` and `tuglaws/arc-work-doctrine.md` (the ending and the build offer), all seven `tugplug/skills/*/SKILL.md`. [D139], [D147], [D149], [D150]

**D152.** **The join's decision surface is the Changes shade. It is revealed, not raised — and because it stands, nothing has to remember that it was closed.** [D147] established that the offer must be *summoned rather than discovered*; [D150] read that as "mount a surface" and put the decision inline at the transcript's live edge. On 2026-08-21 the structural cost came due: a reflex Escape answered "Not yet", the dismissal mark `branch.tugarc/<name>.tugjoinprompted` suppressed every re-ask, and recovery took a hand-run `git config --unset-all`. **A transient surface has no reopen gesture by construction**, and every dialog that can be dismissed therefore needs a durable record of the dismissal, a policy for when that record expires, an answer frame to write it, and a re-ask rule nobody can hold in their head. A standing surface needs none of it: closing the Changes shade discards nothing, because the arc row is still in there. So the shade — which already hosts files and arcs, and which [D142] had explicitly disarmed of any join gesture — becomes the room the decision happens in, and the whole dismissal apparatus is deleted rather than tuned. **What survives is a fact, not an ask.** `ArcJoinState.prompt: Option<ArcJoinPrompt>` becomes `offer: Option<ArcJoinOffer>`; `question`, `options`, `ArcJoinPromptOption`, the `changeset_join_prompt_answer` control frame with its parser, dispatch arm and three-armed handler, the store's `answerJoinPrompt` sender and its `_err` arms, `join-prompt-inline.tsx` with `useJoinPrompt` and `JoinLandingBody`, and `verify.rs`'s `prompt_mark_key`/`read_prompt_mark`/`write_prompt_mark`/`clear_prompt_mark` are all gone. The pilot's own `tugjoinpilot` mark is untouched — it bounds the *machine's* re-runs, a different job with a different key — and is now the only mark on the arc. The three answers were never lost: *Join now* was always the composer's ⬆ (`changeset_join`, the same handler), *Review first* is entering the landing mode, and *Not yet* is closing a room. **The reveal is quiet-moment-gated and passive.** The shade is a *view swap* — it replaces the transcript pane rather than pushing content down — so it is a heavier surface than the row it replaces, and revealing it over streaming output would cover exactly what [D150] fought the modal's scrim to protect. It fires only when no turn is in flight, no landing is active, the composer holds no typed text, and the shade is not already showing; and it calls `show("changes")` and nothing else, entering no mode and touching no composer, because a reveal that commandeered the composer would be the interruption the prompt was criticized for, wearing a new shape. **The held signal is an accent dot** on the Z4A Changes segment while an offer stands and the room is closed — the unread idiom, derived per render from live facts, painted through `data-join-offer` and CSS ([L06]), with no label swap (the group stays invariant) and no pulse (pulsing is the registers' *running* vocabulary, and nothing is running). **The reveal is remembered per mount, and keyed on the arc head — not on the offer's `request_id`.** This is the one thing the design got wrong on the first pass and the app-test caught: `request_id` is `<arc>:<base_sha>:<arc_head>` and moves when *either* head does, so keying on it re-opened the room on every push to the base. The arc head is the fact that means *work you have not been shown* — the identical distinction the retired dismissal mark was keyed on, surviving the mark's death as a client-side rule. Nothing durable records it, so a reload re-reveals once; that cost is a glance, and the cost of remembering was the 2026-08-21 lockout. **The fold shows what would land, with its provenance.** When an offer stands the fronted row's `draft` section becomes **lands as** — the composed `tugarc(<name>): …` subject from `landing_message_preview`, which shares its composition with `integrate_message` so preview and act cannot drift — plus a note naming the precedence arm when the words are nobody's (`description`, `fallback`). This is the one place the landing-message precedence breaks its silence, and it now lives where the decision lives. `tugcast-core/src/types.rs`, `tugcast/src/feeds/join_board.rs` (`standing_offer`), `join_pilot.rs` (the one-mark docstring), `agent_supervisor.rs`, `tugarc-core/src/verify.rs`, `tugdeck/src/lib/changeset-types.ts`, `changeset-join-store.ts`, `components/tugways/cards/session-card.tsx` (the reveal effect), `tug-prompt-entry.tsx`/`.css` (the dot), `cards/session-changes/session-changes-arc-lane.tsx`/`.css` (lands-as). Amends [D150] (whose inline mount this deletes; its "one place, stops speaking when done" half is inherited by the shade) and [D142] (whose "prompt that summoned itself" is now the shade reveal, and whose disarming of the shade's join face this reverses only as far as *showing* the decision — the ⬆ is still the only control that lands). [D111], [D142], [D147], [D149], [D150], [L02], [L06], [L22]

**D153.** **A surface that shows an arc lets you act on one — by routing to the room where the act already lives, never by growing a new one.** Three surfaces displayed arcs and none of them let you touch one usefully: the Arcs card row had verbs but no destination, the Changes shade's lanes had a menu with two items in it, and the masthead's arc cluster was a face. Meanwhile every fact a decision needs was already painted. **Activation is navigation, never a verb** ([D142] holds): an Arcs card row fronts the card working that arc and reveals its Changes shade — [D152]'s one reveal path, sharing the card's own `revealedOffersRef` and `shadeViewController` so an explicit reveal spends the standing offer's head and the passive one never re-opens a room the reader has just been shown. The quiet-moment gate is deliberately not consulted: it exists to keep an *unbidden* reveal from covering what somebody is reading, and a click is its own license. **The dispatch target is `${cardId}-card-content`, and that is not a detail.** `sendToTarget` walks the responder chain *upward* from its target; the bare card id belongs to `card-host`, which answers only `SET_PROPERTY`, and the session card's handlers register one scope beneath it — so an action aimed at the bare id is swallowed with no error to observe. The first design made exactly that mistake and only reading `responder-chain.ts` found it, which is why the target is pinned by an app-test rather than by inspection. **A row that opens nothing must not look as though it opens something**: the same `resolveWorkerCard` value that decides what a click does paints `data-activatable`, and the CSS withholds the flush hover and the pointer from an inert row ([L06]). An inert row is not a refusal and owes no message. **Replay is the one act with no other door, and its reach is divergence, not boundness.** `decide_for_arc` gates the automatic replay on the autoreplay flags, an in-flight replay, the join journal and worktree dirt — boundness appears nowhere in it, and `bound_session` is read only to decide who to notify. So "unbound" never meant "untended": the population with no recourse is any diverged arc the engine's gates skip, bound or not. The item is enabled whenever `base_ahead > 0` or a previous replay stopped conflicted, and disabled *with its reason in the label* ([L31]) for the two no-ops the wire entry proves. Neither autoreplay flag is on the wire and neither is put there — a client predicting whether the engine is about to act would be re-implementing that gate behind glass. **And the outcome always speaks, because three of the five move nothing.** `Current`, `Deferred` and `Conflicted` leave the row byte-identical, which is the dead-button failure this arc has already paid for twice; the outcome posts to the pressed card's pane bulletin through `arcReplayOutcomeStore` + `ArcReplayNoticeController`, the `arcBindErrorStore` precedent for exactly this problem — a zero-render controller subscribing directly ([L22]), keyed on a monotonic `seq` so two identical answers both speak. `ReplayOutcome` serializes itself (`#[serde(tag = "outcome")]`), so `Deferred`'s reason and `Conflicted`'s round and paths ride the wire intact; a hand-written word would have thrown away the only text a refusal can be read from. **One menu grammar, on both surfaces**: the Arcs row's standing Bind and Discard buttons are gone behind the shade's own `⋯`, with `resolveBindTarget`'s refusals as disabled labels — Unbind is deliberately not among them, because it belongs to the fronted shade row and an Arcs row's job is to route you there. **Creation gains no door**: starting an arc stays a composer and skill act, and the masthead stays display-only. `tugcast/src/feeds/agent_supervisor.rs` (`changeset_replay`), `tugdeck/src/lib/changeset-verb-store.ts` and `lib/arc-replay-outcome-store.ts`, `components/tugways/cards/arc-replay-notice-controller.tsx`, `cards/session-changes/arc-row-menu.tsx` (`replayDisabledReason`) and `session-changes-arc-lane.tsx`, `components/arcs/arcs-card.tsx` (`resolveWorkerCard`) and its CSS, `components/tugways/action-vocabulary.ts` (`REVEAL_CHANGES`, `REQUEST_REPLAY_ARC`). [D142], [D147], [D149], [D152], [L02], [L06], [L11], [L22], [L31]

**D155.** **Durable ink belongs to the line of work, not to the session id it was written under: it is keyed by the line at every gateway, and it outlives the cap that bounds chatter.** The model this replaced settled that a rewind-fork *is* the conversation continued and moved the callsign to prove it — but it moved only the name. The receipts stayed behind, and five separate times a `/commit` or join receipt vanished from a transcript after a rebuild-and-relaunch. The mechanism was measured live: a fork mints a new `sessions` row and supersedes the parent (closed, tagless), while `shell_exchanges.db` and `refs.db` keep their rows keyed under the parent id. Nothing breaks until the relaunch, because the deck's card binding does not switch at fork time — a join receipt was observed written under the parent **twelve hours** after the fork. Then resume picks the fork, the restore read asks for the fork, and the server truthfully answers `total: 0`. **Every safeguard from the fork-restore incident worked exactly as designed and none of them could help**: unbounded retry, the `total`-versus-rows census and the `answered` flag all defend against a *lost* answer, and this was a well-formed answer to the wrong question. A receipt lives only in the ink ledgers — it is the user's act rather than session context ([D111]) — so no replay can reconstruct one, which makes this a plain violation of [L23]. **The fix is a key, not chain-walking.** Ink is **keyed by the line** ([D167]): `shell_exchanges.line_id` and `refs_runs`' `line_id` primary key, resolved at every gateway by asking the sessions ledger which line a segment belongs to, so a read issued under any segment's id — including a superseded one the deck is still bound to — finds the whole conversation's rows. Reads stay single-id and the wire is untouched: writes at `record_landing_receipt`, the shell settle persist and the refs run persist; reads at `do_list_shell_exchanges` and `do_list_refs`, which resolve the *query* while echoing the *requested* id, since the deck routes an answer back to the asking store by that echo. There is nothing left to transfer and therefore nothing that can be forgotten: the lineage-head resolver, the boot-time edge sweep, and the containment-evidence adoption pass are all gone, replaced by a one-time backfill that fills the new column on ledgers written before it existed. **And the rescue is protected from the cap that would undo it**: merging a fork's ink into one line makes `MAX_EXCHANGES_PER_SESSION` reachable, and eviction is oldest-first — precisely where the rescued receipts sit — so `LANDING_RECEIPT_COMMANDS` is excluded from both sides of the eviction predicate. The cap bounds chatter, which is all it was ever written for. `tugrust/crates/tugcast/src/shell_ledger.rs` (`line_id`, `LANDING_RECEIPT_COMMANDS`), `refs_ledger.rs` (`line_id` as the primary key, which resolves a collision by keeping the newer run rather than raising), `ink_backfill.rs`, `feeds/agent_supervisor.rs` (`resolve_ink_line`), `feeds/shell.rs`, `feeds/refs.rs`, `main.rs`. Amended by [D167], which supplies the key. [D111], [D167], [L23], [L29], [L31]

**D156.** **An arc with work ready to join is presented on the Changes route, not glanced at from the Prompt route — and the presentation is gated on live signals, so a deferral re-arms instead of dying.** [D152] made the Changes shade the room the join decision happens in, and the card revealed it by raising the shade and deliberately doing nothing else — a passive glance that entered no mode and touched no composer. Lived on, that half-measure is the workflow's most-reported failure: the room opens, but the Z4A route toggle still reads **Prompt** (its value derives from `landingActive`, and no mode was entered), the composer is still a prompt composer holding no draft, and the ⬆ still submits a prompt — so the join, which *is* mode entry plus that press, is behind a door the reveal declined to open. The card looked switched and was not. So the reveal becomes **route entry**: the same `enterChanges()` every other door uses, which picks join or commit from what the card is mated to, raises the shade through the existing mode↔sheet coupling, flips the toggle for free, seeds the draft, and arms the press. Nothing new guards the way out — Escape, ⌘., the shade's ✕, the Prompt segment, ⌃⌘C, and a completed land all already drop the mode and the shade, and the composer restores an in-progress prompt verbatim on exit, which is what makes an unbidden entry safe. **The second half is that gates must be signals.** The quiet-moment conditions are unchanged in substance — no turn in flight, no landing up, an empty composer, the shade not already showing — but two of them were read imperatively as one-shot peeks rather than as dependencies, so an offer arriving at a busy moment was deferred *permanently*: nothing woke the decision when the composer emptied or the shade closed, and the behavior read as random. Every gate is now a subscribed reading (the composer reports emptiness on transitions, never per keystroke), so the entry fires on its own the moment the room goes quiet. The once-per-arc-head memory is unchanged and still mount-local: the head is recorded only when entry actually fires, so a deferral retries and a dismissal stays dismissed. The explicit path ([D152]'s one reveal, an Arcs card row activating) takes the same form when an offer stands and stays a glance when none does — an arc mid-implementation has no join to arm. [D147], [D152], [L02], [L06], [L22]

**D157.** **A conflict is a complete block or it is content; and a resolution this arc already made is replayable on exact stage identity, above rerere.** Two findings from reading Jujutsu's source (Apache-2.0) against the landed conflicts-as-data round. First, `has_conflict_markers` matched any line *starting with* `<<<<<<<`, `=======`, `>>>>>>>`, or `|||||||` — and `=======` is markdown's setext heading underline, which two files in this repository carry today. The damage was worse than a wedged workshop: `is_clean_merge` is what rungs 3, 4, and 5 use to validate their **own output**, so a file whose three stages `git merge-file` merged perfectly cleanly was rejected because the clean result contained an underline, and the path fell through every machine rung to `unresolved` — the AI seam handed a conflict rung 3 had already settled. Detection is now structural, jj's `parse_conflict` shape: a marker line is a run of ≥7 identical marker characters at column 0 followed by end-of-line or a space and a label, and a conflict exists only where a complete ordered block does (opener, optional base section, separator, closer), with a second opener abandoning the block it interrupts. Git only ever writes complete blocks, so completeness *is* the signature. The trade is that a half-dismantled block reads as content, which an advisory names rather than refuses — and that advisory deliberately ignores a **lone `=======`**, because the separator is the one marker with a life as ordinary content and an advisory that fired on every conflicted markdown file would reintroduce the noise one channel over. Second, conflict validity is strict head equality, so any base motion invalidates the chain and the ladder starts over — cheap for the machine rungs, and fatal for the resolver's committed checkpoints, which are taught to nothing and are the most expensive resolutions in the system. A **salvage rung** replays them wherever the three stage oids and modes are byte-identical to the current ones and the path is marker-free at the prior tip: strictly narrower than rerere (object identity rather than textual preimage), and cheaper (one blob read rather than a scratch worktree and a real merge), so it sits above it. It is computed as a batch because `rerere_rung` is itself a batch call above the per-file loop, and skips that call entirely when salvage covers every path. Checkpoint work only — a prior run's machine-rung results re-derive through their own rungs. The prior chain is read raw at ladder entry, upstream of both `clear_conflict` writers. jj's N-way `Merge<T>` algebra, marker round-tripping, and marker-length escalation on write are deliberately not ported: git materializes our markers and resolvers rewrite whole files, so detection is the whole need. `tugrust/crates/tugarc-core/src/resolve.rs` (`scan_conflict_blocks`, `marker_kind`, `orphan_marker_lines`, `salvage_rung`, `ResolvedBy::Salvage`). [D115], [D138]

**D158.** **Where a durable ink row sits in the transcript is a written fact, recorded when the row is written; and no transcript entry ever wears a fabricated time.** The sixth vanished-receipt incident was the first in which nothing was lost. The `/dash-join` receipt was in the ledger under the exact id the deck resumes, the restore census read complete, and the block was painted — at `top: -5011px`, behind the final assistant turn, invisible from where a relaunch puts the user. Identity ([D155]) and completeness had each been fixed in turn; this was the third seam of the same reconstruction, because the transcript is rebuilt at every boot by merging three streams that share no ordering spine — JSONL replay, shell-ledger restore, refs-ledger restore — so position was re-derived from clocks at read time when it had been known with certainty at write time. The clock was lying, too: the committed transcript sorts on `messages[0].createdAt`, and `handleContentBlockStart` minted that value as `Date.now` for every replayed block, so an assistant-opened turn came back dated to the relaunch — measured on the incident at four minutes and thirty-six seconds *after* its own `endedAt` — and every receipt written before that relaunch sorted ahead of a turn it actually followed. Both halves are repaired, and either alone would leave a hole. **Anchors:** `shell_exchanges` and `refs_runs` each carry a nullable `anchor_msg_id`, the newest assistant `message.id` in the session's JSONL at the moment of the write, stamped **server-side at the three ink gateways** so one authority covers every writer and the write-path wire is untouched — the rejected alternative had N decks send their last turn's id and covered only deck-initiated writes. Stamping happens *after* lineage-head resolution, so the anchor names a turn in the file the deck will replay. The tail reader skips `isSidechain` and `isMeta` entries, which is load-bearing rather than defensive: replay drops both, so an anchor naming one could never resolve — a silent permanent fallback that looks like a working anchor from the write side. **Honest time:** `content_block_start` now carries the JSONL entry's own timestamp on the replay path. That invents nothing; it finishes a seam already half-built, since the reducer *already read* `event.timestamp` on this event, `ToolUseEvent` already shipped the identical replay-stamps-it/live-omits-it contract, and `turn_complete` already resolved `event.timestamp ?? Date.now` — only the producer was missing. It is what fixes placement for legacy rows that will never have an anchor, and what keeps the fallback honest forever. **Every failure degrades placement and never drops a row** ([L23]): a null anchor, a legacy row, an unparseable transcript, or an anchor outside the loaded turn window all fall back to the timestamp insert, and on a long session that fallback is the *common* path at first paint, because ink restores whole while Claude turns load windowed — a late-arriving turn then hoists the ink anchored to it, so both orders of the boot race converge on one transcript. The project dir that locates a session's transcript is an **input** every gateway passes from what it already holds, not a lookup: the session's ledger row is written when tugcode announces the session, measured at three seconds after a card already accepts a `$` command, and a lookup in that window stamped NULL on a session whose transcript was on disk all along. Live placement is untouched — the transcript's end *is* a live row's correct position at the moment of the act, and `landingExchangeId` already gives the live copy the identity the restore replays, so a row never re-keys between them ([L26]). `shell_ledger.rs`, `refs_ledger.rs`, `session_ledger.rs` (`latest_assistant_msg_id`), `code-session-store/reducer.ts` (`insertInkAnchored`, `hoistInkAnchoredTo`), `tugcode/src/replay.ts`. [D111], [D155], [L23], [L26]

**D159.** **The docs directory's top level means *live paperwork*, and only a filter can make that true — presence there says nothing about who owns a document.** The Arcs card listed every parseable plan in the declared docs directory with an Implement button, and by the time anyone looked, every row was a lie: six plans already implemented and landed, each offering to implement them again. Two of the three causes were individually correct decisions composing badly, and the third was a misreading of this file. **The scan threw the answer away:** `plan_doc_entries_in` parses each document and keeps only `doc.steps.len`, discarding the `ledger_rows` whose status cells are the exact done/pending facts. **The review stamp deliberately excludes progress** — correct, since a plan must not go stale by being implemented — so a finished plan still reads `reviewed`, and `reviewed` mapped to Implement. **And [D139] does not say what it is usually paraphrased as saying.** Its own words are that "a committed, clean base copy is not a second live copy — it is ordinary branch divergence the squash resolves like any other file": `BasePlanState::is_dirt` is `TrackedDirty | Untracked` only, and `clean_base_plan_copy` returns `"untouched"` for `Clean`. A git log over deletions in the docs directory returned nothing — no adoption had ever deleted a plan from this repository. So the plan an arc is implementing *right now* sits there for the arc's whole life, its ledger frozen at all-`pending` because the run writes to the worktree copy, reading `reviewed · N steps` with an Implement button directly beneath the arc row that already represents it. That row was the worst of the three: an invitation to start a second arc on work in flight. **Three answers, all at the producer.** `PlanDocEntry` carries `steps_done` and `steps_begun` — two fields because a plan whose first row is `in progress` with nothing finished has begun (`0` done) and wants Resume, so a single count would conflate the fraction with the gesture; both are counted over `ledger_rows`, which is what the step verbs already trust. A document whose ledger is non-empty and wholly `done` is dropped. A document whose repo-relative path equals an arc entry's `plan_path` is dropped, matched exactly ([L29]) because a linked worktree is a full checkout and both sides spell the same path. All three live in the scan rather than in the deck, so the wire's `plans` list *is* the actionable paperwork and no reader subtracts anything from it. **And the join sweeps its own paperwork:** the landing moves the adopted plan into `<docs>/archive/`, riding the squash commit itself so the default join still lands exactly one commit, taking a follow-up commit on the merge and rebase shapes that commit atomically. `archive/` is a fixed convention inside whatever home the project declared, not a second config key — it automates what a 480-file was already doing by hand, and the scan's top-level-only walk never descends into it. It never clobbers: a destination that exists is somebody else's file, so the sweep skips and warns and the join still lands. **The general lesson is the review rubric's now:** a feature that projects repository files must trace the file's whole lifecycle, and must be verified once over real data rather than only over fixtures — `arcs_hidden_for`, added for the good reason that a fixture's subject is never the tree somebody is working in, is exactly what made that impossible here. `tugcast/src/feeds/changeset.rs` (`plan_doc_entries_in`), `changeset_all.rs`, `tugarc-core/src/ops.rs` (`archive_adopted_plan`), `arcs-section.tsx`, `arc-prompts.ts`. [D138], [D139], [D141], [L29]


**D160.** **Resolver occupancy is derived from the conflict chain, not declared anywhere — the derive-don't-declare law applied to who holds a workshop.** tugcast's occupancy registry is exact and in-process, which leaves it blind to the case that matters most: `tugtool arc join`, `tugtool arc discard`, and `tugtool arc join --resolve` run the same core from a second process, and each tears a workshop down under whatever is working in it. That was R03 in, accepted at the time because nothing durable described a resolve in flight. The conflict chain changed that, and the answer is to make it say so itself: the resolver commits an empty-delta `tugresolve(<arc>): begin` when it opens a chain-backed workshop and `tugresolve(<arc>): end` in the one wrapper every exit passes through, so the chain reads as the resolve's own operation log — root, begin, checkpoints, end — and liveness becomes a pure function of the tip. `resolve_lease` holds when a chain stands, its tip is a `tugresolve(` commit that is not the end marker, no candidate is anchored, and the tip is younger than the window; each clause answers a case the others cannot, and the chain is read **without** the validity gate for the same reason the keepalive is. **The window is the resolver's own deadline**, aliased rather than duplicated, so the ceiling a resolve dies at and the window a stranger trusts cannot drift. **No new daemon, lock file, registry, or heartbeat** — two `git log` reads and a `rev-parse`, which is why it survives any process crash. The refusal is a named blocker in the existing vocabulary, `live-resolve`, so it reaches the card with no client change, and its sentence names both ways out because it has two audiences: `--break-lease` for the CLI, resolving again for the card ([L31]). The override is consent rather than capability — the teardown is unchanged and the op-log keepalive already holds the chain, so `tugtool arc undo` restores the resolver's checkpoints either way; what the flag adds is `broke_lease` in the record and a warning naming the op that reverses it ([L23]). The in-process guard stays the fast path and suppresses the lease wherever it applies: the model is jj's op-heads lock, which exists only to avoid duplicated work and never carries correctness. `--resolve` is guarded at the CLI boundary rather than in the ladder, because the ladder clears the chain on both arms — so a check in `join` fires too late — and a check in the core would wedge the join pilot for the whole window after any resolver crash. `tugarc-core/src/resolve.rs` (`resolve_lease`, `mark_resolve_begun`, `mark_resolve_ended`), `ops.rs` (`live_resolve_detail`, `join_blockers_from_detail`), `oplog.rs` (`OpBefore.broke_lease`), `tugcast/src/feeds/join_resolver.rs`, `tugtool/src/arc.rs`. [D138], [D142], [D157], [L23], [L28], [L31]

**D161.** **A join's forward state and its reverse state are one record: the operation log carries the teardown's phase, and a join that lands nothing records nothing.** `tugtool arc join` kept two parallel files in the project state dir describing the same event — `oplog-<seq>.json`, the reverse record undo reads, and `join-journal-<name>.json`, the forward record `--continue` resumes from — written and cleared at different moments by different code, under different retention regimes, and free to disagree. The seam was already showing in three places. A conflicted join returned its outcome without calling `oplog::abandon`, so the record it opened before integrating stayed incomplete forever and `arc undo` refused by name over a join that never happened. The teardown found its own record by searching for this arc's newest incomplete join, because it received the journal and the journal carried no sequence number — a search that could land on the wrong record, and whose failure arm admitted as much in a warning. And the op log pruned at 50 while the journal lived until cleared, so a journal whose operation had been pruned was a resume record with no undo record. The fold puts the journal's four facts — phase, integrate commit, strategy, message — on the payload as `join: Option<JoinProgress>`, additive and defaulted like every payload field before it, and leaves them there after completion, where they are the receipt of how the teardown went that the journal never was. **The rule that makes an incomplete record mean something is jj's**, stated in `transaction.rs` as a transaction that is not committed writing no operation: the integrate is one function returning `Landed | Conflicted`, and every exit that leaves the base as it found it drops the record. So an incomplete join op carrying progress *is* a teardown to resume, which is what `--continue` reads and what every "is a join in flight" caller now consults through one published predicate instead of five callers stat-ing a file ([L28]). That predicate reads payload files directly and spawns no git process, because the blockers it feeds are recomputed per arc per tick and are explicitly never cached; and it tests the whole predicate all the way back rather than stopping at the newest record for the arc, since `undo_in` skips an incomplete operation and can record an undo above a join it could not reverse. **The last phase of a teardown is the branch being gone**, so `--continue` now sits above the branch-exists guard — a join killed after `branch -D` was previously refused as `Arc not found`, the one resume that most needed to run. A journal an older build left on disk folds onto the op log at the reader, lazily and idempotently, the shape `migrate_worktrees` established: it attaches to the arc's open join record or records one, prefers the journal's base branch over what `capture_before` would guess with the branch already deleted, and deletes the file only after the payload write lands ([L23]); an unreadable one is named rather than ignored. Undo's `incomplete-op` refusal and `abandon`'s deletion of both halves are untouched — they were always properties of the payload. The wire keeps its words: `stale-journal`, `join-journal`, and `join_journal_phase` name a condition the client already handles, not a file. `tugarc-core/src/oplog.rs` (`JoinPhase`, `JoinProgress`, `record_join_progress`, `join_in_flight`, `payload_seqs_desc`, `fold_legacy_join_journal`), `ops.rs` (`integrate_join`, `finish_join_teardown`), `tugtool/src/arc.rs` (`print_oplog`). Pinned by three CLI tests that SIGKILL a real join at each phase boundary through a `PATH` git shim, with no crash hooks in product code. [D138], [D157], [D160], [L23], [L28], [L31]

**D162.** **A house rule about how to read output is guidance, not a gate; when its enforcement costs a round trip more often than it saves one, the enforcement goes.** `gate-app-test-output.sh` was a PreToolUse hook that intercepted a piped `just app-test` invocation, stripped the filter where it could rewrite the line, and denied where it could not. The report contract it defended is real and unchanged — the recipe prints per-file results, `Diagnostics:`, `Failures:` with each message and locator, and a `VERDICT:` line, and a pipe still replaces the recipe's exit status with the filter's, so a green run can come back empty and non-zero. What failed was the enforcement, not the fact. The rewrite only fired when the invocation *opened* its segment, so the ordinary reflex spelling — `timeout 400 just app-test X 2>&1 | tail -40` — fell to the deny arm and cost a round trip, in session after session, for a habit no amount of denying retrained. A gate that fires on the same mistake forever is not teaching; it is a toll. So the hook, its cases, `tugplug/hooks/tests/run-gate-tests.sh`, and the `hooks-test` recipe are deleted, and the doctrine in `CLAUDE.md` and `tests/app-test/README.md` states the cost of a filter instead of forbidding one. **Do not re-propose this gate**, in any form — a warn-only variant, a rewrite-always variant, a reminder injected on the deny. The gate's applicability doctrine survives it in [D151]: an inapplicable gate must never fire, and a broken gate must never block work. `gate-file-ops.sh` is untouched — it denies commands whose *file effects the change ledger cannot read*, which is a correctness fault with no other detector, not a preference about reading. [D151]

**D163.** **A project declares the surfaces it is made of, not one command; a path no surface claims refuses the fit check before a single check runs.** `[tugtool.arc].verify` was one opaque string, and *nothing in Rust ran it* — five consumers, four of them prose, each instructing a model to fetch it from `tugtool arc config --json`, substitute `{base}`/`{head}`, and shell out. **A string cannot say what it covers.** This repository's own instance, `scripts/verify-fit.sh`, branched on two directory prefixes and exited **0** with `no built surface moved by this replay` for a diff touching anything else — the same exit code as a verified tree. Of the three arc joins on `main` before this, two moved files under directories the script did not name, and records one such report verbatim. A declaration that can be silently partial is one that will be. So `verify` is retired and a config still carrying it is a **loader refusal** naming its replacement: a project declares zero or more `[[tugtool.arc.surface]]` entries — a `name`, the repo-relative `paths` it claims, and either `check` (shell commands) or `checked_by` (another surface's commands, borrowed one level deep). `tugtool arc verify <name>` derives the arc's own contribution live from `merge-base(<base>, <branch>).<branch>` — never from a recorded range, because `replayed_note`'s hashes are abbreviated to nine characters and its tail is not the branch tip whenever a remap commit lands after the swap — resolves every touched path to the surface declaring the **longest** matching path, and **refuses on any path claimed by nothing, exit 2, before running a single check**. That refusal is the enforcement the string could never carry: a project's table is complete exactly when the verb stops refusing, and the first incomplete arc is the one that says so. Every other absence keeps a stated sentence rather than an invention, which is [D151]'s doctrine held: `check = []` is a claim reported as `claimed, unchecked` and counted apart from a checked surface; an empty table is *declares none*, exit 0, falling back to the plan's own checkpoint commands; and an **empty range is its own state** — exit 0, `nothing in range`, recording nothing, because a `verified` line for a run that checked no bytes is the same lie in a quieter voice. `{paths}` joins `{base}`/`{head}`, shell-quoted and scoped to the surface a command runs *for* — the borrower's, not the lender's, since a borrowed command runs as the borrower's own. **The result is a fact that gates nothing** ([D149] stands, and this sentence exists so it cannot be read otherwise): a green verify appends one `verified` arc log line naming the full head **and** the full base it was verified onto, collected into `ArcDeclarations.last_verified` without moving the stage exactly as `replayed` is. Both endpoints are recorded because the fit is the arc *replayed onto the live base*: a base that moved leaves the head untouched while making the verified tree no longer the tree a join would land, so a one-sha derivation would say `verified` about a tree that no longer exists. Staleness is derived at read time, never stored. The fact rides `replayed`'s whole rail — `ArcDetail` → the changeset entry → `arcMetaFacts` — saying `fit verified` or `fit unverified` and never that a join is blocked, and reaches the join receipt as an optional `fit:` line captured before teardown. That line is what forced `parseJoinReceipt` from a positional read to a **cursor**: it tested `lines[1]` for the `files:` prefix at a fixed index, so inserting any line above it would have silently orphaned the file list of every join receipt already in JSONL — and a transcript replays from its record on every card reload, so "already written" means forever. A red or refused run records **nothing**; the absence of a line is the honest record of not-verified, where a durable "red at `<head>`" would describe a state the next fix erases. `tugtool-core/src/config.rs` (`Surface`, `ConfigRefusal`, `ArcConfig::validate`), `tugarc-core/src/surfaces.rs` (`plan_verification`, `verify_in`), `arc.rs` (`FitFact`), `ops.rs`, `tugcast/src/feeds/changeset.rs` (`format_join_summary`), `base_motion.rs`, `session-join-receipt-block.tsx`, `arc-meta-line.tsx`, `.tugtool/config.toml`. Supersedes [D151]'s `verify` key, leaving `post_create`, `build`, `docs`, and the stage-model keys exactly where they are. [D149], [D151]

**D166.** **The content cards are laid out first; the rails take what is left — and where they cannot leave enough, the lap is drawn as a lap.** The space allocator scored a candidate rail total on a lexicographic key and then wrapped a **tier gate** around the search: it ran the comfort domain `[Σ comfortFloor, Σ ceiling]` first and descended below the comfort floors if and only if some total down there reached a strictly better CLASS of picture — clean over cramped, cramped over occluded. The reasoning was that comfort should be spent to *fix* a picture and never merely to *improve* one, since cramping a rail on a chain no total can repair leaves the user a picture they still see is wrong at the cost of a rail they can no longer read. Lived with, that is backwards: on a deck too small for its cards the gate held every rail at its measure and let the cards lap further over one another than they had to. Three slim cards on a 2560px canvas lapped 128px a seam under the gate and 102px without it, and the 52px the gate was protecting were 52px of the user's own cards. **The cards are the subject; the rails are the frame.** So the gate is deleted and comfort becomes a TERM in the same key — `[worstOverlap, worstShortfall, discomfort, worstError, distance]` in fit, `[hairline, discomfort, distance]` in flow, where `discomfort` is how far under `Σ comfortFloor` a candidate stands and is zero at or above it. The picture terms come first, so the arrangement is settled before anything the rails prefer is read at all; comfort is then spent by the pixel, exactly as far as the picture is bought by spending it. Its two neighbours are both load-bearing: **below `worstShortfall`**, because the total that tiles a three-up deck of slim cards can land a handful of pixels under the comfort floors and a comfort-first rule would paint every interior seam at 2px instead of 5 to save width nobody was reading; **above `worstError`**, because raggedness is a matter of degree on a chain that already reads as arranged, and a rail must not be cramped to shave a pixel off it. A deck that reads well still does not move — every picture term has a rest state at zero, so the last term decides and the rails stay where their owner put them. **The search becomes exhaustive in the same stroke, and that is a correctness fix rather than a simplification.** The scan was a 16px stride with a 1px rescan around the coarse winner, seeded with the closed-form least-squares fit and, in flow, with the totals that put the band on a slot edge or on the sliver threshold — every one of those an apology for the stride. `imposeRect` clamps a pane's travel at zero, so a chain of mixed card widths has a picture full of narrow valleys the stride steps over: on a four-up deck of alternating slim and wide cards it settled on the range's low end at 816px of lap while a total 268px away sat at 814px and cost the rails no comfort at all. A rail stands between its hard floor and the shared slim ceiling, so the range is at most a thousand integers and one solve measures 0.1ms at its worst — the stride was buying nothing. `TOTAL_SCAN_STRIDE_PX` and `flowSeedTotals` are gone; `solveSidebarWidths` survives as the honest closed form and no longer seeds anything. **What the allocator cannot fix, the drawing must state.** A rail's hard floor is inviolable, so a canvas narrower than `Σ card + Σ floor` laps whatever the key says — and in fit a lap eats a card's TRAILING end, leaving only a strip at its leading edge. The Layout card's plan drew those strips in one fill separated by hairlines of the ground, which reads as a picket fence rather than as cards behind cards, so **fit's blocks now alternate**: odd slots are drawn a step quieter, `color-mix`ed between the card tone and the ground the drawing already uses so the second tone can only ever land between them and cannot drift into looking like a distinction the reader should be reading. Fit only — flow lays a running sum with a real gap between every pair, so nothing laps and banding there would be a pattern over a picture with no ambiguity in it. **And the arrangement marks move to the leading edge in fit**, off the block's centre, because a centred mark on a lapped card lands under the next card: every mark but the last buried, and — since a mark is a control — a target the pointer cannot always reach. The leading edge is the one part of a lapped card that is visible by geometry rather than by luck. The rails keep the centre; nothing ever laps a rail. `tugdeck/src/lib/layout-imposer.ts` (`scoreRailTotal`'s key, `chooseRailTotal`'s single domain and exhaustive sweep, the deleted stride and seeds), `components/layout/layout-miniature.tsx` + `.css` (`data-band`, the fit-only banding), `components/layout/layout-places.tsx` + `.css` (`data-layout`, the leading-edge mark). Comfort may be spent to REDUCE an unremovable overlap, and invariant 3 is restated as *bought a better picture* with its converse: comfort spent no further than the picture pays for. Supersedes the comfort-tier rule and the coarse-to-fine scan of [D136]. [D121], [D128], [D136], [L06], [L09]

**D167.** **A line owns the callsign and the user's name; a `sessions` row is a segment of one and carries no identity at all.** Identity used to be copied: minted onto a row, then transferred by whichever write path knew it should — `inherit_fork_identity` on a rewind, a repair migration when a rotation moved it wrongly, a read-time resolver when the picker showed four rows where the user had one conversation, an adoption sweep when the receipts stayed behind ([D155]). Every one of those was a correct fix to a symptom of the same cause, and each added a path that could forget. The cause is that a card's claude session id changes — per arc stage, per rewind, per crash respawn — while the conversation does not, so anything stored on the row is stored on the thing that moves. **So identity moves off the row.** A `lines` table owns `tag`, `name`, `name_user_set` and `card_id`; `sessions` gains `line_id` and loses all three; and `SessionRow` keeps its identity fields as a **projection** — a `LEFT JOIN lines` in every query — so the forty-odd readers that already asked a row for its callsign went line-correct without changing. There is nothing to inherit, strand, displace, or climb for, and the functions that did those things are gone rather than rewritten: `inherit_fork_identity`, `resolve_to_line_root`, `line_identity`, `get_for_display`, `resolve_to_lineage_head`, `rekey_session`, `reconcile_orphaned_rows`, `ink_adoption`, and three repair migrations. **A line is born in exactly one place** — a card's first spawn (the deck mints the uuid from the drop, so the optimistic callsign and the ledger's line name the same thing), a plain `/new`, or an external scan, which births a card-less line because a spelling spent in `minted_tags` needs an owner that exists ([D123]). **Every other id change joins the card's line by reference**: tugcode announces each one with a single `session_segment` frame carrying `kind` and the parent id, and the bridge records the new segment against `entry.line_id` — read from the supervisor's own entry, never looked up, because a lookup is a place to be wrong. A caller that knows the line names it and wins over anything the scanner guessed from a file on disk; an empty `line_id` is a caller saying it has none, which is what keeps adoption seating the line the picker has been showing. **A restore seats the line's resume segment**: live first, then a tip nothing forked from, then newest, with failed segments excluded — the picker offers lines rather than transcripts, and turns and bytes sum across the line because that is what the line holds. **The trailer names the line and pins the segment**: `Tug-Session: <callsign> (<line shortid8>)` beside `Tug-Session-Id: <segment uuid>`, so every commit a card ever makes cites one conversation while still opening the exact transcript it was made from. **Ink is keyed by the line** ([D155]), and **a rename is a unique write**: a partial unique index over user-set names, and a name another line wears is taken from it by the newer `/rename`, the wire reporting the displacement in the ack (**amended by [D170]**, which restored the displacement rule this decision had replaced with a refusal). **One transactional migration** assigns every existing row: it unions both edge kinds — rewind and rotation — into components, and each component becomes a line wearing its oldest minted spelling and its newest user-set name, with a `sessions.db.pre-lines` copy written beside the ledger before the transaction opens. It was dry-run twice against copies of a live ledger before it shipped, and a ledger that cannot migrate is not served. `tugrust/crates/tugcast/src/session_ledger.rs` (`LineRow`, `birth_line`, `ensure_scan_line`, `resume_segment_for_line`, `list_lines_with_card`, `line_of`, `rename`, `migrate_sessions_to_lines`), `shell_ledger.rs`, `refs_ledger.rs`, `ink_backfill.rs`, `feeds/agent_bridge.rs` (`parse_session_segment`), `feeds/agent_supervisor.rs`, `tugcode/src/session.ts` (`session_segment`), `tugdeck/src/lib/session-line-store.ts` and the identity stores it keys. [D111], [D123], [D132], [D141], [D155], [L23], [L29], [L31]

**D168.** **One grammar, two registers: the track where the arc is the subject, the mark where it is one fact about a session.** **Amended 2026-08-27 — the eyebrow says WHO, the line says WHAT.** The block's first line is the identities alone: the arc atom, the hairline, the workers. Every reading of the arc's STATE is on the second line, in one order at both registers — the track, the phase glyph, the count, the word: `[track] [glyph] [i/N] [phase]`. The glyph led the arc's NAME for a while, which put half the state on the line whose subject is the identity; the compact mark takes the same order one scale down (`pill · glyph · fraction`), the pill leading because it is the mark that touches the identity run it follows. Three things travel with it. The **step's title is no longer a run**: during implement it rode the note, a sentence in a slot sized for a word, eliding mid-word on every host but the placard and, on the placard, restating the row its own list already lights — it is the fraction's hover sentence now (`stepTitle` on the line, never text). The **Arcs card's rows carry no `⋯`**: Bind / Discard / Replay answer the row's right-click, the same items from the same `useArcRowMenu`, so the eyebrow's right end is the worker's atom and nothing else — a sentence [D176] and [D178] spent and [D200] restores, by seating the card's controls on the line beneath. And the **Z2 dots read the arc, not the git stage** — an arc devising or reviewing has no stage, so `arcCellPose` showed two idle dots beside `Devise` on a session that was plainly working, the same blindness the glyph was moved off the stage to cure. Two alignment rules fell out of the same look: the line's type runs share a **baseline** while its two graphics stay centred — which lands the cap-height strip on the type's cap band — and the phase glyph is sized one pixel proud of that band at each scale rather than four (`11` at read, `9` at rail), so it reads as the strip's neighbour instead of as a taller mark beside it; and a Z2 cell's width budget is each of its two rows' `min-width` with the rows stretched to the cell, so a reading wider than its budget — `None` between two dots in the 10ch JOBS wears while an arc is up — takes the label rule with it instead of spilling out from under it. The lifecycle went out to six surfaces at once as a single strip, and on two of them it did not fit — it grew with the plan, and on a row leading with an eliding name it grew into whatever was beside it. So the strip belongs to the three surfaces whose subject IS the arc: the Arcs card, the Changes shade's arc lane, and the ARC placard, all of them `TugArcTrack` inside `ArcLifecycleLine` / `ArcLifecycleBlock`. The two where a SESSION is the subject — the session card's masthead title run and the Cards card's session rows, both `SessionIdentityRow` — take `ArcLifecycleMark` instead: one pill, the phase glyph, and `TugStepFraction`. The pill wears the track's own palette and breathes on the track's own cycle, so the two registers read as one grammar rather than as two marks. **The track is constant width:** its implement cell is three cells wide at every plan length and its ticks divide it, so the strip is the same graphic at three steps and at twenty-four. **The phase glyph keys on the lifecycle phase** (`arcTrackModel`'s six phases), never on the git stage — an arc has no stage at all until `arc create` cuts its branch, so a stage-keyed mark was blank for the documented half of every arc's life. **The Z2 ARC cell is an instrument, not a graphic.** The whole track lived in it for a while, retuned by four knob overrides to survive a 78px box, and drew ticks a pixel wide — a graphic too small to read at the size it was drawn. The cell now wears STATE's construction exactly: a dot pinned to each edge of the value wrap and the reading centred between them, three siblings rather than one indicator carrying a label. It reads NUMBERS whenever there are numbers — the declared run, else the plan's own pair, so a reviewed plan reads `0/10` rather than falling back to a word for an arc that plainly has steps to count — the lifecycle phase in Title Case only for an arc with no plan at all, `Cut` for a cut, and `Stopped` for a stopped arc, which outranks every other reading and turns both dots. Because it wears STATE's construction it takes STATE's 18ch while an arc is up, and JOBS gives back exactly that, so the row's 80ch never moves and every `@container` rung keeps the value it was measured at. **The binding is read once, by the row that holds it** (`SessionIdentityRow`'s `arc`, threaded into `TugSessionIdentity`'s `arc`), and never re-read beneath it: the marker under the row was making literally the same `useArcForSession` call a beat later, which is two subscriptions free to disagree about one fact. `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/tug-arc-track.tsx`, `arc-phase-mark.tsx`, `arc-lifecycle-mark.tsx`, `arc-lifecycle-line.tsx`, `arc-lifecycle-block.tsx`, `tug-arc-atom.tsx`, `tug-step-fraction.tsx`, `session-identity-row.tsx`, `tug-session-identity.tsx`, `cards/session-card-telemetry-renderers.tsx`, `tug-status-cell.css`, `cards/gallery-arc-lifecycle.tsx` (the components' documented home). Amends [D141], [D143], [D148]. [D138], [D147], [L02], [L06], [L19], [L20] **Amended 2026-09-04 — the line is a sentence, and the order is `[track] [glyph] <Doing> [i/N] · <what is in the way>`.** The reading was three mechanisms appended in source order — a phase word, a fraction, and however many divergence facts applied — which produced `⚒ 3/6 implement base overlap (1) planned`: four things a reader had to rank for themselves, in a register nothing agreed on. The word now leads the numbers and is a **verb in the reader's tense** (`Implementing`, `Awaiting review`, `Finished`), so the fraction that follows reads as the verb's object and drops entirely when no step is in hand; only the **loudest** trouble clause is painted, with every applicable sentence stacked into its hover; and one derivation, `arcReading(model)`, writes the word and the fraction for every face — the mark's tooltip (`<arc> · Implementing step 3 of 6`, and `Stopped in review · needs a decision`, which names the phase its glyph gave up), the Z2 cell's 18ch word (`Awaiting review` · `Stopped` · `Cut`), and the receipt row's note (`Finished · 3 stages` · `Picked back up` · `Stopped · <why>`). A stop's **sentence** now rides the wire as `stopped_why` from `ArcStopReason::sentence`, so no face keeps a second table of a closed vocabulary, and the glyph loses its tooltip, keeping its `aria-label`: the word is beside it now, and a bubble repeating it is the second copy this decision's own rule forbids. `arcLifecycleNote`, `arcLifecycleFacts`, `NOT_ON_THE_LINE`, the hosts' `note` prop (the receipt block excepted) and `ARC_PHASE_LABELS` are retired rather than filtered. The tables are in `arc-lifecycle.md`'s faces section. **Amended 2026-09-24 — every Z2 cell is sized to its widest possible reading, and the implement phase reads `Executing`.** The verb table above outgrew the cell it was measured for: `Implementing` is twelve letters against the nine the ARC cell's box was sized on, and a reading wider than a cell's budget widens the cell, so the row moved every time a planned arc walked a step. Two rules replace the tuning note that had stood for one. **Every Z2 cell is sized to its widest possible reading, in its widest face, and the reading set is closed** — no live value may ever change a cell's rendered box. The widest word is **declared beside the table that produces it, never measured into a CSS number by hand**, and it is the widest word the face RENDERS rather than the longest one — `Reviewing` and `Executing` are both nine letters and `Reviewing` is 2.69px the wider, so a box declared from the second grew every time the first was live: `SESSION_PHASE_WIDEST_LABEL` beside `SESSION_PHASE_LABELS`, `ARC_CELL_WIDEST_WORD` and `ARC_CELL_WIDEST_FRACTION` beside the arc reading tables, `WORK_CELL_WIDEST_WORD` beside `formatCellCount`; the STATE and ARC value spans carry them as `data-widest` / `data-widest-alt`, and `tug-status-cell.css` renders each as a zero-height hidden pseudo-element face under the live text, so the span is the widest reading's width from the first paint and the reading swaps inside it. The `ch` budgets stay as the floor the `@container` rungs are measured against; the declaration is what guards them. Pseudo-elements rather than ghost spans, because the ARC value's `textContent` is what every app-test reads. **The implement phase reads `Executing` everywhere the phase has a word** — nine letters like `Reviewing`, the sense of running a plan that already exists, and clean inflection: `Executing the plan`, `Executed`, `Not yet executed`, `Executing · 3 of 6 steps closed`. The phase key, the skill names, the git stage and the arc verbs stay `implement`. The two resting clauses get a cell-only short form — `Review` and `Audit` for `Awaiting review` and `Awaiting audit` — the line keeping the clause; these and `Stopped`, `Cut`, `Ready` are the cell's only departures from the line's table, so no third vocabulary exists for the cell to disagree with. Two tests enforce the rule at each level: a unit test iterates every reading a cell can produce, the fraction shapes to `99/99` included, against its declaration in characters — the cheap half, which catches a word that is simply longer — and `at0484-arc-z2-instrument` walks a bound session through the cell's readings, both nine-letter words among them, asserting the five rendered boxes are identical at each, which is the half that can see pixels. The measurement behind it: the STATE face `Disconnected` needs 124.6px of wrap against 18ch's 108px, and `Reviewing` between two dots needs 114.4px against 17ch's 102px — both overflowed at base whenever the word was live, and the older comment's `17ch holds 113` had counted the cell's 12px of anchor padding that layout cancels. Sized to those faces, the full five-cell row at the slim preset needs 616.6px of a 617px content box and the bound row 596px, so no budget or rung moves. `session-phase-visual.ts`, `select-work.ts`, `tug-arc-track.tsx`, `tug-status-cell.css`, `cards/session-card-telemetry-renderers.tsx`, `cards/gallery-arc-lifecycle.tsx`.

**D169.** **The arc audits what it implemented, and a run still checking its work does not wear the join.** Two faults with one cause: nothing read the landed code except the session that wrote it, and nothing on any face could tell "the last commit landed" from "the run is finished." **The arc gains a fourth stage.** `ArcStage::Audit` follows implement — a fresh session opening on the plan and the branch's whole diff, judging the code against what the plan promised, fixing what does not match as ordinary rounds, and marking the arc `audited`. It is a stage rather than the implement run's last step for exactly the reason the review is one: a session cannot be surprised by code it remembers writing, so the reader worth having is the one that has never seen it. The **mark is the stage's product**, exactly as the stamp is the review's, and the predicate reads the arc's own `audited` declaration rather than anything the stage said about itself — a stage announces nothing and is believed about nothing. An audit that ends unmarked stops the arc as `audit did not mark`, with **no second round**: the review's cap buys something because an unstamped plan is still walkable, while an unmarked audit leaves committed code and a finished run, so a retry would re-read the same bytes to reach the same silence. The direct `/arc` runs the same four questions in-thread at its own ending and marks the same way — cheaper, and honestly weaker, which its skill says out loud. **And `/arc-plan` writes the plan rather than a brief.** The route settled an idea into a brief so a devise session could turn it into a plan, which paid twice for one settling and paid the second time on a session that had lost the first one's context; the conversation that produced the idea is richer than any brief summarizing it. So the plan is authored in that conversation, on the user's own model, linted clean, and handed over — `tugtool plan lint`'s positive `{#execution-steps}` detection is the switch, so the arc opens at **review** on a plan and still at devise on a brief, which keeps every older arc working. What the route gives up is a devising pass; what it keeps is the cold review, which was always the load-bearing half. **The track gains a `check` cell between implement and join**, drawn on both routes. `holders_busy` and the git stage were already side by side on the wire and deliberately not folded into each other — the stage says the last round is committed on a clean worktree, which is all `ready` ever meant, and `holders_busy` says whether the person who built it has stopped. The strip is what reconciles them: a joinable arc whose holder is mid-turn or waiting on a job it launched reads `check`, and so does a walked ledger whose stage has not caught up, and so does the arc's audit stage. One more arm covers the seam neither fact can: **an arc still rotating has not arrived**, because between implement's last step and the audit being seated nobody is busy and every git fact reads joinable. `tugarc-core/src/arc.rs` (`ArcStage::Audit`, `ArcStopReason::AuditDidNotMark`, `stage_model`), `tugtool-core/src/config.rs` (`audit_model`), `tugcast/src/feeds/arc.rs` (`audit_declared`, `audit_action`), `arc_runner.rs`, `tugcast/src/wheel/prompt.rs`, `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/tug-arc-track.tsx`, `arc-phase-mark.tsx`, `tugplug/skills/arc-audit/`, `arc-plan/`, `arc/`. Amends [D168] (six phases, not five), [D147], [D149]. [D138], [D152], [L06], [L19], [L31] **Amended:** the `check` cell is the **`audit`** cell — key, label, `data-phase`, and every pin — because a strip that said Audit beside a note that said `check` was one stage wearing two words; a stopped audit never arms the join and reads `unaudited` on the shade; and the audit is the author of record for the join message. The evidence and the decisions are in the `arc-audit-face` arc's brief, and the law is in `arc-lifecycle.md`'s joining section.

**D170.** **A `/rename` always takes the name: the newest naming gesture wins, and the line that wore it falls back to its callsign.** [D167] made a user-set name unique across lines and enforced it by **refusing** a spelling somebody already wore — `name_taken`, the holder's callsign in the bulletin. Lived with, that refusal is the wrong half of the trade. A rename is the user saying what the conversation in front of them is called; answering it with the callsign of some other session — very often one of their own, from last week, that they have no intention of keeping named — makes the user go find that session and un-name it before they may name this one. The uniqueness invariant was never the thing worth protecting at the gesture's expense: it is worth protecting at the *write*, and the write can satisfy it by taking the name rather than by declining to. **So the displacement is back, and it is announced.** `SessionLedger::rename` clears the previous holder's `name` and `name_user_set` in the same immediate transaction that writes the new one, so the partial `UNIQUE` index over user-set names still holds at every commit, and it returns the `DisplacedName` rows it cleared. **The target's existence is proved before anything is displaced** — a rename addressed at an unknown line is `NotFound` and writes nothing, where the refusing version found the holder first and could not tell the two failures apart. `LedgerError::NameTaken` is deleted rather than left unproduced. **What made the old displacement wrong was silence, not displacement** ([D167] called it "silently, at a distance"), so every displaced line is un-taught exactly the way the renamed one is taught: a `session_renamed` fact on its own conversation recording the loss, and a `session_updated` push so its chip falls back to its callsign live. The `rename_session_ok` ack carries a `displaced` list of `{line_id, tag}`; the deck drops those names from `sessionNameStore` on the ack rather than waiting for the push, and the rename bulletin reads *The name was taken from `stocky-pixie`.* beneath its success line. There is no `rename_session_err` reason for a taken name any more, and `NameSettle.holderTag` becomes `NameSettle.displaced`. **The identity rule of [D141] is untouched and its premise still holds**: a custom name is still unique across lines at the write, so a custom-named title is still the name alone with no callsign to disambiguate — the uniqueness is now maintained by the newer gesture winning rather than by the older one holding. `tugrust/crates/tugcast/src/session_ledger.rs` (`DisplacedName`, `rename`, the `lines_user_name` index comment), `feeds/agent_supervisor.rs` (`do_rename_session` — the per-holder fact + push and the ack's `displaced`), `tugdeck/src/protocol.ts` (`encodeRenameSession`), `lib/session-name-store.ts` (`NameSettle.displaced`, `renameRefusalDetail`), `action-dispatch.ts` (`rename_session_ok`), `components/tugways/cards/rename-session-sheet.tsx` (the bulletin), `lib/session-identity.ts` (the collision note). Amends [D167] (the refusal it replaced) and the premise sentence of [D141]. [D123], [D132], [D141], [D167], [L31]

**D172.** **⌃⌘⟨letter⟩ names a sidebar card, one letter per card, and a menu-promoted chord takes the key from any scoped binding that held it.** Every sidebar card has a Window row, and each row carries the chord that runs it: **⌃⌘A** Arcs, **⌃⌘W** Cards, **⌃⌘J** Jots, **⌃⌘L** Layout, **⌃⌘O** Overview. Four are initials; Cards takes W against its coming rename to *Workspaces*, because C is the Changes shade. The set is complete rather than open-ended — `layoutRole === "sidebar"` registration is a boot step, so five is what the deck has — and it stands beside the ⌃⌘←/→ rail pair without competing with it, because **the pair addresses a SIDE of the deck and a letter addresses a CARD**. The pair's closure is structural (two edges) where the letters' is contingent (five cards), so a sixth sidebar card is the letter family's to answer for. **The chords need no new mechanism and get none:** each `.show` entry already carried the `toggle-<card>` id, which routes to `toggleSidebarCard`'s three-rung ladder, so a chord reaching that id runs show-and-activate / activate / hide by construction and cannot disagree with the mark and dynamic title the row draws. They are `menuEligible` with **empty** Swift key equivalents, so `applyCommandChords` writes them — recursing into each card's submenu to reach the toggle — and every one stays rebindable. **The displacement rule is the durable half.** A `menuEligible` chord becomes an `NSMenuItem` key equivalent and AppKit resolves those before the web view sees the keydown, so a promoted chord leaves any responder-scoped binding on the same keys reaching nothing — *even inside the surface that scoped it*. There is no sharing to arrange: the scoped binding moves or it dies silently. So Claim All takes **⌃⌥⌘A** and Disclaim All **⌃⌥⌘D**, on the advanced tier ⌃⌥⌘P debuted, Disclaim taking a letter of its own rather than a ⇧-counterpart because the two were never a set-inverse ([D126]) and D says *Disclaim* with one fewer modifier. ⌃⌥⌘D is distinct from the reserved ⌃⌘D — the system dictionary is the ⌥-less chord. Next Theme sits on **⇧⌘T**, which [D126]'s own reasoning permits (no ⌘T base to be a variant of) and which is free only because Tug sets `allowsAutomaticWindowTabbing = false`. **Two orderings and one glyph settle alongside.** The Window group is alphabetical **by the noun it displays**, not by component id — the Arcs card's id is `dashes` ([D141]), so sorting by id would put Cards first while reading as alphabetical — and the same order is spelled in `SIDEBAR_MENU_CARDS` and in the Swift construction loop. The three-state mark's mixed glyph is an empty **`square`** at the menu font's own point size, `.regular` weight — a mark in the same column as the plain check, at the same footprint, not competing with it for ink. Not `checkmark.square`, whose inset check draws *smaller* than the plain check and so shrinks the mark on the emphatic rung; not a filled shape, which reads as a blob beside a check. The diamond stays rejected; a square is adjacent to AppKit's own minimized-window diamond in weight while distinct in shape. `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/command-registry.ts` (`SIDEBAR_MENU_CARDS`, `SIDEBAR_CARD_COMMANDS`, `next-theme`, `claim-all-changes`, `disclaim-all-changes`), `tugapp/Sources/AppDelegate.swift` (`mixedStateGlyph`, the Window group's order, the `view.nextTheme` construction literal), `tuglaws/chord-tiers.md`, `tuglaws/menus.md` (generated). The menu-eligible collision lint holds it in both directions, each toggle carries a swept key equivalent, the chord walks all three rungs, and ⌃⌘O lands as a real keystroke. Amends [D126], which placed the three chords it re-homes. [D130], [D131], [D141], [L30]

**D173.** **The Changes shade lists this card's arc and the unbound arcs; an arc another live session holds is the Arcs card's row and that card's shade, never a row here.** The shade's arc lane was widened to every arc in the project on the argument that a count is a rumor and a row is a situation you can act on. That argument is right about an *unbound* arc and wrong about a *held* one, and the two were being treated as one bucket. On a held arc there is nothing to act on from this card: Discard is withheld by the reach rule, a Join or Resolve lands work this card never touched, Bind would co-bind onto a session mid-run, and Replay has its own reason-labelled door. Meanwhile [D153] already gives that arc a room — the Arcs card row routes to the card working it and reveals *that* shade — so the row here was a second, weaker face for a room one click away, under a label ("also on this project") that said nothing about why the reader was looking at it. An unbound arc is the opposite case: `bound_session` is computed from live sessions only, so an arc whose builder closed reads unbound — the arc analogue of the shade's orphaned file bucket — and every act on its row is real and this card's to take (adopt it, discard it, land it if it is ready with nobody to offer it). So the lane keeps the fronted row — fronted whatever holds it, because fronting is about what is being *landed* and `/arc-join <name>` may aim at an arc the card never bound — and a rest group that is exactly the arcs no other live session holds, labelled `unbound — no session is working these` in the sibling buckets' grammar, with no count because the rows are the count, and ordered nearest-to-done first by the same comparator the Arcs card uses (`lib/arc-order.ts`, one implementation for both surfaces). The predicate is one fact already on the entry, read the way `canDiscardFromHere` reads it: present and not this card's session, with an absent field reading as unbound ([D177] made it a scalar). **The Arcs card is untouched**: it is the fixed-address, every-arc, every-project surface by design, and "every arc is a row" stays accepted there and rejected for the shade, whose subject is this card's work. `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/cards/session-changes/session-changes-arc-lane.tsx` (`orderArcLane`), `changes-section-labels.ts` (`ARC_UNBOUND_LABEL`), `tugdeck/src/lib/arc-order.ts`. A held arc renders no row in the shade and no rest label, while the Arcs card still lists it. Amends the lane's own widening. [D120], [D153], [P02]

**D174.** **A commit wears the identity pill everywhere it is named, and the pill's face is the one thing it does not borrow.** A commit sha was drawn by the generic read-only atom skin — glyph, label, resting underline — which is the skin a `file_path` in a tool header wears, so a transcript paragraph naming one arc, one file and four commits came out as six undifferentiated runs and the reader had to read each label to learn what kind of thing it was.
