A spike drew four looks against the five surfaces that name a commit (transcript prose, the History shade, the Overview's refs row, the `/commit` and join receipts, the composer's baked chip) and the **Node pill** settled it: a commit is a named object like the arc and the session standing beside it, so it wears their box. **Rail** (an underlined run with a leading tick), **Slab** (a filled block), and **Ink** (bare recoloured characters) are retired, and they are recorded here so they are not re-proposed — each failed the same way, by being legible in isolation and invisible in a paragraph carrying two other kinds of citation. **The enclosure is borrowed, not authored.** `TugCommitAtom` sets no box, corner, border, padding or state colour of its own: it wears `tug-session-identity` at `data-tier="chip"` exactly as `ArcSigil`'s atom does, reached by the same selectors, so `data-interactive`'s hover and `data-missing`'s dashed shape arrive for free and retuning the skin retunes all three pills at once — siblings by construction rather than three sets of numbers kept equal by hand. The mark is a **static ring** at the register's own dot diameter: a session's dot carries a phase and pulses, a commit has no phase and never will, so the pill publishes no `atomPillMarkVars` because that cap exists to bound a pulse's reach and there is no pulse to bound. **The face is the surface's, and that scopes the identity pill's own rule rather than contradicting it.** The pill pins sans because a *name* has a face and there is no mono rendition of a word; a hash is not a name in that sense, so the commit mount reverses that single declaration and inherits — proportional in a transcript paragraph, an Overview post and a receipt header, monospace in the History shade's mono rows. `tabular-nums` on the hash is what survives, and it earns more in proportional type than it ever did in mono. In the composer the pill is a baked chip and takes the chip convention as it stands, so a commit reads the same before and after it is sent, and the `GitCommitHorizontal` glyph retires from the chip vocabulary along with the skin that carried it. **The baseline is the label's, by the strut** — the borrowed skin's zero-width `::before` in the pill's own face, centred with the label — and no `vertical-align` goes with it on the atom or on any host, because a length there is a correction layered over a lie that drifts the moment the register or the face moves. Two consequences follow and both are part of the decision. The rollover moves **onto the atom**: `commit-sha-text.css`'s hover underline retires, since a pill does not take a text underline and the border's 30% → 45% firm is what every identity pill already does, authored once rather than at each of five hosts. And the commit becomes a **real atom on the clipboard and the wire** — `atomSegmentFor` and `payloadForAtom` gain a `commit-sha` arm carrying the full sha as its value with `commit:<8>` as its label and its `text/plain` flavor, so a copied sha pastes back as a live pill instead of as text. `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/tug-commit-atom.tsx`/`.css`, `commit-sha-text.tsx`/`.css`, `components/overview/overview-card.tsx` (the refs row), `components/tugways/commit-tip-portals.tsx` (the prose portal), `lib/annotator/atom-segment.ts`, `lib/tug-atom-img.ts` (the pill painter), `lib/commit-format.ts` (`commitAtomLabel`, now the one home), and `tug-atom-ref.tsx`, whose `commit` arm is deleted rather than left as a second way to draw one. `tuglaws/entity-presentation.md` carries the doctrine. [D132], [D141], [L06], [L15], [L19], [L20]

**D175.** **A promoted chord displaces whatever was under it, and when the collision is a card's letter against a verb's mnemonic cluster, the card moves.** [D172] gave every sidebar card a ⌃⌘⟨letter⟩ and, finding Arcs' initial already under the Changes shade's Claim All, moved *the verbs* — to ⌃⌥⌘A and ⌃⌥⌘D — on the reasoning that a deck-level verb is not a surface's to decline. The displacement mechanic is right and stands: a `menuEligible` chord becomes an `NSMenuItem` key equivalent, AppKit resolves those before the web view sees the keydown, and a responder-scoped binding beneath one reaches nothing even inside the surface that scoped it. **What was wrong was which side paid.** A card's letter is the most arbitrary claim on the tier — one letter per card, and nothing about *Arcs* demands A over R — while ⌃⌘A was carrying a derived cluster: ⌃⌘C the shade, ⌃⌘M the message, ⌃⌘A the claim, the neighbourhood [D126] built and then had to break. So **Arcs takes ⌃⌘R** — the noun's own second letter, unbound on the tier, and permitted over ⌘R Reveal Stack because the ⌃⌘ band is read *by tier* rather than as a variant of its plain-⌘ base, exactly as ⌃⌘C sits over ⌘C Copy — and **Claim All returns to ⌃⌘A**, its scoped binding live again once nothing is promoted onto those keys. Two of the five card letters are now non-initials, each derived: Cards' W against its coming rename, Arcs' R against this cluster. **Disclaim All takes ⌃⇧⌘A, and the reason it is not ⌃⌘D is the scope.** ⌃⌘D is macOS Look Up, on the never-bind list in `tuglaws/chord-tiers.md`, and Look Up is live *inside a text field* — which is precisely where this binding is scoped, the composer holding focus under the passive Changes shade. A responder binding there would contest the system in the one place it is least likely to win, so the ⇧-counterpart carries it: same key as its pair, opposite sense. The ⇧ still means "the opposite bulk verb of this shade" and not set inversion — Claim acts on what is not yet this session's, Disclaim on what is — the distinction [D126] named and the limit R1 stops at. This vacates ⌃⌥⌘A and ⌃⌥⌘D, leaving Cycle Permission Mode ⌃⌥⌘P the advanced tier's sole resident again, and gives the ⌃⇧⌘ counterpart tier its first. `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/command-registry.ts` (`SIDEBAR_MENU_CARDS`, `claim-all-changes`, `disclaim-all-changes`), `tugdeck/src/sidebar-toggle.ts`, `tuglaws/chord-tiers.md`, `tuglaws/menus.md` (generated). No Swift change: every sidebar toggle carries an **empty** key equivalent and `applyCommandChords` writes the chord from the table, so the menu follows the registry without being told. Pinned by the menu-eligible collision lint, the routing-drift check, and the generated menus document. Amends [D172], which displaced the two verbs this entry returns; restores [D126]'s placement of both. [D130], [D141], [L30]

**D176.** **An arc row on the Arcs card is the `ARC` placard's reading, folded — one component draws the ledger on both, the expansion bit belongs to the card, and a row click still means only what it meant.** The card held every arc's step *count* and none of its step *titles*, though the titles were already on the wire: `ArcChangesetEntry.steps` is read off the same plan parse the fraction comes from, and the row's own track was already drawing one tick per ledger row from it. So the row was withholding something it had. **The fold is the tool-call header's fold**, `BlockFoldCue` at `subtype="icon"` / `size="xs"` with the default scroll stabilization, mounted in the `trailing` slot `ArcLifecycleBlock` had reserved for exactly this and which the Arcs card is the first surface to fill — a leading disclosure triangle was rejected because it shifts every atom right by a glyph, including on rows that will never have steps, and fights the eyebrow's "who" reading. **The ledger it opens to is `ArcStepItems`, and the placard keeps no private copy**: the component moved out of `session-card-telemetry-popovers.tsx` to `components/tugways/arc-step-list.tsx` + `.css` carrying the three rules that make it read as prose — the muted tabular ordinal, the finished row's strikethrough, and the 22px lead column and sans face — onto the row itself, so the list carries its reading wherever it mounts, and its slot is `arc-step` rather than the popover's spelling since it no longer belongs to one. Two surfaces drawing one ledger from one component cannot disagree about it; two components drawing it from one wire field can, and would, the first time either was tuned. **The expansion bit is the card's, keyed by the arc's owner key** — a `ReadonlySet<string>` and a `toggle` held in `ArcsBody` and handed through `CockpitRowsDataSource` to the cell, following `tug-changes-list`'s host-controlled per-file collapse. A per-cell `useState` — the History row's pattern — loses the bit when a cell is recycled by virtualization or when a changeset beat recomputes the snapshot and replaces the row, and on a rail card that re-projects on every beat that is a row folding itself shut while you read it. Card-local is the right lifetime: closing the card forgets every fold. **Expansion is structure rather than appearance**, so the steps mount while open and unmount when folded and never sit hidden under an attribute — [L06] still governs the cue's and the row's *paint*, and a mounted ledger on every arc in every project that never shows one is not a rendering detail. **A row with no steps draws no cue at all — absent, not disabled**: an arc with a brief alone, or one still being devised, has nothing to fold, and a disabled chevron there is a promise about a future the row does not know it has. The cue appears the moment the entry carries a ledger, which is the moment the track grows ticks. **And a row click stays what it is** — front the worker card on an activatable row ([D152]), nothing on an inert one. Making inert rows toggle on click would give one list two click meanings distinguished only by whether a card happens to be open, which is the ambiguity `data-activatable` exists to remove; right-click stays the verbs menu in both states ([D142]). No `stopPropagation` and no selection guard rides the cue: `TugListView` commits selection at pointerdown and excuses any descendant that refuses focus, which every `TugButton`-family control does, so a press on the cue never reaches the row's delegate. Plan-document rows are untouched — their entry is a different shape and their affordance a different question. `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/arc-step-list.tsx`/`.css`, `tugdeck/src/components/arcs/arcs-card.tsx`/`.css`, `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/arc-lifecycle-block.tsx` (the docblock's first filled `trailing`), `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/cards/session-card-telemetry-popovers.tsx`/`.css` (the lift). The cue is present on a planned arc and absent on one with no steps, the rows it opens to match the track's ticks, the press fronts no card and picks no row, and a second press unmounts the list. [D141], [D142], [D152], [L06], [L19], [L20] **Amended 2026-09-24 by [D200]:** the cue's seat on the Arcs card is the LINE's trailing slot, `lineTrailing`, not the eyebrow's — the eyebrow could not hold two names and two controls at the sidebar's width, and the names were what paid. The fold, the bit's lifetime, and the row-click rule are unchanged.

**D177.** **One arc, one card: an arc is bound to at most one live card, the server refuses a second by name, and the wire says so with a scalar.** Two cards on one arc had been written down as doctrine rather than as a race, on the reasoning that a list costs nothing and a second reader is harmless. It is not harmless once the machine drives an arc rather than a person: the wheel rotates one card, a stop hands back to one card, a receipt lands on one card, and a transport control on a row reads and moves one card's state — so a second holder is a second driver of one steering wheel, and every one of those surfaces has to pick a holder to mean. Nothing in the system was ever designed to answer "which of them", and the answers that existed were iteration order dressed up as a rule. The refusal is `arc_api::other_holder`, called in `bind` after the arc id is resolved and in `arc_resume` before the resume line is written, returning `<holder> is running <arc>` with the holder named as the user knows it — the same shape and the same place as `bind`'s own displacement refusal, which already said a card runs at most one arc; this says the mirror of it. `arc_resume` keeps `calling_segment`'s resolved id rather than discarding it, because the comparison is against live segment ids and a card posting the frozen `$TUG_SESSION_ID` a stage carries would otherwise be refused its own arc in a sentence naming its own callsign. The scalar runs the whole way through — `SessionLedger::bound_session_by_arc` keyed one-per-arc and warning when a second row reaches it, `ChangesetEntry::Arc.bound_session` and `DocumentArcEntry.bound_session` on the wire, `ArcStatus.bound_session` for the CLI, and `bound_session?: string` on the deck, whose guards reject an entry still carrying the retired array rather than reading it as unbound. What would revisit this is a designed multi-driver — two cards deliberately working one arc, with a rule for whose stop wins and whose receipt is drawn — and no such thing exists or is wanted. [D153], [D173], [L31]

**D178.** **One transport control per arc surface: its face is the arc's own state, its actor is the surface's, and a refusal stays pressable and says why.** Every surface that shows a whole arc showed one you could not run: the Arcs card row routed you to the card working the arc, the `ARC` placard read the arc back, and starting or stopping either meant typing a command into a composer — which is worst exactly when it matters, because an arc that is running is the one whose card you cannot reach to stop it. So both surfaces now carry one button, and everything about it derives. **The face is a pure function of the entry**: no run and a document on disk reads Start, a run somebody stopped reads Resume, a live run reads Stop, and a done arc — or one with nothing to start — draws nothing at all, so a row with no verb has no control rather than a dead one. **The actor is a pure function of the surface**: Stop and Resume act as the session already bound to the arc, because a stop keeps the binding and the bound card is the one doing the work; Start — and a Resume on an arc no card holds — acts as the Arcs card's *followed* card, which is the same ladder [D153]'s Bind climbs and stops at the same rung, because reaching past the followed card would perform work in a card the reader was not looking at. The placard never shows Start: it exists only for the arc its card is already bound to. **Start's kind is a pure function of the documents** — a task list without a plan is a plain arc, a brief or a plan is a planned one — so the deck names what it is opening rather than letting the server sniff a directory. All three derivations live in one module with their truth tables as unit tests, and the control adds exactly one thing they cannot hold: the pending bit of a press that has been sent and not answered ([L02] carries it into React; [L06] paints it).

Two consequences are the point rather than the trim. **A refused control is never DOM-disabled**: a `disabled` button takes no pointer events, so the `title` carrying the reason could never be read and an icon has no label to put it in — the refusal renders `aria-disabled`, keeps its pointer events, and answers a press by posting the reason to the bulletin of the card the press would have acted as ([L22], [L31]: a gesture yields the act or a visible reason). And **the row's control is a verb, which [D153] said a list row's controls are not.** That rule's premise is that the work belongs to the room the row opens, and it already named Replay as the exception because an arc that cannot be replayed has no room to open. Start and Stop are the same exception from the other end: an arc that is not running has no card to route to, and one that is running is precisely the arc whose room is occupied by the work you want to interrupt. Row activation is untouched and still opens the worker's card ([D142]); the control sits in the trailing slot beside the fold cue ([D176]) and stops the click from reaching the row. The Start on a plan row is not a return of the button [D176]'s card removed: that one wore a word and composed a `/tugplug:…` line into the followed card — a label where a control should be — and this one is an icon performing a server verb, which says before it is pressed where the press would land. [D142], [D153], [D176], [L02], [L06], [L22], [L31] **Amended 2026-09-24 by [D200]:** on the Arcs card the control sits in the line's trailing slot beside the fold cue, not the eyebrow's; the face, the actor and the refusal rule are unchanged.

**D179.** **The arc lane has one door: `/arc` decides whether the work earns a plan, and every second place that carried the kind is gone.** Two doors were a routing decision made by typing, and the earlier brief (`briefs/arc-plan-brief.md`) considered exactly this collapse and rejected it twice — "one door with a flag, `/arc --plan`" because a flag hides the routing choice inside argument parsing, and "a single `/arc` door that decides settling itself and asks the user to confirm" because it turns a decision made by typing into a question on every arc. The user overrode both, and the reasoning that answers them is the one the override supplies: *this is an AI experience, not a TUI/CLI experience.* A door that cannot form an opinion about whether work wants settling is not a door worth having, and the model reading the conversation that produced the idea is better placed to judge that than a user choosing between two names in a slash popup. So **the door carries the bit in its product rather than in a flag or a dialog**: it reads the user's prose first, makes the call itself where the prose is silent, and confirms with one two-option `AskUserQuestion` only where it is genuinely ambiguous — which is the narrow residue the rejected "asks on every arc" option would have made universal — and it says which shape it chose before it hands off. The choice is written down as *documents*, not as a word: a plain arc leaves `tasks.md` beside the brief, a planned one leaves the brief alone. **`open_arc` derives the kind from exactly that** (`kind_from_documents`: a task list with no plan is plain, a brief alone or a plan is planned, `plan.md` outranking `tasks.md` as it does for the ledger), records it once as the `arc-kind` line the record already carried, and every reader afterwards consults the record. That derivation is what let three second sources of truth be deleted rather than merely re-spelled: `tugtool arc run --plan`, the `arc_run` control frame's `kind` field with the server refusal that policed it, and the deck's `startKind`, which had the Start button computing a fact about the responder's own data and sending it back — the inversion [L11] names, and the argument for the removal rather than a rename. Nothing downstream of the opening changed: the stages, the wheel's rotation, the audit, the join, and both document formats are as they were. `tugarc-core/src/ops.rs` (`kind_from_documents`, `open_arc` losing its `kind` parameter) and `arc.rs`, `tugtool/src/cli.rs` and `src/arc.rs`, `tugcast/src/arc_api.rs`, `src/server.rs`, `src/feeds/agent_supervisor.rs` (`ARC_DOOR_PREFIXES`, now two spellings of one name) and `src/feeds/arc.rs`, `tugdeck/src/lib/arc-transport.ts` and `components/tugways/arc-transport-control.tsx`, `tugplug/skills/arc/SKILL.md` (rewritten whole) with `tugplug/skills/arc-plan/` deleted, `plugin.json`, `tugplug/CLAUDE.md`, `tuglaws/work-grammar.md`, `arc-work-doctrine.md`, `wheel.md`, `slash-commands.md`, the project `CLAUDE.md`, and `tuggram/data/commands.json`. The decisions are `briefs/one-door-brief.md` [B01]–[B09]. Amends [D178]'s "Start's kind is a pure function of the documents, so the deck names what it is opening" — the derivation is right and it belongs to `open_arc`, so the deck names only the arc. Amends [D169] on the **door's name only**: what that entry calls `/arc-plan` is now the one door choosing a planned arc, and nothing here restores its plan-in-the-door route, which was itself reversed afterwards — the door writes a brief, and devise writes the plan. `/arc-plan` retires totally, with no alias and no notice; the spelling survives in the work grammar's retired-names table. [D169], [D178], [L11], [L31]

**D180.** **The door decides an arc's kind from the work, and nothing inside a document decides it.** The first arc opened through the one door of [D179] came out planned for no reason tied to the work: the door's first decider treated a handed-in brief's Exit line ("**A plan**, devised against this brief") as the user's prose, and that line was the brief skeleton's own default — "**A plan** — the usual exit", written when a brief's only successor was `/arc-plan`, so "a plan" there meant "hand this to the planning door" and not "this work is complex". Under one door that clause routed every brief made the ordinary way to planned before any judgment happened, inverting the calibration [D179] rests on. The mistake was structural rather than a bad call: the handed-in brief is the door's main road, since the user writes briefs with the brief skill and hands them in, and on that road the door skips sharpening and reads no conversation — so a decider that defers to a template line leaves nothing for the model to decide. So **only the user's own words are prose** — typed at the door or said in the conversation — and a brief's Exit section, a "next steps" paragraph, or the word "plan" in a heading is never one. **The reading is the decision, and it is made from the work**: the brief's `[B##]` decisions counted against the open questions that would change what gets written, the files its findings name opened rather than glanced at, and an attempt at the task list itself — a handful of concrete steps in an order defensible from the code is a plain arc, and an order the brief already states is a settled order rather than a reason for a plan. **The brief skeleton's Exit names an arc, not a plan**, and says the kind is not its to choose; its Open Questions section is named as the one the door weighs most. **The hand-off sentence says why**, in one clause from the work's own facts, so a call made for a bad reason is visible on the card in the first sentence rather than a stage later. And because the brief skill writes down what a conversation settled without investigating it, a confident brief and a plain arc together had no cold read before code was written — the door's reading shares the brief author's blind spots, and the implement stage was told a task list "has no review to check". **So the implement stage is a plain arc's cold read**: before opening step 1 it reads the task list against the brief and the code, and a step that rests on a design decision the brief does not make stops the arc through `tugtool arc ask` rather than being built on. The bar is the never-ask list, so a task list whose steps follow from the brief — the ordinary case — costs nothing and says nothing. `tugplug/skills/arc/SKILL.md` (Input grammar rule 1, step 4, step 7, guardrails), `tugplug/skills/arc-implement/SKILL.md` (the ledger-check step), `tugplug/skills/brief/brief-skeleton.md`, `tugplug/skills/brief/SKILL.md`, `tugplug/CLAUDE.md`, `tuglaws/work-grammar.md`. Amends [D179]'s "reads the user's prose first" by saying what counts as the user's prose. [D179]

**D200.** **The Arcs card's controls seat on the row's second line, at its trailing edge; the eyebrow is the two names' alone, and the arc's name outranks the worker's when they still cannot both fit.** At the sidebar's width every row elided both pills — `^arc-card-lifecycle-ha…` beside `tug/svelte-…` — because the eyebrow was spending its width on two things that are not identities: the transport ([D178]) and the fold cue ([D176]), about eight characters of pill type between them, plus a hairline charging the names for a 24px minimum. Nothing on the line beneath elides in practice. So `ArcLifecycleBlock` gains a second, optional slot, `lineTrailing`, beside the eyebrow's `trailing`: when a host passes it, line two becomes a flex row of the lifecycle line and the slot, the slot fixed at the trailing edge and the line taking the width left — where it goes on centring its run, so the reading keeps its relation to line one's centre and the controls stand in the column the eyebrow's cue occupied, one x-position on every row. A host that passes nothing gets the DOM it always had, which is what keeps this additive: the Changes shade's lane, the receipt headers and the placard keep filling the eyebrow's slot, since they have the room. The Arcs card fills the new one on every row — the transport then the cue on a live row, the transport then the delete on a paperwork row, so a brief's Start stands in the same column as a branch's. **The hairline's floor drops from 24px to 12px**, published by the block as `--tugx-arc-lifecycle-rule-min` so the one host that caps a name against it reads the number rather than authoring a second. **The arc pill has priority**: the arc is the row's subject and the worker is who holds it, so scoped to the card's block the arc atom does not shrink and is capped at the width that leaves the worker its 56px floor, the hairline its minimum and the gaps their own, while the worker shrinks to that floor — the clamp idiom `tug-session-identity.css` already argues for, because a shrink ratio would still take a sliver off the arc and a sliver is enough to paint an ellipsis on a name that fits. **The atom register is held.** A smaller pill font for the card was asked for in the same breath and set aside: it collides with the register's one-number doctrine and `at0513`'s pin, [D143]'s rail scale had already been reversed for making the track unreadable, and it buys about three characters where the seat buys eight. Whether it is wanted after all is a judgment about the result, answered by looking at the card with this landed; if it is, it is an amendment to the register and a deliberate re-point of `at0513`, recorded here, never a CSS override scoped to the card. `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/arc-lifecycle-block.tsx`/`.css` (`lineTrailing`, the row wrapper, the published floor), `components/arcs/arcs-card.tsx`/`.css` (the slot filled, the pill priority), `cards/gallery-arc-lifecycle.tsx` (the slot shown filled). Verified in the running app with three arcs at the brief's name lengths, one bound to a long-named session: every arc name whole, the worker pill carrying the whole deficit, the hairline at its floor, the cue flush with the eyebrow's edge; `at0407` re-read with no assertion moved, `at0405` and `at0513` untouched and green. The evidence and the decisions are in the `arcs-card-legibility` arc's brief. Amends [D176] and [D178] (the controls' seat on the card), and restores [D168]'s sentence that the eyebrow's right end is the worker's atom and nothing else. [D141], [D143], [D168], [D176], [D178], [L06], [L19], [L20]

**D181.** **A pinned rail is a panel of the window, not a card on the paper: flush to the window's top and outer edge, square corners, the sunken surface, a strong hairline on its top, bottom, and inner edge, a 12px gutter to the first card that a one-sided shadow fills most of, and no air between its split members.** The rail tier's chrome ([D132]) made the rail's *bar* say what kind of thing it is, and the frame around it still said "card": the same 5px stand-off from every edge, the same radius and shadow, so a rail read as a card that happened to be pinned. The rail distinction spike drew six treatments over a miniature deck — A today's look, B a tinted rail, C a tinted margin, D a ruled gutter, E the panel, F tint on rail and margin together — and E is chosen because shape does the whole job on its own: a rail that is part of the window and a card that floats on the paper differ in kind, which no tint or livery in the gap achieves by degree. The seams are placed so the others stay one block away rather than one rewrite away. **Two named lengths replace the card gap in every rail expression**: `RAIL_EDGE_INSET_PX` (0) is the rail's stand-off from the window on its outer side and top, `RAIL_GUTTER_PX` (12) is the air between the rail's inner edge and the band the cards stand in, each with a CSS twin (`--tug-rail-edge-inset`, `--tug-rail-gutter`) stamped on the document root at boot, and `IMPOSITION_GAP_PX` stays 5 for every seam between cards and between a split column's members, so the deck's rhythm does not move with the rail; a split rail's members keep `RAIL_SEAM_PX` (0) — they touch, meet at one hairline (the member below a seam drops its top rule, read off `data-rail-member-index`), and the seam is the same weight as the outer rules — because a gap between them is the content cards' rhythm and a panel has none. A rail's span inset is edge inset plus width plus gutter minus the card gap, because `imposeRect`'s own gap becomes the last of the gutter; a rail's run is its own (`RAIL_RUN`) rather than the column's, and its foot keeps exactly the maker strip's height with no gap of air, because a panel meeting the strip is furniture meeting furniture. **One treatment, one attribute**: `RAIL_TREATMENT` (`panel`) is written once as `data-rail-treatment` on the deck root, and the panel's CSS is one block under that attribute keyed on a *pinned* rail (`data-rail-side` present), so a released rail keeps its livery and regains a card's frame, which it needs to read as a thing that can be dropped. **The rail's shadow is the canvas's, one strip per side.** Drawn per member it is only as continuous as the member frames are and breaks at every seam of a split rail — loudest on the light themes — which contradicts the panel's own reading at the one boundary the panel is about, since a rail reads as one flush surface from window top to foot. `deck-canvas.tsx` stands one strip per railed side at `RAIL_EDGE_INSET + width`, spanning the rail RUN rather than the window so it stops at the rail's foot, at the margin cap's own z (above every free card, below the rail band), inert to the pointer. It is a **gradient**, not a blurred `box-shadow`: a blur fades in around a box's corners as well as along its edge, which left a soft notch at the top and bottom of every member — the same break arriving from the blur instead of the seams. Peak and falloff are tuned by eye and live in `rail-shadow.css`. **The margin cap is the rail margin**: it spans edge inset through gutter at one z below the rails, still paints the body's graph paper in phase, and still swallows the press as canvas. **Tint is declared at strength zero**: `--tugx-rail-tint-hue` and `--tugx-rail-tint` on `body`, with the rail's ground and border and the margin's canvas and grid each a `color-mix` toward the hue, so B, C, and F are a strength and a block, and D is one rule on the margin. Rejected, *do not re-propose*: **B**, **C**, **D**, and **F** as the shipped treatment — they were seen on the spike beside E and set aside, not left untried — a user-facing setting for the treatment, a change to the rail tier's bar or stripes, a change to the card gap, and a panel frame on a released rail. The spike is deleted; its treatments are reachable on the live deck by changing the attribute from the console. `tugdeck/src/lib/layout-imposer.ts` (`RAIL_EDGE_INSET_PX`, `RAIL_GUTTER_PX`, `railSpanInsetPx`, `railGapBottomPx`, `RAIL_RUN`, `RAIL_TREATMENT`), `deck-manager.ts`, `main.tsx`, `components/chrome/deck-canvas.tsx`, `components/chrome/margin-cap.css`, `components/tugways/tug-pane.css`, `tuglaws/pane-model.md` § rail. The decisions are `briefs/rail-panel-brief.md` [B01]–[B07]. Amends [D132]'s rail tier by adding the frame around it; the bar is unchanged. [D132], [L06]

**Amended: a rail's hairlines mark boundaries, and the window's own edges get none.** The panel first drew a strong rule on its top, bottom, and inner edge, and the top and bottom were wrong on the panel's own argument: a hairline marks a boundary with something else, and the window's top and bottom are not boundaries with anything — which is the same reason the outer edge never carried one. So a seated rail member draws a rule only where it borders something: its inner edge against the gutter, and a seam against the member below it. The seam's one hairline is the **upper** member's bottom rule; no member draws a top rule, and the last member draws no bottom rule, which puts both window edges on the same footing without a special case for index 0 and leaves an overflowing rail's clipped members nothing to hide. `TugPane` stamps `data-rail-member-last` beside `data-rail-member-index` because "am I last" is a fact the pane has and the stylesheet needs. A stacked rail — one frame, no seams — draws only its inner-edge rule and reads as one flush panel from window top to window bottom. A rail card **in the air** regains the whole card frame for the length of `data-gesture` — lift to landing — because a card being carried over cards has to read as a thing that can be dropped; that is the same rule the released rail already had, so afloat and in-flight are one statement rather than two. The full hairline still runs the rail's width, with no terminator at the gutter. Decisions [B01]–[B05] of the rail-refinements brief. `components/tugways/tug-pane.css`, `components/chrome/tug-pane.tsx`.

**D182.** **A sidebar card reserves its scrollbar's lane at all times, so its content never relays out when the bar comes and goes.** Every sidebar scroller — the Cards, Jots and Arcs lists, the Layout card's section and the Overview transcript — is `overflow-y: scroll`, never `auto`. Under classic scrollbars the track is always drawn, so a row's width is the same whether or not the list overflows and nothing hops sideways the moment a group collapses, a file is added, or the Layout mixer unfolds past its run; under overlay scrollbars the bar takes no space either way, so the rule costs nothing there. The technique is the session transcript's own (`session-card.css`), and it carries that rule's prohibition: **never `scrollbar-gutter: stable`** on these scrollers — on overlay-scrollbar Macs it forces a reserved gutter that WebKit re-evaluates under reflow and flickers the column by one bar width. The reasoning is the rail-seams brief's (`briefs/rail-seams-belong-to-the-hand-brief.md` [B01]) one axis over: a rail is a panel the user reads while it changes under them, and a control must not move because the content beside it did. Sheets, menus and popups keep `auto`, because a transient surface is sized once and dismissed. `components/cards/cards-card.css`, `components/jots/jots-card.css`, `components/arcs/arcs-card.css`, `components/layout/layout-card.css`, `components/overview/overview-card.css`. [L06]

**D183.** **A rail is always divided, its sashes are the hand's, and one verb resizes it on request.** This reverses [D134] and deletes what grew up under it. D134's synthesis was that a shared rail's arrangement is a *choice* — Stack, Fit or Flow, stored per side — and the arc that built it out added a measurement pipeline to feed the choice: a `ResizeObserver` per content card publishing a natural height through `cardAppetiteStore`, a `heightSource` registration field, a greed rank to break ties, and a **seed** that recomputed a rail's division from those naturals whenever a card joined or left. Every piece was individually defensible and the whole was not: the deck moved the user's own sashes for reasons the user never asked for, most visibly when hiding a card and showing it again threw away a division the hand had made. Four implementation efforts went at the resulting behaviour and none of them made it right, which is the evidence this decision rests on — not a taste for less machinery, but a rule nobody could implement correctly because the rule itself was overdetermined.

**What is left is the smallest thing that works.** A rail divides its run among its members by stored shares bounded below by their floors, with one derived exception — floors and seams that do not fit the run leave nothing to divide, so the rail stands as a strip until a member leaves or the window grows. An absent record is a rail nobody has divided, and it stands at equal shares, which is what the same arithmetic already answers for weights of 1; no second regime stands beside it. The record outlives its members the way `order` already does: a card that leaves keeps its share, a card that arrives with none weighs 1, and nothing anywhere drops the record. **Only two things write it** — a seam drag's release, and ***Resize Sidebars to Fit***.

**The verb is the only algorithm left, and only the user runs it.** ⌃⌥⌘R and a View menu row above the sidebar-card rows; on invocation it reads each card's content element once from the DOM — the `data-card-content` marker is all that survives of the measurement arc — and stands the rail at what its cards ask for. Two regimes of one formula, both filling the run exactly: when the naturals fit, every card takes its natural plus a share of the slack in proportion to that natural, so a long list keeps its advantage into the room left over; when they do not, every card takes its floor plus a share of what remains in proportion to how far its natural stood above that floor. Overview is a stream and has no content height to read, so it takes three quarters of the run as a named constant beside the verb. The answer converts through `placeSharesFromHeights` and commits through `setRailShares` — the seam drag's own path — after which it is the hand's division like any other. **Nothing re-runs it**: not a resize, not a content change, not a membership change, not a relaunch. A card whose content outgrows its share scrolls inside itself, as a split pane does in every editor.

**Deleted, not repurposed:** `RailMode` and `PlaceLayout` and every `layout` parameter threaded through the allocator; `flowHeightsOf` and the rail's Flow arithmetic; the Layout card's `Stack | Fit | Flow` rows and the rail place marks; `set-rail-mode`, `set-rail-layout`, `equalize-rail` and `fit-rail-to-content`; `card-appetite-store.ts` with `useMeasuredCardAppetite` and `DeckState.appetites`; `heightSource` and `CardHeightSource`; `seedSharedHeights`, `seedPlaceShares` and the deck manager's appetite subscription and settle; `withoutRailShares` and `withoutColumnShares`, since nothing drops a record any more. Columns lose Flow with the type and keep Stack, which is a different question about a different place; column *Fit to Content* went with the seed, having no arithmetic left, and the column seam's double-click now equalizes. `greedRank` stays, read by the **width** allocator alone. The tests of the deleted machinery were deleted rather than adapted — `at0359`, `at0401`, `at0540`, `at0542`, `layout-imposer-flow`, `layout-imposer-heights-census`, `card-appetite-store` and `place-allocation` — and two small ones replace them: `at0543`, which drags a sash and reads it back across a relaunch and across a card leaving and returning, and `at0544`, which runs the verb once and reads both regimes off the content elements' own heights, then writes a jot and watches the sashes not move. Full doctrine: [pane-model.md](pane-model.md). Supersedes [D134]; amends [D135], whose rail-mode-flip choreography now describes a member arriving or leaving rather than a mode changing. [D134], [D135], [D172], [L06], [L23], [P04]

**D184.** **Directional card focus takes the whole ⌥⌘ arrow band, and the transcript's turn family moves to the bracket row to vacate it.** A reader standing in a split column had no gesture for the card above them: the deck moves focus only by **ring order** — ⇧⌘[/] around every visible card, ⌥⌘[/] around a stack's depth — and neither consults geometry, so neither can answer "which card is to my left". The chord a hand reaches for is ⌥⌘↑, and Previous Turn held it. So the allocation moves before the feature exists, and the move is derived rather than traded: **arrows are geometry, brackets are series** ([chord-tiers.md](chord-tiers.md) § "Arrows are geometry, brackets are series"). ⌃⌘ arrows move the **furniture** — a rail on that side, a card up its column — and ⌥⌘ arrows move the **reader** through that same geometry, so on the horizontal pair the two read as a pair: ⌃⌘← opens the left side, ⌥⌘← walks into it. Stepping turns is stepping an ordered run and nothing about it is spatial, so the arrows were the wrong key class for that family all along and the arrow shape was carrying a resemblance rather than a meaning. **Previous / Next Turn are ⌃⌘[ / ⌃⌘] and First / Last Turn ⌃⇧⌘[ / ⌃⇧⌘]**, where `[` is back and `]` is forward exactly as in the row's three other families, and ⇧-as-extreme shares its base's key — which satisfies R1 outright instead of being exempted from it under R2. The family moves whole, because split across two key classes First/Last would have no base to be the extreme of; the cost is the user's own muscle memory, accepted explicitly, and it is NOT paid back at the card — the attempt to do so is withdrawn, below. **Neither bracket set collides with CodeMirror, and that is load-bearing rather than lucky**: `defaultKeymap` binds `Mod-[` / `Mod-]` to `indentLess` / `indentMore` and Tug installs that keymap in both the composer and the text card, so plain ⌘[/] would be either destructive when promoted or dead in the composer — the one surface turn stepping is used from, since a reader steps back through turns with the caret still in the prompt. The ⌃⌘ and ⌃⇧⌘ compositions are untouched by it, so the chords keep the property the arrow binding had.

**Focus travel is a spatial reckoning over the arrangement, not a walk of a ring.** The rule, in order: candidates are the cards a reader can see or conceive of — the front of each column member, plus the members of a **showing** rail; keep those strictly beyond the source's edge on the arrow's axis; prefer candidates whose perpendicular span overlaps the source's, because a card diagonally away is not "to the left" of you; among those, nearest edge wins. **On a stacked column ↑/↓ reads as z**, front and back — there is no geometry to consult when every member draws the same rect, and refusing would make the chord dead on an unsplit slot, which mirrors `move-in-column` exactly and duplicates the ⌥⌘[/] stack ring deliberately: one gesture, one meaning per arrangement. Entered *laterally*, a stacked column hands focus to its **z-frontmost** member, the same answer the rail pair's three-state ladder gives. **The travel remembers where it entered**, the way a caret remembers its goal column, so ← then → returns you to the card you started from and ↑↓ through uneven cards does not drift sideways; the memory resets on any focus change that is not itself a directional move. **A target outside the visible band is still a target**: the band travels so the card is visible when focus arrives and the arrival flashes, the way Go to Slot does — focus landing somewhere invisible is the worse failure, and the flow strip draws the whole arrangement while the reader is using it, so a slot past the band's edge is inside the mental model. A hidden **rail** is the opposite case and stays out: summoning a surface that is not on the deck is ⌃⌘←/→'s job. **A refusal is visible** — at the arrangement's edge the gesture flashes the blocking pane's border, the answer a refused `moveInColumn` already gives. Focus arrives through `transferFocusForActivation`, the same activation taxonomy the click path uses, never a raw `activateCard`. All four are promoted to the Window menu with **empty** Swift key equivalents (R6), and the preemption is intended: ⌥⌘ arrows are not text currency in any Tug surface, and deck-level focus is not a surface's to decline. **The muscle-memory cost is not paid back at the card, and the attempt to pay it there was withdrawn (2026-09-11).** The Session card published its four Go in Transcript verbs to `paneTitleBarItemsStore` as rows in a `⋮` popup inside the title-bar rollup, each rendering its chord through `commandShortcut`; all of it is now removed, along with the `⋮` itself and the menu-row presentation it was the only user of — see `pane-model.md` § the rollup. A menu behind a glyph inside a row that appears only on hover teaches nobody: it is reachable, not visible, and discoverability is a property of what stands at rest. What it did leave standing at rest was an unexplained glyph on every Session card's masthead, which is how the user found it. Where the verb went is said by Session ▸ Go in Transcript and by the Keyboard pane, and a title bar states a card's verb with a glyph or not at all.

**The ⌃⌥⌘ set is recorded as unavailable, and that is a standing constraint rather than a note on this feature.** The set is claimed machine-wide on the user's Mac (Keyboard Maestro drives special-character entry from it), so a grant there is a chord that never arrives. It matters here because the algebra's own answer for this feature *derives* onto it — ⌥-as-variant over ⌃⌘↑/↓ reads exactly as "the same move, the reader instead of the card" — and without the constraint written down the next grant rediscovers the dead set. ⌃⌥⌘P and ⌃⌥⌘R are grandfathered; a third would not be granted.

**Not done, deliberately:** **Focus Leftmost / Rightmost / Topmost / Bottommost** — `move-in-column` keeps all four of its chords, the user's call over an earlier rate argument, so the extremes of focus travel are simply not commands and **⌥⇧⌘↑/↓ stays free**, recorded as available rather than spent in the change that vacates it. **⌘[ / ⌘] for turn navigation**, for the CodeMirror reason above. **A fall-through ladder on ⌥⌘↑/↓** — stepping the transcript while it has somewhere to step and handing the gesture to the deck at the last turn — which matches the rail pair's three-state ladder and fails the job: mid-transcript in a long session, leaving the card upward would cost one press per turn, and a gesture usable only from an edge is not a way out of a split. **Retiring or re-scoping the lateral and stack rings**, which stay exactly as they are: a ring and a geometry are different gestures for different intents and the deck wants both. **Any wider re-allocation of the arrow bands** — only the turn family moves. `tuglaws/chord-tiers.md`, `tugdeck/src/components/tugways/command-registry.ts` (the turn family's four bindings, the four focus entries), the spatial resolver's own module under `tugdeck/src/lib/`, `components/chrome/deck-canvas.tsx` (the handlers, the remembered entry position, the band travel and the flashes), `components/tugways/host-menu-state.ts` (the per-direction menu fact), `tugapp/Sources/AppDelegate.swift` (four Window rows), and the Session card's title-bar contribution. [D126], [D130], [D131], [D175], [L30], [P12], [P15]

**D185.** **A Session card folds to a masthead and its status row — one fact on the pane, three doors onto it, and a fold that runs on the deck's own settle clock in every shape the card can stand in.** The form is settled ink from the brief ([B01]–[B03]): a masthead one line taller than the ordinary 72px tier, with the extra line spent on the ACCOUNT run — **amended 2026-09-12 and again 2026-09-13**, and both amendments are in the register paragraph below — and the Z2 telemetry row with all five instruments, their placards, and the fold control at its trailing edge. A full-width `Show Transcript` bar once stood under the row as a third band; it retired when the control took the Z2 seat, because one verb wants one seat and two doors of the same kind on one card is what the bar had become ([B03]). Everything else — the transcript pane, the find bar, the prompt entry — **folds rather than unmounts** ([L26]), so a half-typed prompt survives a fold, the transcript store keeps streaming, and the beat is where the reader was already looking. The point of the whole shape is a **wall**: one split column holding a column of watched sessions, each readable without being opened.

**Folded is a fact about the pane, stored with the layout, set by one action.** `TugPaneState.folded?` is additive-optional on the deck blob, so every existing layout restores to exactly the deck it described, and `paneFoldedOf` / `cardFoldedOf` are the only readers. One action (`TUG_ACTIONS.TOGGLE_SESSION_FOLD`) and one setter (`setPaneFolded`) stand behind **three doors** ([L11]): the **fold control at the trailing edge of the Z2 status row** ([D97], where the zone and its diagram are recorded), a row on the Session card's section menu, and **⌃⌘Y** — a Tug-tier grant read as a glyph rather than derived, which is the reading `chord-tiers.md` records after AppKit turned out to claim the ⌥⌘M the derivation had landed on. The appearance is `data-folded` on `.tug-pane`, a projection of the stored fact, and the whole folded form is CSS keyed on that attribute ([L06]) rather than a second React tree.

**One control, one seat, two glyphs.** `SessionFoldControl` stands at the trailing edge of Z2 in both of the card's forms, as a ghost button, and only its glyph and its label change with the flag — `ChevronsDownUp` collapses, `ChevronsUpDown` expands. The seat is a **sibling** of the strip's status content rather than a child of it: `.session-card-status-bar-main` carries `data-tug-focus="refuse"` so that pressing an instrument never pulls the caret off the editor, which is right for a measurement and wrong for a door, so the control sits inside the strip's box and outside its refusal while the cells and the gaps keep refusing exactly as they did. It is a real cycle stop, after the cells. Folded it carries `persistentDefaultRing` and is the card's **Return-home** — the one live stop that is not a cell, taking the role at the order the retired bar held it, which is what makes Return mean "show the transcript" while the transcript, the find bar and the composer are all `inert`. Open it declares no default ring, because Return belongs to the composer's submit and one persistent mark per scope is [P08]'s rule.

**The tier is measured, not guessed, and it is fixed in both directions.** `SESSION_FOLDED_HEIGHT_PX` is **145** — the masthead's 72 plus `--tugx-session-masthead-extra-line`, plus the status row, with nothing under it — and the number is read back off the built card rather than estimated from the drawing. **Amended 2026-09-24: 144 → 145, and the pixel is the strip's fraction.** Z2 builds to 53.8px, the tier was rounded down to the 53 the tuning block's arithmetic reads, and the 0.8px the strip overhung by raised a scrollbar on the pane's `overflow: auto` content box. A scrollbar is 12px of *inline* size, so Z2's `@container` rungs were then asked about a box 12px narrower than the one nothing had resized, and the first rung dropped the TIME cell: a folded card's instruments re-laid-out on every fold, against a width the user never changed. The tier now clears the fraction, and a folded pane's content box is a clip at rest as well as mid-crossing (`tug-pane.css`), so no future metric change can spend a card's width on a scrollbar it has nothing to scroll. `at0552` claim 5 pins the strip's width and its cells across the fold. It was 173 while the `Show Transcript` bar stood under the row; retiring the bar is what a wall is paid in, and a 900px run now holds six cards where it held five. The TIER does not grow for a long line or shrink for a short one, because a column of folded cards whose rows changed height as their lines changed length would ripple every time any session spoke — and the height is declared on the pane, so it holds however the runs inside it measure. **Amended 2026-09-12: the wrapping run's own box is not pinned with it.** It takes the lines it has ink for and the beat under it follows, because a box held open at two spends a one-line post's slack as a blank line between the two readings; released, that slack falls to the foot of the band, where it reads as the edge of the tier. A folded card's registered size policy drops its **minimum height to the tier** and leaves its width policy alone (`CardRegistration.foldedSizePolicy`, `getStackSizePolicy(ids, { folded })`), which is what lets a split slot pack them at all.

**A wall packs on a share of zero AND a ceiling; the share alone would have made five stretched cards.** A folded member weighs zero in `placeMembers`, which is the right answer while an open card stands beside it — but a column whose members *all* weigh zero divides its run equally by the allocator's own stated reading, so five folded cards on a tall run would each stand at a fifth of it. `PlaceMember.ceiling` is what makes the tier a tier: the most run a member will take however much the place has to give, set from the pinned policy. What no longer fits overflows into the strip and scrolls, which is machinery the column already had. **One card is open per split column** (the brief's [B07]): opening one writes the flag onto every other member of the same column in the same commit, so the wall stays a wall, and `wallRevealOffset` slides the strip so the opened card lands just under its neighbour above rather than at the top of the run — the reader keeps their place.

**The control is the card's key view when folded, and the home Return goes to.** It is the only live stop a folded card offers besides its instruments, so Return on a focused folded card shows the transcript with no aim required — the focus language of [D122] and `focus-language.md`, applied to a card that has almost nothing else to focus. Because the seat no longer unmounts when the form changes, the show **releases the key view explicitly** rather than relying on the old bar's disappearance to do it.

**The Session card's masthead reads in its own register, and the rows read in theirs.** A folded session's two lines are written for someone who will not open the card, so the **wall** register gives one of them a second line where the ordinary **line** register gives each of them one — and the two lines carry two different facts, which is the whole of the register. **Amended 2026-09-12: the masthead wears it in both forms.** The tier is the taller one whether or not the card is folded, keyed on which masthead the pane wears rather than on the fold, so the register no longer turns over with the form — the post is worth its second line with a transcript under it as much as without, and a tier that changed height on a fold would move every card in a packed column. The **line** register is the Cards card's rows and the picker's rows now, and nothing else. **Amended 2026-09-13: the two lines are the standing sentence and the account run, and the beat is off the masthead.** The DESCRIPTION run is [D132]'s ladder on one line in every form. **Amended 2026-09-14: it has a turn override now, and the override is the current line.** At rest it says what the session is FOR across its whole run and holds still while the work under it moves; during a turn it reads the Observer's current line for that turn, else the ask the turn is answering, and it returns to the standing sentence when the turn ends. The complaint that bought this was a topline still describing the previous turn a minute after a new ask, which the standing sentence cannot fix without ceasing to be a through-line. The ACCOUNT run's own ladder was deliberately left alone, so in the seconds before a line is written both runs can carry the same ask — accepted rather than overlooked, because re-cutting the run below was not what this change was about. The ACCOUNT run beneath it is the two-line run the tier was widened for: a compaction pin, else during a turn the newest Observer post about this session ([D187]) shown whole and cut with the ordinary mark when it does not fit, else the ask the turn is answering — with a 60 s sitrep the first post lands no sooner than a minute in, and for that minute the ask is the one line that cannot be wrong — else the rest sentence, which names when the last turn finished. The digest's newest line ([D186]) reads nowhere on the masthead: a beat of the Editing-foo.ts kind is too low-level to tell one session from another, and it keeps the history popover the account run opens, one click away. This reversed the second-line brief's [B03] (the post as the upper line, the beat under it) and the band-and-lede brief's [B03] (the post cut to its first sentence), both by `briefs/masthead-sentence-over-post-brief.md`. *What stood from 2026-09-12 to then:* the upper line said what the turn was FOR — the newest post, falling back to the ask — and the lower line said what was happening NOW, the newest digest line; the second line went to the beat first and then to the upper line, because the post is budgeted at 200 characters against a slot that holds some 70 and refreshes on the Observer's 60 s sitrep, while the beat is a digest line short enough for one line, broadcast about once a second, that would have flickered between one and two lines of ink. That arithmetic is why the second line is the account run's now — the post is still the longest and slowest run on the tier — and the tier stays 88px because the term it grows by did not move. A wall of watched sessions is read by asking "what is that one doing" and "what did that one just do", and this is the register that answers both without the card being opened. The digester narrates **waits** as first-class beats — a permission request out to the user reads `Waiting for permission: <tool>`, a question reads `Waiting on: <header>` — and puts the superseded line back when the request is withdrawn **or** when the approved call's own `tool_result` arrives, which is the only frame that lands on both an allow and a deny. Two frame kinds are in the tap's allowlist to make that visible at all; the wait is state the digester keeps per scope, never a timer. The register is a prop the identity row takes (`activityRegister`, still the activity's name because the register is the ROW's), not a stylesheet decision: a stylesheet keys the stacking on `data-register` ([L06]), but the one thing it cannot decide is which run the attribute rides.

**The fold is one beat on the deck's clock, in every shape the card can stand in, and that follows from what the settle actually animates.** Three motions were drawn against real-sized cards before one was picked: a staged fold with the bar arriving late, a cross-fade under a held tier, and a single collapse under a pinned masthead with nothing staged and nothing fading. The third is what ships, and the argument is structural rather than a preference. The imposer's settle tweens a frame's **height as a real geometry term** — the gestures that change a frame's height halve or double it, which no cap admits, so the settle never smears height — which means the card's interior re-lays-out at every intermediate height the frame passes through. A staged or split-beat interior would therefore be visibly out of step with the frame carrying it, and the seam would show. So the interior's `transition-duration` reads `--tugx-imposer-settle-duration` scaled by `--tug-timing`, the same custom property the canvas writes from `IMPOSITION_SETTLE_MS`: **one clock, and a retune anywhere up the tree retimes both halves**. Terminal state is written only at the end — the card stamps `data-fold="moving"` when the flag flips and `"settled"` on `transitionend`, with a slack timeout behind it, and the `inert` attribute on the folded regions is written by that effect rather than by React, because a `display: none` starting state has no transition to run and would turn the unfold into a cut. `prefers-reduced-motion` collapses the whole thing to a cut.

**Amended: one clock only holds where the settle is ARMED, and a fold did not arm it.** The settle's window opens on a change in `arrangementSignature`, and a pane's folded flag was not one of its terms — so the fold ran on the deck's clock only where some *other* term happened to move. A split column's allocation does move, so a wall folded correctly and the shape looked settled; the same card on a free pane got the 100ms [D07] window-shade ease under a 400ms interior collapse, and alone in a stacked slot — a height the imposer writes as geometry with no transition at all — it cut. One gesture drew three ways depending on where the card stood. The flag is now a term, so the fold arms wherever the card is, and the Last pass interpolates the frame's real height delta as the geometry it always was. The route was chosen over letting the card drive its own frame height: this decision's own argument is that the frame's height is a real tween the settle runs, and the interior has to follow the frame rather than the other way round. The [D07] ease then has to stand down — `[data-imposer-settling] .tug-pane { transition: none }` — because two clocks on one property is exactly what the free pane was showing; the second clock is not started at all rather than out-specified.

**Amended: the frame moves and the transcript does not — the fold sweeps its edge over a still picture.** The clock was right and the motion was still distracting, and the reason was not the frame. `.session-view-slot` is `flex: 1 1 0` and the column's floor stands down while folded, so the slot — and the `TugListView` scroller inside it — shrank continuously with the frame, and the list's container `ResizeObserver` answered every one of those deliveries with a pin to the bottom and a scroll tick. For a reader at the live edge that slid the text upward by exactly the amount the viewport had just lost, every frame, in both directions; for every reader it re-windowed rows under the closing edge. The unfold was the same defect for longer, since the slot gets its box back at the first frame and then grows for the whole tween. So the slot keeps flexing to zero exactly as it did — the frame's tween and Z2's ride are untouched — and the slot's **child** is given the open box's height for the length of the motion, which the slot's own `overflow: hidden` then clips. A scrollport whose size never changes delivers nothing to observe, so none of it runs. A fade over the transcript would have hidden the same churn rather than stopping it, and would have put a second animated property in a block whose whole argument is that only the frame animates.

**The height is the open box's, watched rather than sampled, and that is forced.** It cannot be read when the fold starts: the flag's DOM lands before the card's layout effect runs, so a forced layout there reports the folded geometry, and at unfold time the slot is `display: none` and has no geometry at all. So a `ResizeObserver` on the slot keeps the open height current — it answers the thing that actually changed, which is what binding a card, resizing a pane and resizing the window all are — and refuses deliveries while `data-fold` is on the root, so a mid-motion box can never become the picture's size. Refusing them is not enough on its own: a fold in and back out is a run of nothing but refused deliveries, so a pane resized while the card sat folded would leave the cache holding the pre-fold height and every later fold would freeze the picture at a size the box no longer is. The open state therefore reads its own height once more when the unfold lands, where the tween is over and the geometry is authoritative. A card that has never been open has no height and does not freeze; its first unfold is the motion as it was, which is no worse than before. The composer's half of the same problem needs no height at all: its child is told `align-self: start` and keeps its own content height, and a measured one there would be actively wrong, since a grid child with a definite height gives the entry region a content-based minimum that props the fold open for the whole motion and cuts at the end.

**The clip eats the OLDEST end, and that was looked at rather than argued.** Both anchors were built and watched in the running app with a ruler in the transcript. Anchored at the top, the newest text is the first thing eaten: Z2 slides down over lines the reader has already read and the live edge is gone before the motion is half over. Anchored at the bottom — `justify-content: flex-end` on the slot while moving — the picture's bottom edge stays welded to the slot's bottom edge for the whole travel, so the fold eats upward and the last thing visible before Z2 closes is the newest thing the session said. That is the reading the Z2 seat was designed for ([D97]), and it is what a Session card's reader is looking at. The composer's child is anchored at the top for the mirror reason: the caret line stays visible longest.

**A folded card is a form that calls for the user and cannot answer for them, so the Changes shade is a room of the OPEN form.** A session whose arc has finished stands at Ready ([D188]) and the card's answer to that is to reveal the Changes room with the join armed — which is right for a card the reader can see and wrong for one folded to a masthead and a status row. The shade mounts over Z2, which is where the fold control stands, so the one door the form has is the first thing covered; and the Join it came to offer is inside the regions the fold made `inert`, so the room it opened is a room nobody can act in. The reveal therefore **defers while the card is folded, without spending the head** — the offer is still standing, the effect re-runs on the unfold, and the entry the fold declined is the entry the user's own gesture carries. A decline that marked the head spent would be worse than the shade: the offer would be silently used up by a card that never showed it.

**And every explicit door unfolds first rather than entering the room directly.** The Z2 arc placard's Show in Changes, the Arcs card row, the View ▸ Changes route and the finished receipt's own Join all dispatch the unfold on a folded card and then enter, in the same commit, so no opener of the shade can mount it over the folded form — the rule holds at every entrance rather than at the passive one. They enter themselves rather than handing the entry to the deferred effect: that effect answers the quiet-moment gate, and a bidden entry routed through it would be swallowed by a head already spent, a half-typed composer or a turn in flight, which is the licence [B07] exists to honour. **The folded form announces in the voices it has**: the masthead's phase dot reads Ready, and Z2's ARC cell takes the arc join register's own word rather than composing a second one, so a card that cannot show the room can still say there is one to open. The masthead beat's rest sentence composes the register's line while the offer stands and returns to the digester's ([D186]) the moment it is spent — one composed line rather than a second narrator, which is [D186]'s own rule applied to the one fact the digester cannot see.

**Not done, deliberately:** a folded pane whose active tab switches to a non-Session card keeps the form (nothing hides it for that tab); and a View ▸ menu row for the focused pane's folded state beside Card Width.

**Still open, and recorded as open rather than answered.** The **count goal** — how many sessions a wall should hold — is the user's call: a 900px run holds six cards at the 144 tier, eight would want a tier near 108, and the floor is the 88px masthead plus the Z2 row, so whether the goal is revised or the tier is (a one-line Z2, a shorter masthead when folded) is a question about the shape and wants its own brief. And **the control on an unbound card**: Z2 exists only for a bound session, so an unbound card has no seat for the control and no real folded form — today it folds to a blank box — and whether it should fold at all, and what it shows if it does, is undecided.

**D186.** **One Rust digester taps the wire once, and every account of what a session is doing reads its lines.** Three producers used to watch `CODE_OUTPUT`, each with its own allowlist, its own spelling and its own store: a TypeScript daemon wrote the masthead's beat, a Haiku job wrote the standing sentence, and the Observer read raw payload JSON. They could describe the same minute of the same session three different ways, and that disagreement was structural rather than a bug anybody could fix. This supersedes [D103]. **The tap** is one subscription, in `feeds/session_digest.rs`: `DIGEST_FORWARD_ALLOWLIST` — the union of what the beat needed and what the Observer needed — and `forwardable_session`, which splices the session id and maintains the one replay-mute set, so a reconnect flood never re-narrates history. `control_request_forward` / `control_request_cancel` are in the list because a session waiting for permission is the state a watched card is most often *in*, and the wait's end rides `tool_result`, the only frame that arrives on both an allow and a deny. **The digester** turns each frame into one sentence in one vocabulary — settled-sentence detection that respects abbreviations and math spans, tool-verb narration, agent-label overrides, skill labels, question beats — and hands it to that session's `SessionDigest`, a rolling deque bounded by a line count (`DEFAULT_BUFFER_MAX_FRAMES`, 256) and a byte budget, newest last, with an explicit elision marker so a truncated window is never read as a whole stretch. A line **supersedes** rather than appends where it re-describes the thing the last line described, which is what makes the deque a digest rather than a log. **Three readings, one deque.** (1) The newest line is the **beat**: `digest_bridge.rs` broadcasts it on the `DIGEST` feed (`0x80`), spaced by `VOICE_THROTTLE_MS` per scope with failures, waits and turn boundaries bypassing the throttle, and a reconnecting deck fetches the tail through the `list_digest_lines` → `list_digest_lines_ok` CONTROL pair — the `list_session_state_changes` shape, never feed replay — answered out of memory. (2) The whole deque is the **Observer's window** ([D187]), the same sentences the reader is watching, which is what makes a post checkable against the beat standing under it, and what lets a ref validate against the rendered digest. (3) The Observer's envelope hands back the **standing sentence**, so the session's description rests on the same evidence. **What this bought and what it cost.** The `tugpulse` daemon is gone with its spawn contract, its build and signing steps and its kill switch; `pulse_lines` is dropped by a registered migration, so the beat's tail lives only in the deque and does not survive a tugcast restart — accepted, because a beat is what is happening *now* and the Observer's posts are the durable record. The TypeScript `OutboundMessage` parse that made wire-shape drift a compile error is paid for instead by a fixture test over a recorded frame stream, which pins the produced lines and is what fails if the vocabulary drifts; that test is the drift guard and is not to be deleted as redundant. **The beat has no switch of its own** and is not to be given one: it is deterministic and free, [D187] holds the one switch there is, and it gates model wakes only. `tugrust/crates/tugcast/src/feeds/session_digest.rs`, `feeds/digest_bridge.rs`, `tugcast-core/src/protocol.rs` (`FeedId::DIGEST`); `tugdeck/src/lib/digest-store.ts` ([L02]), `lib/beat-line/`, `components/tugways/tug-activity-line.tsx` + `.css`, `beat-text.tsx` + `.css`, `cards/session-activity-card.tsx`. [D97], [D103], [D127], [D132], [D185], [D187], [L02], [L06], [L19], [L20], [L26]

**D188.** **`Ready` is the session dot's first green, and it is the arc's fact rather than the session's.** An arc that finishes leaves no turn in flight, so the reducer says `idle` — true about the turn and wrong about the session, which is done and waiting on a person. `Idle` is the one reading that turns a call for the user into the same quiet a session nobody has spoken to all day wears. So `ready` is flattened in beside `background` and the pending ask, on the same seam those two already ride in `session-phase-visual.ts` — a **presentation key only**: the turn machine is untouched, `canSubmit` stays true, and Ready is a state a reader may keep working under rather than a block. It promotes from `idle` alone — every other phase is a turn saying something more specific about the session right now, and a standing join offer is patient — and it sits below transport, below an interrupt in flight and below a dialog holding the user's answer, because Awaiting blocks the turn and Ready blocks nothing. Its visible label is **Ready**, not a second Awaiting: the two are different asks and the words say which.

**The dot goes green while it breathes, and the crossing is deliberate.** Across the design system `action` is work in flight and `success` is the finished reading, paired so that key-while-running resolves to green-when-finished; `ready` is the one place the pairing is crossed — `{ role: success, state: running }` — for a session whose work is finished and whose next move is a person's. Nothing else on the session dot has ever worn `success`, so green-and-breathing means exactly one thing across the room, and the pulse is truthful on Awaiting's own argument: what is depicted is a live wait on the user, not a settled session.

**The fact is derived once, card-keyed, and every surface inherits it.** Readiness belongs to the arc the card is mated to rather than to the session store, so the walk the phase hook already makes — session → card → services — grows one leg: the card's arc binding, and that arc's row in the changes feed. `joinOfferStands` is the whole condition, and it is the server's own `join_ready` made durable rather than a second derivation: the offer is raised when the machine's work is done and a candidate stands, and it is gone the moment the join is taken, discarded, or the arc head moves under a reopen. So "present" is exactly "standing", and there is nothing here to age out or spend twice. One derivation is what keeps the masthead dot, the Z2 STATE cell, the Changes card's citation dots and the Arcs card row from each computing their own and disagreeing about one arc. The input is **optional** for the reason `runningJobCount` and the pending ask are: a replayed historical state-change row has no live register to consult, and absent correctly reads as making no claim.

**Ready says what it is waiting for, in the four places the reader is already looking.** The dot carries the word. Z2's ARC cell takes the arc join register's own word — `ready`, in place of the step fraction — rather than composing a second one. The composer's placeholder says both halves, that the arc finished and the one act that lands it, and keeps the composer's own invitation in it, because a session you can go on working under should say so. And the **finished arc's transcript receipt grows a Join**: the quiet line reporting the ending becomes a line and an act while the offer stands. That receipt is a frozen record of a past moment and makes exactly one live reading — whether the offer standing *now* is about the arc this row names — so a card that has moved on to another arc shows an old receipt with no button on it. The Join performs no join: it routes through the card's one reveal path, arms the room, and leaves the decision in front of the diff where it belongs.

`tugdeck/src/lib/code-session-store/session-phase-visual.ts` (the key, the label, the visual), `lib/code-session-store/use-session-phase.ts` (`joinOfferStands` and the card-keyed hooks), `lib/join-offer-reveal.ts`, `lib/arc-join-register.ts` (the register's word and its line), `components/tugways/session-phase-dot.tsx`, `components/tugways/session-identity-row.tsx`, `components/tugways/cards/session-card.tsx` (the placeholder and the reveal paths), `cards/session-card-telemetry-renderers.tsx` (the ARC cell), `cards/session-arc-receipt-block.tsx` + `.css` (the receipt's Join). **D197.** **A session reference resolves against the whole machine, and the pill says truthfully which of three things it found: here, elsewhere, or absent.** `sessions.db` is per-instance and a callsign is unique only inside one, so before this every reference to a session another Tug instance recorded — or to one in a project this instance had never opened — came back `unknown` and was drawn dashed with "Session not found" over it. That was a lie about a session sitting on the same disk, and it is the failure the third verdict exists to end. **The finding facts live in `session_index.db`**, a machine-global ledger tugcast is the only writer of, carrying uuid, line, callsign, project dir, recording instance and title — enough to FIND a session, never enough to pretend this ledger holds it. Instance isolation of `sessions.db` is untouched, which is the point of splitting the two: the index answers *where*, and the session's truth stays where it was. **`resolve_sessions_ok` gains an `elsewhere` half** beside `sessions` and `unknown`, and an `elsewhere` answer **seeds none of the identity stores** — those hold what this ledger knows, and seeding a foreign session's callsign into them would make it resolve as a local one everywhere they are read, which is the whole distinction gone. A pill renders an `elsewhere` from the answer's own fields instead. **The rendering is solid, dotless, and inert.** Solid because the session exists and dashing it would say the opposite of the truth; dotless because this client subscribes to no phase for a session it does not hold and an idle dot is a claim ("asleep") rather than an absence of one; inert because there is no card to raise and adopting another instance's session is not this gesture. The tooltip carries where it actually is. `absent` keeps the dashed pill and now says **"Not on this machine"**, which is the honest sentence — the old "Session not found" described a search this client was no longer the whole of. **Freshness is pushed, never polled** ([D172]'s rule, met here): `session_index_watch` is one `notify` watch over the data dir that broadcasts one `session_index_changed` CONTROL frame, and the deck answers by dropping exactly the two answers the index can contradict — the misses and the foreign findings — so the next repaint re-asks. A `found` survives, because this ledger's own word is not something another instance's write can overturn. The watch's file-name filter runs **inside the notify callback, before the channel send**, because the directory it watches is the app's hottest: `changes.db` and `prompt_history.db` live there and are written on ordinary editing, so an unfiltered watch would spend a wake and a broadcast per keystroke. **One ask key, one predicate.** `sessionVerdictAskKey` is the single spelling every asker uses — the atom's uuid where it carries one, else the whole `project/callsign` value, never the callsign half — because answers are filed under the spelling that was asked, and two askers spelling it differently give one session two half-answers each of which reads the other's as pending forever. `isSessionResumable` is the same discipline for the resume gesture, which three surfaces now offer. `tugrust/crates/tugcore/src/session_index.rs`, `session_finder.rs`; `tugrust/crates/tugcast/src/feeds/session_index_watch.rs`, `feeds/agent_supervisor.rs`; `tugdeck/src/lib/session-citation-store.ts`, `lib/session-atom.ts`, `lib/session-resume.ts`, `lib/annotator/session-resolution.ts`, `components/tugways/tug-session-identity.tsx`.
